Generic regularity for minimizing hypersurfaces in dimension 11
Otis Chodosh, Christos Mantoulidis, Felix Schulze, Zhihan Wang

Abstract
. We prove that area-minimizing hypersurfaces are generically smooth in ambient dimension 11 11 in the context of the Plateau problem and of area minimization in integral homology. For higher ambient dimensions, n + 1 ≥ 12 n+1\geq 12 , we prove in the same two contexts that area-minimizing hypersurfaces have at most an n − 10 − ϵ n n-10-\epsilon_{n} dimensional singular set after an arbitrarily C ∞ C^{\infty} -small perturbation of the Plateau boundary or the ambient Riemannian metric, respectively.



This article is dedicated to Leon Simon on the upcoming occasion of his 80th birthday for his groundbreaking contributions to the study of singularities in geometric analysis.

1.
Introduction

Consider a smooth, closed, oriented
(n-1)
-dimensional submanifold
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. We are interested here in Plateau’s problem. Among all smooth, compact hypersurfaces
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with
\partial M=\Gamma
, we want to find one of least area. It’s now well-known that such
M
always exists for
n+1\leq 7
, while when
n+1\geq 8
and for certain choices of
\Gamma
, no minimizer
M
can be found among smooth hypersurfaces. Using geometric measure theory, one can prove the existence of a minimizer among a wider class of objects which are smooth hypersurfaces except perhaps along an
(n-7)
-dimensional singular set. See
[
Fed69
,
MM84
,
Giu84
,
Mag12
,
Fle62
,
DG65
,
Alm66
,
Sim68
,
BDGG69
,
HS79
]
.

In
\mathbb{R}^{8}
, the first dimension that singularities can appear, a fundamental result of Hardt–Simon
[
HS85
]
shows that for a generic choice of Plateau boundary
\Gamma
, there does exist a smooth
M
minimizing area. An analogous result in
8
-dimensional manifolds was proven by Smale
[
Sma93
]
. These generic regularity results were recently extended to cover
\mathbb{R}^{9}
and
\mathbb{R}^{10}
in
[
CMS23a
]
using new ideas from the works of the first three authors with K. Choi on generic mean curvature flows, and specifically
[
CCMS24a
,
CCMS24b
]
.

In this paper we prove that solutions to Plateau’s problem in
\mathbb{R}^{11}
are generically smooth. We also prove that in any
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, an area-minimizing
M
will have a
\leq n-10-\epsilon_{n}
dimensional singular set after perhaps a
C^{\infty}
-perturbation of the Plateau boundary. See Theorem
1.1
. We had previously obtained the upper bound
\leq n-9-\epsilon_{n}^{\prime}
in
[
CMS24
]
.

We also prove the analogous results in the context of area-minimization in integral homology classes of a closed oriented manifold
(N^{n+1},g)
. See Theorem
1.2
. As is well-known, this extends Schoen–Yau’s stable minimal hypersurface obstruction to positive scalar curvature up to dimension
11
and also implies the positive mass theorem in these dimensions after a well-known reduction of Lohkamp. See also the work of Schoen–Yau and Lohkamp
[
SY22
,
Loh23
]
.

1.1.
Non-technical overview of new ideas

Before giving the precise statement of the results, we describe the new contributions in this paper as compared to
[
CMS23a
,
CMS24
]
. Recall that the idea there was to study and exploit the interaction of singular points in a “foliation” (possibly with gaps) of area-minimizers coming from variations of the Plateau boundary or Riemannian metric. There were two main ingredients. (i) A cone-splitting argument implied that the singular set of the entire foliation obeys the same
(n-7)
-dimensional Hausdorff estimate as a single leaf does. (ii) Analyzing positive Jacobi fields on minimizing hypercones (following L. Simon
[
Sim08
]
) implied that distinct leaves of the foliation obey a quantitative separation estimate at small scales. Using a Sard-type theorem and a stratification argument, the rate of quantitative separation in (ii) is sufficient to overcome the
(n-7)
-dimensional singular set in (i) when
n+1\in\{8,9,10\}
.

Our starting point here is the observation that for each possible spine dimension
k
of a tangent hypercone, i.e.,
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1-k}
, the singular points
least
amenable to being perturbed away by our previous strategy are product cylinders
\mathcal{C}
over quadratic hypercones (generalizations of Simons cones)
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1-k}
.

We proceed to closely analyze the behavior of the minimizers near such singularities.
1
1

1

In fact, we consider a broader class of cylindrical hypercones. See Remark
1.7
.
We go one blow-up level deeper and model the minimizer locally as a graph of a Jacobi field over such a cylindrical hypercone. This requires the use of non-concentration estimates pioneered by L. Simon in
[
Sim93
,
Sim94
]
.
2
2

2

Simon’s non-concentration estimates have been extremely influential in regularity theory for minimal hypersurfaces; we refer to the works
[
Wic14
,
CES22
,
MW24
,
Szé20
,
Szé21
,
ES24
]
.
Crucial in our analysis is the rate of decay toward the origin of these graph Jacobi fields. It is measured by a novel discrete Almgren frequency with a suitable monotonicity.
3
3

3

Almgren’s frequency monotonicity has also played a key role in regularity theory of higher codimension minimizing surfaces; we refer to the works
[
Alm00
,
DLS16
,
KW23a
,
KW23b
,
LS25
,
LS24
,
LMS24
]
.
(See Definition
4.1
for the definition.) The decay is super-linear (“fast”) or linear (“slow”). These two cases bootstrap our previous genericity strategy in different ways. The case of fast decay (which, a posteriori, implies tangent hypercone uniqueness) improves the separation estimate in (ii). The case of slow decay does not, but new cone-splitting arguments at the Jacobi field level (still exploiting the pairwise disjointness of two such graphical models in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
) provide a second-order refinement of (i) in the previous strategy.

As we explain in Section
1.4
, our previous strategy in
\mathbb{R}^{9}
,
\mathbb{R}^{10}
was borderline in
\mathbb{R}^{11}
for the “least non-generic” hypercones in
\mathbb{R}^{11}
of largest spine, i.e.,
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, with
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
any quadratic hypercone (e.g., a Simons cone). Our refined second order analysis near generalizations of this setting provides enough of an improvement to imply generic regularity in
\mathbb{R}^{11}
. The improved dimension of the singular set in all dimensions
\geq 12
follows similarly.

Let us end with a brief remark concerning this work in the context of the generic regularity theorem of Figalli–Ros-Oton–Serra for the obstacle problem
[
FROS20
]
. Already in
[
CMS23a
]
it was observed that the strategy of combining an estimate for the singular set of a “foliation” with a “separation” estimate was a posteriori similar to the strategy used in
[
FROS20
]
. The nature of the area minimization problem is such that
[
CMS23a
]
had to approach these estimates very differently than
[
FROS20
]
. In this paper, we obtain improved estimates over
[
CMS23a
]
by further analyzing higher-order “blowup” information (here, the Jacobi field). This remains in the spirit of
[
FROS20
]
(see also
[
FS19
]
) but, again, there are important differences in this problem. A core issue is that our Almgren-type frequency monotonicity quantity involves geometric data, but its monotonicity is at the linear (“blow up”) level. Going between the two requires wrestling with the possibility of tangent cone non-uniqueness, the lack of an equation for the singular set, and with non-concentration estimates. We must also treat curved background metrics, and curvature interferes with second-order analysis, particularly non-concentration estimates. These facts create considerable technical complications. On the other hand, we do not carry out nearly as refined of an analysis of second-order blowups; cf. Remark
1.8
.

1.2.
The main theorems

We now describe the results of this paper more precisely. We obtain here the following generalizations of the main result of
[
CMS23a
,
CMS24
]
. All submanifolds in this paper are embedded, and if
\Sigma
is such, we denote
\operatorname{sing}\Sigma=\bar{\Sigma}\setminus\Sigma
. All singular set dimensions are Hausdorff dimensions.

For the Plateau problem in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
we have:

Theorem 1.1
.

Consider a smooth, closed, oriented,
(n-1
)-dimensional submanifold
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. There exist
C^{\infty}
-small perturbations
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
(in the space of
C^{\infty}
submanifolds) such that every minimizing integral
n
-current with boundary
[\![\Gamma^{\prime}]\!]
is of the form
[\![\Sigma^{\prime}]\!]
for a smooth, precompact, oriented hypersurface
\Sigma^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with
\partial\Sigma^{\prime}=\Gamma^{\prime}
, and

\operatorname{sing}\Sigma^{\prime}=\emptyset\text{ if }n+1\leq 11,\text{ else }\dim\operatorname{sing}\Sigma^{\prime}\leq n-10-\epsilon_{n},

where
\epsilon_{n}>0
is a dimensional constant.

For the homological Plateau problem in a manifold we have:

Theorem 1.2
.

Consider a closed, oriented,
(n+1
)-dimensional Riemannian manifold
(N,g)
. Let
[\alpha]\in H_{n}(N,\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{[0]\}
. There exist
C^{\infty}
-small perturbations
g^{\prime}
of
g
such that every
g^{\prime}
-minimizing integral
n
-current in
[\alpha]
is of the form
\sum_{i=1}^{Q}k^{\prime}_{i}[\![\Sigma^{\prime}_{i}]\!]
for disjoint, smooth, precompact, oriented hypersurfaces
\Sigma^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\Sigma^{\prime}_{Q}\subset N
without boundary and

\operatorname{sing}\Sigma^{\prime}_{i}=\emptyset\text{ if }n+1\leq 11,\text{ else }\dim\operatorname{sing}\Sigma^{\prime}_{i}\leq n-10-\epsilon_{n},

and multiplicities
k^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,k^{\prime}_{Q}\in\mathbb{Z}
; again,
\epsilon_{n}>0
is a dimensional constant.

The respective proofs are given in Sections
8
and
9
.

Remark 1.3
.

It is a well-known consequence of Allard’s interior regularity theorem
[
All72
]
and Hardt–Simon’s boundary regularity theorem
[
HS79
]
that
\operatorname{sing}\Sigma^{\prime}=\emptyset
is an open condition in such a multiplicity-one setting. Therefore, when
n+1\leq 11
, the set of
\Gamma^{\prime}
,
g^{\prime}
for which the corresponding minimizers are smooth objects is simultaneously open (by this observation) and dense (by Theorems
1.1
,
1.2
), and thus Baire generic.

1.3.
The technical ingredient

Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
will be consequences (after some appropriate geometric considerations) of the following main result.
4
4

4

The geometric construction in the homological setting is improved here relative to
[
CMS23a
]
, so we can work here with a genuine “foliation” rather than “sufficiently dense set of leaves.” This relieves some complications in this part of
[
CMS23a
]
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Fix an
(n+1)
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary
(M,g)
,
n+1\geq 8
. Let
\mathscr{F}
be a set of minimizing boundaries in
M
. Denote:

\displaystyle\operatorname{spt}\mathscr{F}

\displaystyle:=\cup_{T\in\mathscr{F}}\operatorname{spt}T,

\displaystyle\operatorname{sing}\mathscr{F}

\displaystyle:=\cup_{T\in\mathscr{F}}\operatorname{sing}T.

Assume the properties:

(1)

\operatorname{sing}\mathscr{F}\Subset M
,

(2)

elements of
\mathscr{F}
have pairwise disjoint supports.

Moreover, let
\mathfrak{T}:\operatorname{spt}\mathscr{F}\to\mathbb{R}
be such that:

(3)

\mathfrak{T}
is constant on each
\operatorname{spt}T
,
T\in\mathscr{F}
, and

(4)

\mathfrak{T}
is Lipschitz continuous with the induced distance metric from
(M,g)
.

Then we have the following Hausdorff dimension bounds:

(i)

\dim\mathfrak{T}(\operatorname{sing}\mathscr{F})\leq\min\{\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{\textnormal{img}},1\}
, and

(ii)

For a.e.
t\in\mathbb{R}
, every
\Sigma\in\mathscr{F}
with
\mathfrak{T}=t
on
\operatorname{spt}\Sigma
has
\dim\operatorname{sing}\Sigma\leq\max\{\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{\textnormal{dom}},0\}
.

Above,
\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{\textnormal{img}}
,
\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{\textnormal{dom}}
are:

(1.1)

\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{\textnormal{img}}:=\max_{k=0,1,\ldots,n-7}\left\{\tfrac{k}{1+\alpha_{n-k}+\min\{\Delta^{\textnormal{non-qd}}_{n-k},\Delta^{\textnormal{qd}}_{n-k}\}},\tfrac{k-1}{1+\alpha_{n-k}}\right\},

(1.2)

\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{\textnormal{dom}}:=\max_{k=0,1,\ldots,n-7}\left\{k-(1+\alpha_{n-k}+\min\{\Delta^{\textnormal{non-qd}}_{n-k},\Delta^{\textnormal{qd}}_{n-k}\})\right\},

where

•

\alpha_{n-k}\in(1,2]
is as in Lemma
5.3
,

•

\Delta^{\textnormal{non-qd}}_{n-k}\in(0,\infty]
is as in Lemma
5.4
,

•

\Delta^{\textnormal{qd}}_{n-k}\in(0,1]
is as in (
7.17
).

Remark 1.5
.

It will appear at first sight that (
1.2
) takes a maximum over fewer terms than (
1.1
). Specifically, it seems as if we are neglecting to include a term of the form

k-1-(1+\alpha_{n-k})

in (
1.2
). However, accounting for such a term is unnecessary since the aforementioned fact that
\Delta^{\textnormal{qd}}_{n-k}\in(0,1]
guarantees

\displaystyle k-1-(1+\alpha_{n-k})

\displaystyle\leq k-(1+\alpha_{n-k}+\Delta^{\textnormal{qd}}_{n-k})

\displaystyle\leq k-(1+\alpha_{n-k}+\min\{\Delta^{\textnormal{non-qd}}_{n-k},\Delta^{\textnormal{qd}}_{n-k}\}).

It is not hard to see by tracing out the algebraic inequalities involved in (
1.1
), (
1.2
) that

\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{\textnormal{img}}<1\iff\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{\textnormal{dom}}<0\iff n+1\leq 11.

Tracing these inequalities carefully in fact yields:

Corollary 1.6
.

Assume the setting of Theorem
1.4
.

(i)

Assume
n+1\leq 11
. For a.e.
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\Sigma\in\mathscr{F},\;\mathfrak{T}=t\text{ on }\operatorname{spt}\Sigma\implies\operatorname{sing}\Sigma=\emptyset.

(ii)

Assume
n+1\geq 12
. There exists
\epsilon_{n}>0
such that for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\Sigma\in\mathscr{F},\;\mathfrak{T}=t\text{ on }\operatorname{spt}\Sigma\implies\dim\operatorname{sing}\Sigma\leq n-10-\epsilon_{n}.

1.4.
Strategy of proof

Let us briefly compare this result and its strategy to that of the previous results in
[
CMS23a
,
CMS24
,
CMS23b
]
.
5
5

5

The proof in
[
CMS23b
]
is most similar, as it required (the parabolic analog of)
\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}
-aware estimates for
\alpha(\mathcal{C})
, unlike those in
[
CMS23a
,
CMS24
]
.
The strategy in our previous works involved modifications of the following main theme:

(i)

Use cone-splitting arguments to show that, near points of
\operatorname{sing}\mathscr{F}
suitably modeled by a hypercone
\mathcal{C}
, we have

\operatorname{sing}\mathscr{F}\text{ is locally (coarsely)}\leq(\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C})\text{-dimensional}.

See also Proposition
7.1
(ii).

(ii)

Use positive Jacobi field decay estimates (originally due to Simon
[
Sim08
]
) to show, near points of
\mathcal{S}
modeled by a hypercone
\mathcal{C}
, that

\mathfrak{T}\text{ is locally (coarsely) }\mathfrak{h}\text{-H\"{o}lder}\text{ for all }\mathfrak{h}<1+\alpha(\mathcal{C}),

with
\alpha(\mathcal{C})
as in Definition
5.1
. See also Proposition
7.1
(iii) and Lemma
7.5
.

(iii)

Deduce using a covering argument such as
[
FROS20
, Corollary 7.8]
that

\dim\mathfrak{T}(\operatorname{sing}\mathscr{F})\leq\sup_{\mathcal{C}}\tfrac{\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}}{1+\alpha(\mathcal{C})},

and that, for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{R}
, every
\Sigma\in\mathscr{F}
with
\mathfrak{T}(\operatorname{spt}\Sigma)=t
also satisfies

\dim\operatorname{sing}\Sigma\leq\sup_{\mathcal{C}}(\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}-(1+\alpha(\mathcal{C}))).

The supremum is over all nonflat minimizing hypercones
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. See also Proposition
6.1
.

This strategy establishes full generic regularity for minimizers up to ambient dimension
n+1\leq 10
. That is because, in this case, one has
\inf_{\mathcal{C}}(1+\alpha(\mathcal{C}))>2
(see Lemma
5.3
) and
\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}\leq n-7\leq 2
, so

\sup_{\mathcal{C}}\tfrac{\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}}{1+\alpha(\mathcal{C})}<1.

This strategy will no longer suffice in ambient dimension
n+1=11
. Indeed, when

\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{Rot}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}),\;\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}\text{ any minimizing quadratic hypercone},

(e.g.,
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
could be a Simons cone) then Lemma
5.3
and Proposition
5.5
predict that

\tfrac{\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}}{1+\alpha(\mathcal{C})}=\tfrac{3}{3}=1,

which is insufficient for showing
\mathfrak{T}(\operatorname{sing}\mathscr{F})\subset\mathbb{R}
has measure zero. See Appendix
A
for some background on quadratic hypercones.

In this paper we delve into a refined analysis of this “borderline” case, i.e., the case where
\operatorname{sing}\mathscr{F}
is locally suitably modeled by a hypercone

\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1},\;\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{Rot}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}),

where

\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1-k}\text{ is any minimizing quadratic hypercone}.

We show a dichotomy in this situation. One can either improve the bound
\leq\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}
in (i) or prove Hölder-regularity beyond
1+\alpha(\mathcal{C})
in (ii).

More specifically, we make use of the fact that Jacobi fields on such
\mathcal{C}
which can be used to model nearby minimizing hypersurfaces obey an important
gap
in their degrees of homogeneity: either the degree equals
1
, or it is
\geq 1+\Delta^{>1}_{\mathcal{C}}
for some
\Delta^{>1}_{\mathcal{C}}>0
(see Remark
3.18
). These cases behave differently from each other in a qualitative manner:

(1)

Near points modeled by
\mathcal{C}
with degree
\geq 1+\Delta^{>1}_{\mathcal{C}}
, we can improve the (coarse) Hölder regularity in (ii) from
1+\alpha(\mathcal{C})
to
1+\alpha(\mathcal{C})+\Delta^{>1}_{\mathcal{C}}
. See Sections
4.4
,
7.6
.

(2)

Near points modeled by
\mathcal{C}
with degree equal to
1
, we can improve the (coarse) dimension bound in (i) from
\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}
to
\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}-1
. See Sections
4.5
,
7.7
.

Remark 1.7
.

Our technical ingredients in fact apply more generally to all

\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1},\;\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{Rot}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}),

where
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
is any hypercone which is:

•

regular (i.e.,
\operatorname{sing}\mathcal{C}_{\circ}=\{0\}
),

•

strictly stable (Definition
3.9
),

•

strictly minimizing (Definition
H.2
), and

•

strongly integrable (Definition
3.12
).

All minimizing quadratic hypercones
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
satisfy these properties. See Appendix
A
.

Remark 1.8
.

The classification of area-minimizing hypercones in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
is a well-known open problem. It is not known if there exist non-strictly stable, non-strictly minimizing, or non strongly integrable hypercones in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
(or higher dimensions). Such a question is likely to present a major issue in any study of generic regularity in
\mathbb{R}^{12}
and beyond.

1.5.
An explanation in the slow-decay case: computing the homogeneous degree-one Jacobi fields on cylinders over Simons cones

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}=C(\mathbb{S}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{3})\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
be the Simons cone. We briefly describe the homogeneous degree-one Jacobi fields on
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
and indicate how the improved “effective spine” estimate (see Section
1.4
) can be seen at the linear level.

The homogeneous Jacobi fields
w
on
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
(cf. Definition
3.5
) satisfy
w(r\omega)=r^{\gamma}\psi(\omega)
for
\psi
an eigenfunction of
-(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}+|A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}|^{2})
with corresponding eigenvalue
\gamma^{2}+5\gamma
where
\mathcal{L}_{\circ}=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\cap\mathbb{S}^{7}
. Using spectral theory on
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, it’s easy to compute (cf.
[
SS86
]
) the small eigenvalues of
-(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}+|A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}|^{2})
to be
-6
(with constant eigenfunction),
0
(corresponding to translations of the cone) and
6
(corresponding to rotations). The associated degrees of homogeneous eigenfunctions on
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
are (respectively)
\gamma=-2,0
and
1
.

Suppose that
u
is an appropriate (cf. Definition
3.13
) Jacobi field on
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
that’s homogeneous of degree one. (Due to Simon’s non-concentration result, this is exactly the model for minimal surfaces with a
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
tangent cone that are exhibiting “slow decay.”) Using coordinates
(r,\omega,y)
on
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
where
r\omega\in\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
is a “polar coordinate” and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
is the direction along the spine
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
, work of Simon shows that
u
can be decomposed as
u=u_{-2}+u_{0}+u_{1}
where

u_{\gamma}(r,\omega,y)=r^{\gamma}\psi(\omega)p_{1-\gamma}(r,y)

for
\psi
an eigenfunction of
-(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}+|A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}|^{2})
with eigenvalue corresponding to
\gamma
as above and
p_{1-\gamma}(r,y)
a polynomial in
(r,y)
with only even powers of
r
that’s homogeneous of degree
1-\gamma
satisfying the “
\beta
-harmonic equation”

\left(\partial^{2}_{r}+\frac{1+\beta}{r}\partial_{r}+\Delta_{y}\right)p_{1-\gamma}=0

for
\beta=2\gamma+5
(cf. Lemma
3.20
). By adjusting by an appropriate rotation, it’s possible (when
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
is strongly integrable, as is the case for the Simons cone; cf. Definition
3.12
) to ensure that
6
6

6

To clarify this, in the strongly integrable case, for
\gamma=1
, we see that
p_{0}
is constant so
u_{1}
corresponds to a rotation fixing the spine
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Similarly, for
\gamma=0
,
p_{1}
is a linear function in
y
(it cannot contain
r
since it only contains powers of
r^{2}
), so this corresponds to a rotation fixing
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
but rotating the spine.

u_{0}=u_{1}=0
. It thus remains to consider
\gamma=-2
. In this case,
\psi(\omega)=1
(up to a constant) and
\beta=1
. We need to determine all
1
-harmonic polynomials
p_{3}(r,y)
in
(r,y)
that are homogeneous of degree
3
and contain only powers of
r^{2}
.

When
k=1
, i.e., when
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}
, there’s a one-dimensional space of
p_{3}
, spanned by
y^{3}-r^{2}y
by
[
Sim21
, Section 3]
. As such, if a minimal hypersurface
\Sigma
in
\mathbb{R}^{9}
has a tangent cone
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}
, and exhibits slow decay at some scale, then we’ll see that (after a slight rotation), the hypersurface is more closely modeled on a Jacobi field of the form

u(r,\omega,y)=c(r^{-2}y^{3}-y).

for some
c\neq 0
. In particular, at any non-zero point
(0,0)\neq(0,y)\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}
in the spine of
\mathcal{C}
we see
r\mapsto u(r,\omega,y)
is growing at rate
r^{-2}
as
r\to 0
. On the other hand, if
\Sigma
is singular (and modeled on
\mathcal{C}
with slow decay) near
(0,y)
, this decay rate must be linear (or faster). In sum, the traditional analysis of the singular set of
\Sigma
near the origin based on the singular set of the cone
\mathcal{C}
would suggest that there could be a one-dimensional singular set near the origin. However, in this slow-decay case this estimate can be improved to show that there are no nearby singularities modeled on
\mathcal{C}
(i.e. this part of the singular set is
0
-dimensional).

We have ignored several crucial issues here. First of all, in the actual analysis, we need to consider
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
for
k\geq 3
. In this case, there are multiple possibilities for
p_{3}
. For example, when
k=3
one possibility is the
1
-dimensional solution extended to trivially to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
:

p_{3}(r,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})=y_{1}^{3}-r^{2}y_{1}

(see
[
Sim21
, Section 3]
for the general solution) in which the Jacobi field would be

u(r,\omega,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})=c(r^{-2}(y_{1})^{3}-y_{1})

which has linear decay rate as
r\to 0
precisely on the
2
dimensional subspace
\{y_{1}=0\}\subset\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Thus, if
\Sigma
has a tangent cone
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
and is modeled to higher order by this Jacobi field, we expect that the singularities of
\Sigma
that are also modeled on
\mathcal{C}
with slow decay are concentrated near
\{y_{1}=0\}
. The reader may check that less trivial possibilties for
p_{3}
such as
p_{3}(r,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})=3(y_{1})^{2}y_{2}-r^{2}y_{2}
have similar behavior (but the resulting “effective spine” has even smaller dimension).

Secondly, we have omitted several essential additional geometric considerations, the most important of which are as follows. We need to allow for the case that
u
also contains a translation term, corresponding to a singularity near—but not exactly on—the spine of the cone. Moreover, we need to extend the above analysis to the case of two disjoint minimizers
\Sigma,\Sigma^{+}
. We describe here how to handle the second issue. In particular, we prove that if
\Sigma
has slow-decay, and thus is modeled on
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
and homogeneous degree-one Jacobi field
u
as above, then under appropriate conditions, a disjoint minimizer
\Sigma^{+}
is modeled on
\mathcal{C}
and a Jacobi field in the span of
u
, a translation, and a positive (corresponding to
\Sigma,\Sigma^{+}
being disjoint) Jacobi field. Using Edelen–Székelyhidi’s
[
ES24
]
Liouville theorem for positive Jacobi fields (with appropriate decay) on
\mathcal{C}
we find that this positive Jacobi field is equal to a multiple of
r^{-2}
(cf. Lemma
3.21
). The key observation in this case is that adding
r^{-2}
(or translations) cannot sufficiently cancel the decay of
u
to create linear decay at some new points on the spine of
\mathcal{C}
. For example, when
k=1
we have (in the case where there’s only a positive Jacobi field and no translation)

u(r,\omega,y)+dr^{-2}=(cy^{3}+d)r^{-2}-cy,

so even if
cy^{3}+d=0
at some
y\in\mathbb{R}
, the decay of the remaining term
-cy
will be
O(1)
rather than
O(r)
as
r\to 0
at such a point. Translation terms can be handled in an analogous manner.

1.6.
Acknowledgements

We are grateful to Nick Edelen, Xavier Ros-Oton, Rick Schoen, Joaquim Serra, Leon Simon, Ao Sun, Gábor Székelyhidi and Jinxin Xue for their encouragement and interest. O.C. was supported by a Terman Fellowship and an NSF grant
(DMS-2304432). C.M. was supported by an NSF grant (DMS-2403728).

2.
Preliminaries and notation

In addition to standard notation in geometric measure theory (cf.
[
Sim83
]
) we adopt the following notation:

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{n}

\displaystyle:=\{\text{nonflat mininimizing hypercones }\mathcal{C}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\},

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd}}

\displaystyle:=\{\text{quadratic }\mathcal{C}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\},

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd-cyl}}

\displaystyle:=\cup_{k=0,1,\ldots,n-7}\{\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}:\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{Rot}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}),\;\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\in\mathscr{C}_{n-k}^{\textnormal{qd}}\}.

For a subset
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
we write
\operatorname{Rot}(S):=\{O(S):O\in SO(n)\}
.

When it’s unlikely to cause confusion we will conflate peremeter-minimizing Caccioppoli sets
E\subset(M,g)
with their reduced boundary
\Sigma=\partial^{*}E
. We then set
\operatorname{sing}\Sigma=\bar{\Sigma}\setminus\Sigma
. For
p\in\bar{\Sigma}
we define the
regularity scale

r_{\Sigma}(p)
to be the supremum of
r\in(0,\textrm{inj}_{(M,g)}(x))
so that
\bar{\Sigma}\cap B_{r}(p)
is a smooth hypersurface with second fundamental form
|A|\leq r^{-1}
. As in
[
CMS23a
, Lemma 2.4]
the regularity scale is continuous with respect to weak convergence
\Sigma
and convergence of
x
. We also define the relatively open sets

\operatorname{reg}_{>\rho}\Sigma:=\{p\in\Sigma:r_{\Sigma}(p)>\rho\}\subset\Sigma.

3.
Jacobi fields on cylindrical hypercones

3.1.
General notions

Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
a minimal hypercone, not necessarily regular.
7
7

7

In our subsequent geometric applications, we will take
\mathcal{C}
to be
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
, with
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
some minimizing quadratic hypercone (see Appendix
A
).

Recall that we adopt the convention that
\mathcal{C}
coincides with its regular part, and can be viewed as an
n
-dimensional submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. In particular, the covariant derivative
\nabla_{\mathcal{C}}
and Laplace–Beltrami operator
\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}
and the second fundamental form
A_{\mathcal{C}}
are well-defined on
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 3.1
.

We denote the space of Jacobi fields on
\mathcal{C}
by

\operatorname{Jac}(\mathcal{C}):=\{w\in C^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{C}):\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}u+|A_{\mathcal{C}}|^{2}u=0\}.

We will also be interested in locally defined Jacobi fields within some open
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
:

\operatorname{Jac}(\mathcal{C}\cap U):=\{w\in C^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{C}\cap U):\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}u+|A_{\mathcal{C}}|^{2}u=0\}.

Certain canonical Jacobi fields, which are induced by ambient isometries, will play an important role in our analysis. Fix a global unit normal
\nu_{\mathcal{C}}
defined on
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 3.2
.

Any fixed
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
induces a
translation Jacobi field
,

W_{\mathbf{v}}(x):=\langle\mathbf{v},\nu_{\mathcal{C}}(x)\rangle,\;x\in\mathcal{C}.

The space of such Jacobi fields will be denoted

\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{trl}}(\mathcal{C}):=\{W_{\mathbf{v}}:\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\}.

Definition 3.3
.

Any fixed
\mathbf{A}\in\mathfrak{so}(n+1)
induces a
rotation Jacobi field

W_{\mathbf{A}}(x):=\langle\mathbf{A}x,\nu_{\mathcal{C}}(x)\rangle,\;x\in\mathcal{C}.

The space of such Jacobi fields will be denoted

\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{rot}}(\mathcal{C}):=\{W_{\mathbf{A}}:\mathbf{A}\in\mathfrak{so}(n+1)\}.

In performing our spectral analysis of Jacobi fields on suitable minimal hypercones, we will be especially interested in homogeneous Jacobi fields:

Definition 3.4
.

A Jacobi field
w\in\operatorname{Jac}(\mathcal{C})
is
homogeneous of degree
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
if

w(tx)=t^{\gamma}w(x)

for every
t>0
and
x\in\mathcal{C}
. The space of such Jacobi fields will be denoted

\operatorname{Jac}_{\gamma}(\mathcal{C})=\{w\in\operatorname{Jac}(\mathcal{C})\text{ is homogeneous of degree $\gamma$}\}.

Clearly:

(3.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{trl}}(\mathcal{C})\subset\operatorname{Jac}_{0}(\mathcal{C}),

(3.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{rot}}(\mathcal{C})\subset\operatorname{Jac}_{1}(\mathcal{C}).

3.2.
Jacobi fields on regular minimal hypercones

Fix a nonflat minimal hypercone

\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1},

that is regular, i.e., one with
\operatorname{sing}\mathcal{C}_{\circ}=\{0\}
, and denote its link by

\mathcal{L}_{\circ}:=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\cap\mathbb{S}^{n_{\circ}}.

We may parameterize
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
by

(0,+\infty)\times\mathcal{L}_{\circ}\to\mathcal{C}_{\circ},\ \ \ (r,\omega)\mapsto x=r\omega\,.

Definition 3.5
.

Denote

\displaystyle\Gamma(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})

\displaystyle:=\{\deg(v):v\text{ is a nontrivial homogeneous Jacobi field on }\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\}

\displaystyle=\{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}:\operatorname{Jac}_{\gamma}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})\neq 0\,\}\subset\mathbb{R}.

Note that a homogeneous Jacobi field
w\in\operatorname{Jac}_{\gamma}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
can be written as

(3.3)

w(r\omega)=r^{\gamma}\psi(\omega).

Following the notation of Definition
5.1
, denote the Laplace–Beltrami operator on
\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
by
\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}
, and the second fundamental form of
\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
in
\mathbb{S}^{n_{\circ}}
by
A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}
. Then, by the simple computation

\Delta_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}w+|A_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}|^{2}w=r^{-2}(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}\psi+|A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}|^{2}\psi)+r^{-2}(\gamma^{2}+(n_{\circ}-2)\gamma)\psi.

It easily follows that:

Lemma 3.6
.

The subset
\Gamma(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})\subset\mathbb{R}
of Definition
3.5
is discrete. Elements
\gamma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
and
w\in\operatorname{Jac}_{\gamma}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})\setminus\{0\}
as in (
3.3
) are characterized by the fact that

(3.4)

\mu(\gamma):=\gamma^{2}+(n_{\circ}-2)\gamma

is an eigenvalue of
-(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}+|A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}|^{2})
on
\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
and
\psi
is a corresponding eigenfunction.

An easy computation shows:

Lemma 3.7
.

Homogeneous Jacobi fields on
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
with finite Dirichlet energy on
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\cap B_{1}
are those whose degrees lie in

\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ}):=\Gamma(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})\cap\mathbb{R}_{>-(n_{\circ}-2)/2}\,.

Remark 3.8
.

It is an easy consequence of Definition
3.5
, Lemma
3.7
(
3.1
), (
3.2
) that
0,1\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
.

When solving for homogeneity degrees
\gamma
in terms of eigenvalues
\mu
of
-(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}+|A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}|^{2})
via (
3.4
), it will be important for us that each
\mu
gives rise to a
\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
. Equivalently, we want
-\tfrac{n_{\circ}-2}{2}\not\in\Gamma(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
. It was shown in
[
HS85
, Section 1.6]
that the following notion of the “strict stability” of
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
guarantees this. Henceforth
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
will be thus assumed to be strictly stable.

Definition 3.9
.

A regular minimal hypercone
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
is said to be
strictly stable
if there exists a
\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}>0
such that for every
\varphi\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
, the second variation

Q_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}(\varphi,\varphi):=\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}|\nabla_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}\varphi|^{2}-|A_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}|^{2}\varphi^{2}

satisfies

Q_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}(\varphi,\varphi)\geq\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}r^{-2}\varphi^{2}.

We proceed and fix an
L^{2}(\mathcal{L}_{\circ})
-orthonormal basis consisting of eigenfunctions of
-(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}+|A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}|^{2})
,

\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\psi_{3},\ldots

where
\psi_{1}>0
, with corresponding eigenvalues

\mu(\gamma_{1})<\mu(\gamma_{2})\leq\mu(\gamma_{3})\leq\ldots\nearrow+\infty,

and where the homogeneity degrees

-\tfrac{n_{\circ}-2}{2}<\gamma_{1}<\gamma_{2}\leq\gamma_{3}\leq\dots\nearrow+\infty,

are related to the eigenvalues via this relationship stemming from (
3.4
):

\gamma_{j}=-\tfrac{n_{\circ}-2}{2}+\sqrt{\tfrac{(n_{\circ}-2)^{2}}{4}+\mu(\gamma_{j})}.

Finally, choose corresponding homogeneous Jacobi fields on
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
with finite Dirichlet energy on
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\cap B_{1}

w_{j}(x):=r^{\gamma_{j}}\psi_{j}(\omega).

After first choosing
\psi_{j}
appropriately, we can assume that for each
j\geq 1
:

•

w_{j}\in\operatorname{Jac}_{\textrm{trl}}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
, or

•

w_{j}\in\operatorname{Jac}_{\textrm{rot}}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
, or

•

w_{j}\perp\operatorname{Jac}_{\textrm{trl}}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})\oplus\operatorname{Jac}_{\textrm{rot}}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\cap B_{1})
.

Remark 3.10
.

Since
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
is nonflat, taking
\varphi=1
in the variational characterization of eigenvalues implies that
\mu(\gamma_{1})<0
and thus, in particular, that
\gamma_{1}<0
.

Remark 3.11
.

We chose not to use
\circ
’s in our notation for the spectral data
\psi_{j},\gamma_{j},w_{j}
so as to minimize notational clutter. For cylindrical hypercones
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we plan to express and understand all spectral quantities in terms of these ones for
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
.

We will eventually have to place another assumption on
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
, i.e., that it be strongly integrable in the sense of
[
Sim94
]
. We recall the definition below for this later use.

Definition 3.12
.

We say that
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
is
strongly integrable
, if

\operatorname{Jac}_{0}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})=\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{trl}}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})\text{ and }\operatorname{Jac}_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})=\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{rot}}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ}),

i.e., all homogeneous degree-
0
, resp.
1
, Jacobi fields are translations, resp. rotations.

3.3.
Jacobi fields on cylindrical minimal hypercones

In this subsection we discuss the case of minimal hypercones of interest. We take

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k},

where
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1}
,
n_{\circ}=n-k
, is a regular and strictly stable minimal hypercone. All our results also clearly apply to all rotations of such
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

With
\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
still denoting the link of
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
, we can parametrize
\mathcal{C}
by

(3.5)

\displaystyle(0,+\infty)\times\mathcal{L}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathcal{C},\ \ \ (r,\omega,y)\mapsto(x=r\omega,y)\,.

Following Section
3.2
above, we continue to denote:

•

\gamma_{j}\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
, the homogeneity degrees of homogeneous Jacobi fields on
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
with locally finite Dirichlet energy, and

•

w_{j}=r^{\gamma_{j}}\psi_{j}\in\operatorname{Jac}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
, the corresponding homogeneous Jacobi fields on
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
, normalized as discussed immediately prior to Remark
3.10
.

Note that, in this cylindrical setting,
r
coincides with the distance to
\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
. We will be concerned with Jacobi fields on
\mathcal{C}
for which the following
r
-weighted Dirichlet energy is locally finite:

Definition 3.13
.

For
u\in\operatorname{Jac}(\mathcal{C})
and open
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, define

\displaystyle\|u\|_{H^{1}_{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap U)}^{2}:=\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap U}|\nabla_{\mathcal{C}}u|^{2}+r^{-2}u^{2}.

We also define the following spaces of Jacobi fields for open
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap U)

\displaystyle:=\{u\in\operatorname{Jac}(\mathcal{C}\cap U):\|u\|_{H^{1}_{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap U^{\prime})}<\infty\text{ for all }U^{\prime}\Subset U\}\,,

where of course
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
. We also define

\displaystyle\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textnormal{pos}}(\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle:=\{tu:t\in\mathbb{R},u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C}),u>0\}\,,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{d}(\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C})\cap\operatorname{Jac}_{d}(\mathcal{C}),\;d\in\mathbb{R}.

For later use, we record here a Caccioppoli inequality that plays an important role in our subsequent construction of Jacobi fields. It only relies on our standing assumptions, i.e., on
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}
being regular and strictly stable.

Lemma 3.14
.

For every
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{\varrho})
and
0<{\tau_{1}}<{\tau_{2}}<\varrho
, we have

\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{\tau_{1}}}|\nabla_{\mathcal{C}}u|^{2}+r^{-2}u^{2}\leq\frac{C(\mathcal{C})}{({\tau_{2}}-{\tau_{1}})^{2}}\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{\tau_{2}}}u^{2}.

Proof.

The strict stability of
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
gives
\mu=\mu(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
with

\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}|\nabla_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}\varphi|^{2}-|A_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}|^{2}\varphi^{2}\geq\mu\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}r^{-2}\varphi^{2},\text{ for all }\varphi\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ}).

Fubini’s theorem then implies that

\int_{\mathcal{C}}|\nabla_{\mathcal{C}}\varphi|^{2}-|A_{\mathcal{C}}|^{2}\varphi^{2}\geq\mu\int_{\mathcal{C}}r^{-2}\varphi^{2},\text{ for all }\varphi\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ}).

Using that
|A_{\mathcal{C}}|^{2}\leq C(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})r^{-2}
we can take
\mu
smaller so that

\int_{\mathcal{C}}|\nabla_{\mathcal{C}}\varphi|^{2}-|A_{\mathcal{C}}|^{2}\varphi^{2}\geq\mu\int_{\mathcal{C}}|\nabla_{\mathcal{C}}\varphi|^{2}+r^{-2}\varphi^{2},\text{ for all }\varphi\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ}).

On the other hand, for
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C};B_{\varrho})
and
\eta\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{\tau_{2}})
we can integrate by parts to find that

\int_{\mathcal{C}}|\nabla_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta u)|^{2}-|A_{\mathcal{C}}|^{2}\eta^{2}u^{2}=\int_{\mathcal{C}}|\nabla\eta|^{2}u^{2}.

Combining these expressions and choosing
\eta
equal to
1
on
B_{\tau_{1}}
, cutting off smoothly inside of
B_{\tau_{2}}
completes the proof.
∎

Jacobi fields in
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
have been studied extensively by Simon
[
Sim94
,
Sim21
]
and Edelen–Székelyhidi
[
ES24
, Section 2.5]
. It will be important for our geometric applications involving “one-sided” perturbations (which yield positive Jacobi fields) that
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textnormal{pos}}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma_{1}}(\mathcal{C})
and this space is generated by the corresponding underlying positive eigenfunctions from
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
. This will be shown in Lemma
3.21
, but first we need to introduce more notation.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
\varrho>0
and

B^{+}_{\varrho}:=\{(r,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}:r^{2}+|y|^{2}<\varrho^{2}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k+1}.

We say that
h\in C^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(B^{+}_{\varrho})
is
\beta
-harmonic on
B^{+}_{\varrho}
if

(3.6)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\;\;r^{-1-\beta}\operatorname{div}_{\mathbb{R}^{k+1}}(r^{1+\beta}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{k+1}}h)=\displaystyle\left(\partial_{r}^{2}+\tfrac{1+\beta}{r}\partial_{r}+\Delta_{y}\right)h=0\,,&\\
\;\;\displaystyle\int_{B^{+}_{\tau}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{k+1}}h|^{2}\,r^{1+\beta}\,dr\,dy<+\infty\text{ for all }0<\tau<\varrho\,.\end{cases}

The following result regarding
\beta
-harmonic functions was proven by Simon
[
Sim21
, Section 3 & Appendix A.19]
.

Proposition 3.16
.

Suppose that
h
is
\beta
-harmonic on
B^{+}_{1}
. Then:

(1)

h
can be smoothly extended to
\{r=0\}
, and

(2)

the extension is real analytic in
r^{2}
and
y
, i.e., there exists
\tau=\tau(k,\beta)\in(0,1/2)
and degree-
i
homogeneous polynomials
p_{ij}(y)
so that,

h(r,y)=\sum_{i,j\geq 0}r^{2j}p_{ij}(y)

with

\sup_{(r,y)\in B_{\tau}^{+}}|D_{r}^{j}D_{y}^{i}h(r,y)|\leq C(\beta,k)^{i+j}i!j!\left(\int_{B_{3/4}^{+}}h^{2}\,r^{1+\beta}\,dr\,dy\right)

for all
i,j\geq 0
.

Moreover, if
d\in\mathbb{R}
and
h
is a non-vanishing homogeneous function of degree
d
, then
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
h
is a polynomial in
r^{2}
and
y
and is uniquely determined by the non-vanishing degree-
d
polynomial
h(0,y)
in
y
.

Below, we will always implicitly extend
\beta
-harmonic functions to
\{r=0\}
.

Definition 3.17
.

For
\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
and an integer
q\geq 0
we let

\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma,q}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma+q}(\mathcal{C})\cap\operatorname{span}\{r^{\gamma}\psi(\omega)p_{q}(r,y)\}

where the span is taken over all:

•

\psi(\omega)
that are eigenfunctions of
-\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}-|A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}|^{2}
on
\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
with eigenvalue
\mu(\gamma)
as in (
3.4
), and

•

p_{q}(r,y)
that are homogeneous degree-
q
polynomials in
(r,y)
that are
(2\gamma+n-2)
-harmonic (see Definition
3.15
); by Proposition
3.16
,
p_{q}(r,y)
is a posteriori a polynomial in
r^{2}
and
y
, i.e., it contains no odd powers of
r
.

Clearly,

\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma,q}(\mathcal{C})\subset\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma+q}(\mathcal{C}).

We denote by

(3.7)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C}):=\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})+\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}=\{\gamma+q:\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ}),q\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}\,.

the set of homogeneities obtained in this way.

Remark 3.18
.

It follows from Lemma
3.6
and Remark
3.8
that
\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})\subset\mathbb{R}
is discrete and contains
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Thus, the following quantities are well-defined,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{<1}

\displaystyle:=1-\max(\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})\cap\mathbb{R}_{<1}),

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{>1}

\displaystyle:=\min(\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})\cap\mathbb{R}_{>1})-1,

and take values in
(0,1]
. Moreover,

(1-\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{<1},1)\cap\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})=(1,1+\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{>1})\cap\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})=\emptyset.

The following crucial result was essentially proven by Simon
[
Sim21
, Section 3]
.

Proposition 3.19
.

Fix
d\in\mathbb{R}
.

(i)

If
d\not\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})
then
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{d}(\mathcal{C})=\{0\}
.

(ii)

If
d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})
then we have an
L^{2}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{1})
-orthogonal decomposition

\operatorname{Jac}_{d}^{*}(\mathcal{C})=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})\\
q\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
\gamma+q=d\end{subarray}}\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma,q}(\mathcal{C})\,.

(iii)

For
d^{\prime}\neq d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})
, we have with respect to
L^{2}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{1})
that

\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{d}(\mathcal{C})\perp\operatorname{Jac}_{d^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathcal{C}).

(iv)

Let
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{R})
. We may assume that
R>1
by rescaling the domain. Then,
u
can be uniquely decomposed in
L^{2}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{1})
as

(3.8)

u(x,y)=\sum_{d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})}u_{d}(x,y),

where
u_{d}\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{d}(\mathcal{C})
for all
d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})
. Moreover, for all
\varrho\in(0,R)
,

\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{\varrho})}^{2}=\sum_{d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})}c_{d}^{2}\varrho^{n+2d},\text{ where }c_{d}^{2}:=\frac{1}{n+2d}\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap\mathbb{S}^{n}}u_{d}^{2}\,.

We also have:

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{R})
. Then, for
r^{2}+|y|^{2}<R^{2}
we define

h_{j}(r,y):=r^{-\gamma_{j}}\int_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}u(r\omega,y)\psi_{j}(\omega)\,d\omega,\;j\geq 1.

Then
h_{j}
is
\beta
-harmonic on
B^{+}_{R}
with
\beta=2\gamma_{j}+n_{\circ}-2>0
.

Proof.

This is a straightforward computation. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{r}^{2}+\tfrac{1+\beta}{r}\partial_{r}+\Delta_{y}\right)h

\displaystyle=\gamma_{j}(\beta-\gamma_{j})r^{-2}h+r^{-\gamma_{j}}\int_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}\left(\partial^{2}_{r}u+\tfrac{n_{\circ}-1}{r}\partial_{r}u+\Delta_{y}u\right)\psi_{j}

\displaystyle=\mu(\gamma_{j})r^{-2}h-r^{-\gamma_{j}-2}\int_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}\left(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}u+|A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}|^{2}u\right)\psi_{j}

\displaystyle=\mu(\gamma_{j})r^{-2}h-r^{-\gamma_{j}-2}\int_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}\left(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}\psi_{j}+|A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}|^{2}\psi_{j}\right)u

\displaystyle=0.

The weighted Dirichlet energy bound follows from the definition of the
H^{1}_{*}
norm.
∎

The following lemma relates the translation, rotation, and positive Jacobi fields to the spectral decomposition obtained in Proposition
3.19
.

Lemma 3.21
.

The translation and rotation Jacobi fields of
\mathcal{C}
(see Definitions
3.2
,
3.3
) satisfy

\displaystyle\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{trl}}(\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle\subset\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,0}(\mathcal{C})\,,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{rot}}(\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle\subset\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{1,0}(\mathcal{C})\oplus\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,1}(\mathcal{C})\,;

cf. (
3.1
), (
3.2
). Moreover

\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{pos}}^{*}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma_{1}}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma_{1},0}(\mathcal{C})

is
1
-dimensional and spanned by
\ r^{\gamma_{1}}\psi_{1}(\omega)
.

Proof.

The first two statements are clear.

The claim about positive Jacobi fields actually follows from the work of Edelen–Székelyhidi
[
ES24
, Lemma 2.7]
. Since their hypotheses are stated slightly differently we recall their proof here.

First, note that by Proposition
3.19
(ii) we have
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma_{1}}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma_{1},0}(\mathcal{C})
. Since

\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma_{1},0}(\mathcal{C})\subset\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textnormal{pos}}(\mathcal{C})

is clear from the fact that
\psi_{1}>0
, it suffices to prove the reverse inclusion. To that end, let
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textrm{pos}}(\mathcal{C})
be arbitrary. For
j\geq 1
, recall that

h_{j}(r,y):=r^{-\gamma_{j}}\int_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}u(r\omega,y)\psi_{j}(\omega)\ d\omega\,.

is
\beta_{j}
-harmonic with
\beta_{j}=2\gamma_{j}+n-2>0
by Lemma
3.20
. Moreover,
h_{j}
extends to
\{r=0\}
and the extension is real analytic in
r^{2}
and
y
by Proposition
3.16
.

As in
[
ES24
, (19)]
, the Liouville property for (nonnegative)
\beta
-harmonic functions implies that
h_{1}(r,y)
is constant. Thus, we get

\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{\varrho}}r^{\gamma_{1}}u\psi_{1}=h_{1}(0,0)\int_{0}^{\varrho}r^{n-1+2\gamma_{1}}\,dr=\frac{h_{1}(0,0)}{n+2\gamma_{1}}\varrho^{n+2\gamma_{1}}.

Using elliptic estimates and positivity of
u
, there is
C=C(\mathcal{C})>0
with

u(\varrho\omega,y)\leq C\varrho^{-n}\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{\varrho/2}(\varrho\omega,y)}u\leq C\varrho^{-n-\gamma_{1}}\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2\varrho}}r^{\gamma_{1}}u\psi_{1}\leq C\varrho^{\gamma_{1}},

so

\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{\varrho}}u^{2}\leq C\varrho^{n+2\gamma_{1}}.

On the other hand, if
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textrm{pos}}(\mathcal{C})\setminus\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma_{1}}(\mathcal{C})
then (iv) in Proposition
3.19
implies that

\varrho^{-n-2\gamma_{1}}\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{\varrho}}u^{2}\to\infty

as
\varrho\to\infty
, a contradiction. Thus,
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma_{1}}(\mathcal{C})
. However,
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma_{1}}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma_{1},0}(\mathcal{C})
by (ii) in Proposition
3.19
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.22
.

It follows from Lemma
3.21
that
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
is strongly integrable (Definition
3.12
) if and only if

\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{trl}}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,0}(\mathcal{C})\text{ and }\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{rot}}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{1,0}(\mathcal{C})\oplus\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,1}(\mathcal{C}).

3.4.
The decay order

We introduce one final definition, whose study we defer to later in the section. We retain all conventions and assumptions on

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}

from the previous section, namely, that
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1}
is a regular, strictly stable minimal hypercone with link
\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
and associated spectral quantities
\gamma_{j}
,
w_{j}
,
\psi_{j}
.

Definition 3.23
.

Consider a nonzero Jacobi field
u\in\operatorname{Jac}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2\varrho})\cap L^{2}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2\varrho})
. For
\kappa\geq 0
, we define the
\kappa
-adjusted
decay order
of
u
at scale
\varrho>0
to be

\displaystyle G^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\varrho)

\displaystyle:=1+\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}\left((2\varrho)^{-n-2}\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2\varrho}}u^{2}+\kappa\cdot(2\varrho)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}\left(\varrho^{-n-2}\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{\varrho}}u^{2}+\kappa\cdot\varrho^{2}\right).

We’ll write
G_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\varrho)
for
G^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\varrho)
.

Remark 3.24
.

The constant
\kappa\geq 0
is an auxiliary damping factor that will be necessary in the subsequent Riemannian setting. We encourage the first-time reader to read this section keeping in mind that
\kappa=0
suffices for all applications to
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

It follows from the definition above that
G_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\varrho)=\gamma
if
u\in\operatorname{Jac}_{\gamma}(\mathcal{C})\cap L^{2}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2\varrho})
. See also Corollary
3.26
below.

For non-homogeneous
L^{2}
Jacobi fields
u
, we will prove a crucial monotonicity property of the decay order. First note that:

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{R})\setminus\{0\}
,
\kappa\geq 0
. The auxiliary function

H^{\kappa}(t):=\log\left(\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{e^{t}}}u^{2}+\kappa\cdot e^{(n+4)t}\right)

is well-defined and smooth and satisfies:

(i)

H^{\kappa}
is a
C^{\infty}
convex function in
(-\infty,\log R)
,

(ii)

(H^{\kappa})^{\prime}
equals the constant
2n+d\in\mathbb{R}
on some sub-interval of
(-\infty,\log R)
if and only if
u
is the restriction to
B_{R}
of a
\operatorname{Jac}_{d}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
element, with
d=2
if
\kappa>0
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.19
(iv) we may write

\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{\varrho}}u^{2}=\sum_{d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})}c_{d}^{2}\varrho^{n+2d}\,

where this series converges for all
\varrho\in(0,R)
. Let
8
8

8

Recall that
2\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})
by Remark
3.18
.

\tilde{c}_{d}=\begin{cases}c_{d}&\text{ if }d\neq 2,\\
\sqrt{c_{2}^{2}+\kappa}&\text{ if }d=2.\end{cases}

We find that

H^{\kappa}(t)=\log\left(\sum_{d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})}\tilde{c}_{d}^{2}e^{(n+2d)t}\right)

is
C^{\infty}
in
t\in(-\infty,\log R)
and

\displaystyle(H^{\kappa})^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})}\tilde{c}_{d}^{2}e^{(n+2d)t}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})}\tilde{c}_{d}^{2}e^{(n+2d)t}(n+2d)\right)\,;

\displaystyle(H^{\kappa})^{\prime\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})}\tilde{c}_{d}^{2}e^{(n+2d)t}\right)^{-2}\left[\left(\sum_{d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})}\tilde{c}_{d}^{2}e^{(n+2d)t}\right)\left(\sum_{d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})}\tilde{c}_{d}^{2}e^{(n+2d)t}(n+2d)^{2}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;-\left.\left(\sum_{d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})}\tilde{c}_{d}^{2}e^{(n+2d)t}(n+2d)\right)^{2}\right]\,.

By Cauchy-Schwarz,
(H^{\kappa})^{\prime\prime}(t)\geq 0
and equality holds for some
t
if and only if all the
\tilde{c}_{d}
but one vanish. This easily implies both (i) and (ii).
∎

Corollary 3.26

(Decay order monotonicity)
.

Let
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{R})\setminus\{0\}
,
\kappa\geq 0
. Then:

(i)

\varrho\mapsto G^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\varrho)
is non-decreasing in
(0,R/2)
.

(ii)

\varrho\mapsto G^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\varrho)
equals the constant
d\in\mathbb{R}
on some sub-interval of
(0,R/2)
if and only if
u
is the restriction to
B_{R}
of a
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{d}(\mathcal{C})
element, with
d=2
if
\kappa>0
.

(iii)

The following limit exists,

G^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{C}}(u;0):=\lim_{\varrho\to 0}G^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\varrho)=\begin{cases}d_{0}(u)&\text{ if }\kappa=0,\\
\min\{2,d_{0}(u)\}&\text{ if }\kappa>0,\end{cases}

with
d_{0}(u)\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})
as in Definition
3.27
below.

Definition 3.27
.

For all
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{R})\setminus\{0\}
, we define

d_{0}(u)=\min\{d:u_{d}\neq 0\text{ in \eqref{Equ_Jac field_L^2 decomp of u}}\}.

This is well-defined by (iii) of Proposition
3.19
.

Proof of Corollary
3.26
.

Recall that

\displaystyle G^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\varrho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}\left((2\varrho)^{-n-2}\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2\varrho}}u^{2}+\kappa\cdot(2\varrho)^{2}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}\left(\varrho^{-n-2}\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{\varrho}}u^{2}+\kappa\cdot\varrho^{2}\right)+1

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(-n-2+\frac{H^{\kappa}(\log(2\varrho))-H^{\kappa}(\log(\varrho))}{\log 2}\right)+1\,,

where
H^{\kappa}
is as in Lemma
3.25
. By (i) in Lemma
3.25
,

\frac{d}{d\varrho}G^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\varrho)=\frac{(H^{\kappa})^{\prime}(\log(2\varrho))-(H^{\kappa})^{\prime}(\log(\varrho))}{2\varrho\log 2}\geq 0\,,

since
(H^{\kappa})^{\prime}
is non-decreasing. This also gives (ii) thanks to (ii) in Lemma
3.25
.

We turn to (iii). It follows from our definition of
d_{0}=d_{0}(u)
and from (
3.8
) that

H^{0}(\log(\varrho))=\log(c_{d_{0}}^{2}\varrho^{n+2d_{0}}(1+o(1)))\text{ as }\varrho\to 0.

Therefore,

\lim_{\varrho\to 0}G^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\varrho)=\frac{1}{2}\left(-n+\frac{(n+2d_{0})\log 2}{\log 2}\right)=d_{0}\,.

The argument when
\kappa>0
is similar, so this completes the proof.
∎

We recall that
G_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\varrho)=G^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\varrho)
. This is all we will need to discuss in the remainder of this section.
9
9

9

We will always know that
\kappa=0
whenever the subsequent results are invoked.
We introduce suitably translated and rescaled decay orders.

Definition 3.28
.

Let
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2})\setminus\{0\}
,
\mathbf{y}\in\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2}
, and
\varrho\in(0,2-|\mathbf{y}|)
. We define

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\mathbf{y},\varrho)

\displaystyle:=G_{\mathcal{C}}(u(\mathbf{y}+\cdot);\varrho)\,;

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle:=G_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\mathbf{y},0):=G_{\mathcal{C}}(u(\mathbf{y}+\cdot);0)\,.

Lemma 3.29
.

G_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\mathbf{y})
is upper-semi-continuous in convergence of
\mathbf{y}\in\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2}
and
u\in L^{2}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{\tau}(\mathbf{y}))
,
\tau>0
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\varrho\mapsto G_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\varrho)
is non-decreasing (Corollary
3.26
).
∎

Next we investigate the relationship between the spectral decomposition of Jacobi fields and the decay order. We agree on the following notation. If
f
is an
\mathbb{R}
-valued real-analytic function on
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
y_{0}\in U
, we’ll write

\operatorname{ord}_{f}(y_{0})

for the degree of the lowest non-zero term in the Taylor expansion of
f
about
y_{0}
. We will apply this to
\beta
-harmonic
h:B_{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}
as follows. Recall that
h
extends to
\{r=0\}
by Proposition
3.16
. We may then write

\operatorname{ord}_{h(0,\cdot)}(y_{0})

to mean what was defined above.

Lemma 3.30
.

Let
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{R})\setminus\{0\}
, and define
h_{j}
,
j\geq 1
, as in Lemma
3.20
. Then, for every
\mathbf{y}_{0}=(0,y_{0})\in\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}\cap B_{R}
we have

(3.9)

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\mathbf{y}_{0})=\inf\{\gamma_{j}+\operatorname{ord}_{h_{j}(0,\cdot)}(y_{0}):j\geq 1\}\,.

Proof.

It suffices to consider
\mathbf{y}_{0}=\mathbf{0}=(0,0)
. By Proposition
3.19
(iii),

u=\sum_{d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})}u_{d}

in
L^{2}
. Observe that

p_{d,j}(r,y):=r^{-\gamma_{j}}\int_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}u_{d}(r\omega,y)\psi_{j}(\omega)\ d\omega

either vanishes or is a degree-
(d-\gamma_{j})
homogeneous polynomial. Using elliptic estimates to exchange the sum and integral, we see that

h_{j}(r,y)=\sum_{d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})}p_{d,j}(r,y)

so

\gamma_{j}+\operatorname{ord}_{h_{j}(0,\cdot)}(0)=\inf\{d:p_{d,j}\neq 0,\;d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})\}.

On the other hand, there must be some
j
so that
p_{d_{0},j}\neq 0
by completeness of the
\psi_{j}
. Thus we find

\inf\{\gamma_{j}+\operatorname{ord}_{h_{j}(0,\cdot)}(0):j\geq 1\}=\inf\{d:p_{d,j}\neq 0,\;d\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C}),\;j\geq 1\}=d_{0}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

We now arrive at our fundamental “splitting” result for homogeneous Jacobi fields, Proposition
3.31
, according to high decay order points. This is akin to cone-splitting along high-density points.

Proposition 3.31
.

Let
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{1}(\mathcal{C})\cap(\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,1}(\mathcal{C}))^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
, where
\perp
is with respect to
L^{2}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{1})
. Denote

\mathcal{J}_{u}:=\operatorname{span}\{u\}+\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,0}(\mathcal{C})+\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{pos}}^{*}(\mathcal{C}).

Then:

(i)

The following is a linear subspace of
\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}
:

V_{u}:=\{\mathbf{y}_{0}\in\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}:G_{\mathcal{C}}(w;\mathbf{y}_{0})\geq 1\text{ for some }w\in\mathcal{J}_{u}\setminus\{0\}\}.

(ii)

u
is invariant under translations in
V_{u}
, i.e.,

u(\mathbf{y}_{0}+\cdot)=u\text{ for all }\mathbf{y}_{0}\in V_{0}.

Remark 3.32
.

In our geometric applications of Proposition
3.31
, via Corollary
3.34
, we will take
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
strongly integrable to have access to Remark
3.22
. We will study minimal hypersurfaces modeled on
\operatorname{graph}_{\mathcal{C}}u
modulo translations (
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textnormal{trl}}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,0}(\mathcal{C})
) and one-sided deformations (
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textnormal{pos}}(\mathcal{C})
). Our function
u
will have linear decay and
\mathcal{C}
will have been tilted to begin with so that
u
is
\perp
to
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textnormal{rot}}(\mathcal{C})
(
=\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{1,0}(\mathcal{C})\oplus\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,1}(\mathcal{C})\supset\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,1}(\mathcal{C})
).

Proof of Proposition
3.31
.

By Proposition
3.19
(ii) we have the decomposition

(3.10)

u(r\omega,y)=\sum_{j\geq 1}r^{\gamma_{j}}\psi_{j}(\omega)p_{j}(r,y)

where

p_{j}(r,y)=r^{-\gamma_{j}}\int_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}u(r\omega,y)\psi_{j}(\omega)\,d\omega\,.

Note that
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{1}(\mathcal{C})\cap(\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,1}(\mathcal{C}))^{\perp}
implies

(3.11)

p_{j}\neq 0\implies\gamma_{j}\neq 0.

Moreover, Proposition
3.16
implies that the non-vanishing
p_{j}
are homogeneous polynomials in
(r,y)
with only even powers of
r
and with

(3.12)

\deg p_{j}=\deg p_{j}(0,\cdot)=1-\gamma_{j}.

Together, (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) imply that

(3.13)

p_{j}\neq 0\implies\gamma_{j}\in\{\ldots,-3,-2,-1\}\cup\{1\}

In particular, there are only finitely many
p_{j}\neq 0
since
\gamma_{j}\to\infty
as
j\to\infty
.

We will show that
V_{u}=\hat{V}_{u}
where

\hat{V}_{u}:=\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq 1\\
p_{j}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\{\mathbf{y}=(0,y)\in\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}:\operatorname{ord}_{p_{j}(0,\cdot)}(y)\geq 1-\gamma_{j}=\operatorname{ord}_{p_{j}(0,\cdot)}(0)\}.

Indeed, by Lemma
D.2
,
\hat{V}_{u}
is an intersection of linear subspaces of
\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}
, so a linear subspace itself, and each
p_{j}\neq 0
is invariant under translations in the
\hat{V}_{u}
directions (of course, so are
p_{j}=0
). Thus,
V_{u}=\hat{V}_{u}
will imply both (i) and (ii).

Proof that
V_{u}\subset\hat{V}_{u}
. Let
\mathbf{y}_{0}\in V_{u}
. Note that

G_{\mathcal{C}}(\varphi;\mathbf{y}_{0})\leq 0

for every
\varphi\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,0}(\mathcal{C})+\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textrm{pos}}(\mathcal{C})
, so having
\mathbf{y}_{0}\in V
means that

(3.14)

G_{\mathcal{C}}(u+\varphi;\mathbf{y}_{0})\geq 1

for some
\varphi\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,0}(\mathcal{C})+\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textrm{pos}}(\mathcal{C})
.
Define, for
j\geq 1
,

\hat{p}_{j}(r,y):=r^{-\gamma_{j}}\int_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}(u+\varphi)(r\omega,y)\psi_{j}(\omega)\,d\omega.

Our choice of
\mathbf{y}_{0}=(0,y_{0})\in V_{u}
, Lemma
3.30
, (
3.12
), and (
3.14
) give

(3.15)

\operatorname{ord}_{\hat{p}_{j}(0,\cdot)}(y_{0})\geq 1-\gamma_{j}=\deg p_{j}(0,\cdot)=\operatorname{ord}_{p_{j}(0,\cdot)}(0)

whenever
p_{j}\neq 0
. To conclude that
\mathbf{y}_{0}\in\hat{V}_{u}
, we need to replace
\hat{p}_{j}
with
p_{j}
on the left hand side of (
3.15
). There are two cases to consider.

•

If
j\geq 2
, then
\gamma_{j}\neq\gamma_{1}
. Moreover,
\gamma_{j}\neq 0
by (
3.13
). Then, Lemma
3.21
together with
\varphi\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,0}(\mathcal{C})+\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\gamma_{1},0}(\mathcal{C})
imply
\hat{p}_{j}=p_{j}
, as desired.

•

If
j=1
, then
\gamma_{j}=\gamma_{1}<0
by Remark
3.10
, so

\deg p_{1}(0,\cdot)=1-\gamma_{1}\geq 2

by (
3.12
) whenever
p_{j}\neq 0
. Lemma
3.21
and
\varphi\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,0}(\mathcal{C})+\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textrm{pos}}(\mathcal{C})
guarantee that

\hat{p}_{1}=p_{1}+c_{1}

for some
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore, (
3.15
) and Lemma
D.1
imply that

D_{y}^{\alpha}\hat{p}_{1}(0,y_{0})=0\text{ for all }|\alpha|\leq\deg p_{1}-1.

Applying this with
|\alpha|=\deg p_{1}-1\geq 1
cancels the
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
in
\hat{p}_{1}=p_{1}+c_{1}
, so

D_{y}^{\alpha}p_{1}(0,y_{0})=0\text{ for all }|\alpha|=\deg p_{1}-1.

Thus, Lemma
D.2
and (
3.12
) imply that (
3.15
) does hold with
p_{1}
in place of
\hat{p}_{1}
on the left hand side.

This completes the proof that
V_{u}\subset\hat{V}_{u}
.

Proof that
\hat{V}_{u}\subset V_{u}
. Let
\mathbf{y}_{0}=(0,y_{0})\in\hat{V}_{u}
. Note that

u(r\omega,y_{0}+y)=u(r\omega,y),

where we used the decomposition (
3.10
) and that, by Lemma
D.2
, each
p_{j}
is invariant under translations in the
\hat{V}_{u}
directions. Thus

u(\mathbf{y}_{0}+\cdot)=u\in\mathcal{J}_{u}.

By assumption,
G_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\mathbf{y}_{0})\geq 1
, which implies that
\mathbf{y}_{0}\in V_{u}
. Thus,
\hat{V}_{u}\subset V_{u}
as claimed, which completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.33
.

One may of course prove more general versions of Proposition
3.31
.

We could have taken
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{1}(\mathcal{C})\setminus\{0\}
and (i) would remain true but (ii) would not, as one can see by taking
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,1}(\mathcal{C})
that rotates
\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}
. One can instead show that (ii) holds in general for the projection of
u
onto
(\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,1}(\mathcal{C}))^{\perp}
with minor modifications.

Generalizing further, one could have taken
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{d}(\mathcal{C})
in place of
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{1}(\mathcal{C})
and suitable replacements of
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,0}(\mathcal{C})
,
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textnormal{pos}}(\mathcal{C})
with
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\hat{\gamma},\hat{q}}(\mathcal{C})
with
\hat{\gamma}+\hat{q}<d
. Each such
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\hat{\gamma},\hat{q}}(\mathcal{C})
would introduce a constraint of working
\perp
to
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\hat{\gamma},d-\hat{\gamma}}(\mathcal{C})
provided
\hat{\gamma}+\hat{q}>d-2
.

We arrive at a quantitative version of Proposition
3.31
whose proof strongly requires an additional assumption on our underlying regular hypercone
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
, namely, that it be strongly integrable (see Definition
3.12
).

Corollary 3.34
.

Suppose that the factor
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
of
\mathcal{C}
is also strongly integrable.
10
10

10

So far,
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
had only been assumed to be a regular and strictly stable minimal hypercone.
Then, for each
\theta>0
, there exists
\varrho=\varrho(\mathcal{C},\theta)>0
with the following significance.

Consider any
u\in\operatorname{Jac}_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{C})\cap(\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{rot}}(\mathcal{C}))^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
, where
\perp
is with respect to
L^{2}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{1})
. Let

\mathcal{J}_{u}:=\operatorname{span}\{u\}+\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{trl}}(\mathcal{C})+\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{pos}}^{*}(\mathcal{C}).

Then, there exists a proper linear subspace
V_{u}\subsetneq\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}
so that

G_{\mathcal{C}}(w;\mathbf{y},\varrho)<1-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{<1}

for every
w\in\mathcal{J}_{u}\setminus\{0\}
,
\mathbf{y}\in\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}\cap\bar{B}_{1}\setminus B_{\theta}(V)
; here
\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{<1}
is as in Remark
3.18
.

Of course, the right hand side could have been any
1-\delta
with
\delta\in(0,\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{<1})
, in which case,
\varrho
would also have depend on our choice of
\delta
.

Proof.

Suppose for contradiction that the assertion fails for some
\theta>0
and a sequence of
u_{j}\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{1}(\mathcal{C})\cap(\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{rot}}(\mathcal{C}))^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
,
\varrho_{j}=j^{-1}
.

Normalize so that
\|u_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{1})}=1
. Using the Caccioppoli inequality in Lemma
3.14
and homogeneity we can pass to a subsequence (not relabled) so that
u_{j}
converges to a Jacobi field
u_{\infty}\in\operatorname{Jac}_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{C})\cap(\operatorname{Jac}_{\textrm{rot}}(\mathcal{C}))^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{1})
. Note that
(\operatorname{Jac}_{\textrm{rot}}(\mathcal{C}))^{\perp}\subset\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,1}(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}
and
\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{trl}}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{0,0}(\mathcal{C})
by the strong integrability assumption (Remark
3.22
), so Proposition
3.31
applies and furnishes the linear subspace

V_{u_{\infty}}=\{\mathbf{y}\in\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}:G_{\mathcal{C}}(w;\mathbf{y})\geq 1\text{ for some }w\in\mathcal{J}_{u_{\infty}}\setminus\{0\}\}

of
\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}
along which
u
is translation invariant.

We claim that
V_{u_{\infty}}\subsetneq\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}
. Otherwise,
u_{\infty}
would be the trivial extension of a
u_{\infty}^{\circ}\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
along
\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}
. The strong integrability of
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
implies that
u_{\infty}^{\circ}\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textnormal{rot}}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
and thus
u_{\infty}\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textnormal{rot}}(\mathcal{C})
, a contradiction to
u_{\infty}\in(\operatorname{Jac}_{\textrm{rot}}(\mathcal{C}))^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
. Thus,

V_{u_{\infty}}\subsetneq\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C},

as claimed.

We may thus invoke
V_{u_{\infty}}
in our construction of
u_{j}
as counterexamples to the theorem. For each
j
there exist
w_{j}\in\mathcal{J}_{u_{j}}\setminus\{0\}
and
\mathbf{y}_{j}\in\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}\cap\bar{B}_{1}\setminus B_{\theta}(V_{u_{\infty}})
such that

G_{\mathcal{C}}(w_{j};\mathbf{y}_{j})\geq 1-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{<1}.

By renormalizing the
w_{j}
as we did the
u_{j}
, the scaling invariance of decay order, upper-semicontinuity of the decay order under
L^{2}
-convergence (see Lemma
3.29
), and after passing to a subsequence, we obtain
w_{\infty}\in\mathcal{J}_{u_{\infty}}\setminus\{0\}
and
\mathbf{y}_{\infty}\in\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}\cap\bar{B}_{1}\setminus B_{\theta}(V_{u_{\infty}})
so that

\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{C}}(w_{\infty};\mathbf{y}_{\infty})\geq 1-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{<1}.

Recalling Corollary
3.26
(iii) and the definition of
\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{<1}
, it follows that

\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{C}}(w_{\infty};\mathbf{y}_{\infty})\geq 1.

This implies
\mathbf{y}_{\infty}\in V_{u_{\infty}}
, a contradiction. This completes the proof.
∎

4.
Minimal hypersurfaces modeled by Jacobi fields on cylindrical hypercones

We build on the previous section’s linear theory to study a (geometric)
L^{2}
decay order over certain cylindrical hypercones
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
for minimal hypersurfaces in
(n+1)
-dimensional Riemannian manifolds.
11
11

11

In our subsequent geometric applications, we will take
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
, with
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
a minimizing quadratic hypercone (see Appendix
A
).

We fix once and for all a Riemannian manifold
(M^{n+1},g)
satisfying

|\operatorname{Rm}|+|\nabla\operatorname{Rm}|+|\nabla^{2}\operatorname{Rm}|\leq C^{-1}\text{ on }M

where
C\geq C(n+1)
, with
C(n+1)
as in Appendix
E.1
. We also take
C
large enough (depending only on
C(n+1)
) for the monotonicity inequality to hold for the weighted area-ratios

\Theta^{1}_{\Sigma}(p,R):=e^{R}\Theta_{\Sigma}(p,R)=e^{R}\frac{\|\Sigma\|(B_{R}(p))}{\omega_{n}R^{n}},\;R\in(0,2];

see
[
Sim83
, §17]
. We will often work with points in the subset

\breve{M}=\{p\in M:\operatorname{injrad}(M,g,p)\geq 2\}.

We also fix the notation

\displaystyle\eta_{p,\tau}(\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle:=\exp_{p}(\tau\mathbf{v}),

\displaystyle g_{p,\tau}

\displaystyle:=\tau^{-2}\eta_{p,\tau}^{*}\;g.

4.1.
The decay order

Fix
p\in\breve{M}
and endow
T_{p}M
with the flat metric
\bar{g}=g_{p}
. Let
C\subset T_{p}M
be a nonempty closed set that divides
T_{p}M
into two components,
\Omega_{+}
,
\Omega_{-}
. Denote the signed distance function to
C
in
\subset T_{p}M
as:

(4.1)

\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{C}(\mathbf{x}):=\begin{cases}d_{\bar{g}}(\mathbf{x},C)&\text{ if }\mathbf{x}\in\Omega_{+}\\
-d_{\bar{g}}(\mathbf{x},C)&\text{ if }\mathbf{x}\in\Omega_{-},\\
0&\text{ if }\mathbf{x}\in C.\end{cases}

In practice,
C
will be
\operatorname{spt}\mathcal{C}_{p}
for some copy
\mathcal{C}_{p}:=\iota_{p}(\mathcal{C})
of
\mathcal{C}
into
T_{p}M
under a
linear
isometry
\iota_{p}:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to(T_{p}M,g_{p})
. We will always abuse notation and denote such
\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{C}
by
\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}
.

Let
\Sigma\subset M
be a hypersurface with finite area in compact subsets of
M
. For
\tau\in(0,2]
, we define

(4.2)

\mathbf{d}_{C}(\Sigma;p,\tau):=\|\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\tau^{-1}C}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{p,\tau}\cap B_{1})},

where
\tau^{-1}C
denotes a standard rescaling in the vector space
T_{p}M
(note that it equals
C
whenever
C
is a hypercone in
T_{p}M
) and

\Sigma_{p,\tau}:=\eta_{p,\tau}^{-1}(\Sigma\cap B_{2}(p))\subset T_{p}M,\\

It is easy to check that for
\varrho\in(0,1)
,
\tau\in(0,2]
,

\mathbf{d}_{C}(\Sigma;p,\varrho\tau)=\varrho^{-\frac{n}{2}-1}\|\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\tau^{-1}C}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{p,\tau}\cap B_{\varrho})}.

Definition 4.1

(cf. Definition
3.23
)
.

Let
p
,
S
,
\Sigma
be as above, and
\hat{\kappa}>0
. We define the
\hat{\kappa}
-adjusted
decay order
of
\Sigma
with respect to
S\subset T_{p}M
at scale
\tau\in(0,1]
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\hat{\kappa}}_{C}(\Sigma;p,\tau)

\displaystyle:=1+\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}(\mathbf{d}_{C}(\Sigma;p,2\tau)^{2}+\hat{\kappa}\cdot(2\tau)^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}(\mathbf{d}_{C}(\Sigma;p,\tau)^{2}+\hat{\kappa}\cdot\tau^{2}).

We fix
\hat{\kappa}:=1
once and for all.

Remark 4.2

(cf. Remark
3.24
)
.

We encourage the first-time reader to read this section keeping in mind that for all applications to
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
we can make these simplifications:

•

\mathcal{N}^{{1}}_{S}
can be replaced with
\mathcal{N}^{0}_{S}
after suitably extending to
\hat{\kappa}=0
in Definition
4.1
.
12
12

12

Note, however, that
\mathcal{N}^{0}_{S}(\Sigma;p,\tau)
suffers from the drawback of being ill-defined if
\Sigma=S
in
B_{\tau}(p)
.

•

\Theta^{1}_{\Sigma}
can be replaced by the usual monotone density
\Theta_{\Sigma}
.

•

Decay bounds
\mathcal{N}^{\hat{\kappa}}_{C}(\Sigma;p,\tau)\leq\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{<2}
can be relaxed to
\mathcal{N}^{0}_{C}(\Sigma;p,\tau)\leq\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
13
13

13

The requirement
\Lambda<2
allows us to disregard the influence of a non-flat background metric
g
; cf. Proposition
4.5
. If we were working in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
this would not be necessary.

Lemma 4.3
.

It always holds that
\mathcal{N}^{1}_{C}(\Sigma;p,\tau)\geq-\frac{n}{2}
.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{C}(\Sigma;p,\tau)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n+2}\mathbf{d}_{C}(\Sigma;p,2\tau)^{2},

\displaystyle\tau^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n+2}(2\tau)^{2}.

This proves the assertion.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

If
\mathcal{N}^{1}_{C}(\Sigma;p,\tau)\leq\Lambda<2
then:
14
14

14

If we were working in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with
\hat{\kappa}=0
(cf. Remark
4.2
), then (i) would need to be omitted.

(i)

\tau^{2}\leq C(\Lambda)\mathbf{d}_{C}(\Sigma;p,\tau)^{2}
, and

(ii)

\mathbf{d}_{C}(\Sigma;p,2\tau)\leq 2^{\Lambda-1}\mathbf{d}_{C}(\Sigma;p,\tau)
.

Proof.

We may rearrange
\mathcal{N}^{1}_{C}(\Sigma;p,\tau)\leq\Lambda
into

\mathbf{d}_{C}(\Sigma;p,2\tau)^{2}+2^{2}(1-2^{2(\Lambda-2)})\tau^{2}\leq 2^{2\Lambda-2}\mathbf{d}_{C}(\Sigma;p,\tau)^{2}.

This proves the assertion.
∎

4.2.
Jacobi fields on cylindrical hypercones

We construct Jacobi fields whenever mass-minimizing boundaries in
(M,g)
suitably converge to suitable cylindrical hypercones, and track properties about their decay rates. Throughout the section we fix

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

where
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
is a regular, strictly stable (see Definition
3.9
), and strictly minimizing (see Definition
H.2
) hypercone. Note that
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
does
not
live in
T_{p}M
, so we will need to study its copies in
T_{p}M
, i.e.
\iota_{p}(\mathcal{C})
for
linear
isometries
\iota:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to(T_{p}M,g_{p})
. All our results also clearly apply to all rotations of such
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

For notational brevity, we will say
\Sigma
is minimizing if it is an minimizing boundary in
M
, and will continue to identify
\Sigma
with its regular part in interpreting
\mathbf{d}_{\iota_{p}(\mathcal{C})}(\Sigma;p,\tau)
and
\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\iota_{p}(\mathcal{C})}(\Sigma;p,\tau)
.

Proposition 4.5

(Jacobi fields via non-concentration)
.

Fix
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{<2}
.

Consider minimizing
\Sigma_{i}
,
p_{i}\in\breve{M}\cap\operatorname{spt}\Sigma_{i}
, copies
\mathcal{C}_{i}:=\iota_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{C})\subset T_{p_{i}}M
, and
\tau_{i}>0
such that

(1)

\tau_{i}\to 0
,

(2)

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})\to 0
, and

(3)

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})\leq\Lambda
.

Then:

(i)

For each fixed
\varrho\in(0,2)
we have the following non-concentration inequality

\limsup_{i\to\infty}\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})^{-2}\int_{(\Sigma_{i})_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\cap B_{\varrho}}r^{-2}\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}^{2}<\infty\,,

where
r
denotes the distance to
\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}_{i}\subset T_{p_{i}}M
with respect to
g_{p_{i}}
.

(ii)

For each
i
, there is an open
U_{i}\subset\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2}
such that the closest-point projection
\Pi
to
\operatorname{spt}\mathcal{C}
is a diffeomorphism from some
V_{i}\subset\iota_{p_{i}}^{-1}((\Sigma_{i})_{p_{i},\tau_{i}})\cap B_{2}
onto
U_{i}
. We may take
U_{i}
to be an increasing exhaustion of
\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2}
. If
h_{i}:U_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
is

h_{i}\circ\Pi:=\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}\circ\iota_{p_{i}}\;(=\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}})\text{ on }V_{i}

(the “height” function) then, after passing to a subsequence, we have

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})^{-1}h_{i}\to u\text{ in }C^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2}),

for some
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2})
with
\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{1})}=1
.

(iii)

After passing to a further subsequence so that (recall Lemma
4.4
)

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})^{-2}\tau_{i}^{2}\to\kappa\in[0,C(\Lambda)),

it holds that
\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\varrho\tau_{i})\to G^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\varrho)
for any
\varrho\in(0,1)
and
G_{\mathcal{C}}^{\kappa}(u;1)\leq\Lambda
.

Proof.

By combining Simon’s non-concentration estimates (see Theorem
H.1
) with Lemma
E.1
we have that for
\varrho\in(0,2)
fixed,

\int_{(\Sigma_{i})_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\cap B_{\varrho}}r^{-2}\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}^{2}\leq C(\varrho)\left(\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2\tau_{i})^{2}+\tau_{i}^{2}\right).

Using Lemma
4.4
, we find

\int_{(\Sigma_{i})_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\cap B_{\varrho}}r^{-2}\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}^{2}\leq C(\varrho,\Lambda)\,\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})^{2}.

This proves (i), the non-concentration inequality.

For (ii), the well-definedness of
h_{i}
is standard; namely, it follows from how (2) implies that
\iota_{p_{i}}^{-1}((\Sigma_{i})_{p_{i},\tau_{i}})\cap B_{2}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2}
together with Allard’s theorem
[
All72
]
. Then, again using Lemma
4.4
and standard elliptic estimates we find that the rescaling

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})^{-1}h_{i}

converges in
C^{2}_{\textrm{loc}}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2})
to a Jacobi field
u\in\operatorname{Jac}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2})
. We used Lemmas
4.4
,
E.1
to see that the difference between the Riemannian metrics
g_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}
(with respect to which the graph of
h_{i}
is minimal) and
g_{p_{i}}
implies that its mean curvature satisfies the following estimates

H_{\Sigma_{i},g_{p_{i}}}=H_{\Sigma_{i},g_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}}+O(\tau^{2})=O(\tau^{2})\,,

uniformly on compact subsets of
B_{2}\setminus\operatorname{sing}\mathcal{C}
. Thus,
H_{\Sigma_{i},g_{p_{i}}}
is negligible compared to the normalizing factor
\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})
in the
C^{2}_{\textrm{loc}}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2})
convergence described above.

By covering
\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2}
by cubes whose doubling is contained in
B_{2}
the estimates of the graphical function Simon’s non-concentration estimates (see Theorem
H.1
) imply that
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2})
. Note that the non-concentration estimate gives

(4.3)
