\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})^{-2}\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\varrho\tau_{i})^{2}=\varrho^{-n-2}\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{\varrho}}u^{2}

for all
\varrho\in(0,2)
. This proves that
\|u\|_{L^{2}(B_{1})}=1
, completing the proof of (ii).

Finally, we relate the non-linear and linear decay orders. Lemma
4.4
implies that after passing to a subsequence, we have

\kappa:=\lim_{i\to\infty}\frac{\tau_{i}^{2}}{\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})^{2}}\in[0,C(\Lambda)).

Combined with (
4.3
) this gives

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}(\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})^{2}+\tau_{i}^{2})^{-1}(\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\varrho\tau_{i})^{2}+(\varrho\tau_{i})^{2})

\displaystyle=(1+\kappa)^{-1}\varrho^{-n-2}\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{\varrho}}u^{2}+(1+\kappa)^{-1}\kappa\varrho^{2}

for all
\varrho\in(0,2)
. In particular,

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\varrho\tau_{i})\to G_{\mathcal{C}}^{\kappa}(u;\varrho)\text{ for all }\varrho\in(0,1).

Finally, we have that

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2\tau_{i})^{2}\geq\varrho^{n+2}\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2\varrho\tau_{i})^{2}

for any
\varrho\in(0,1)
so

\displaystyle\liminf_{i\to\infty}(\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})^{2}+\tau_{i}^{2})^{-1}(\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2\tau_{i})^{2}+(2\tau_{i})^{2})

\displaystyle\geq(1+\kappa)^{-1}2^{-n-2}\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2\varrho}}u^{2}+(1+\kappa)^{-1}\kappa 2^{2}.

This gives
G_{\mathcal{C}}^{\kappa}(u;1)\leq\Lambda
after sending
\varrho\nearrow 1
.
∎

4.3.
Decay order monotonicity

We continue to denote

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

where
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
is a regular, strictly stable, strictly minimizing hypercone, and to study its isometric copies in various
T_{p}M
’s.

Recall that, for
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{R})\setminus\{0\}
, the decay order
\varrho\mapsto G^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\varrho)
is non-decreasing in
(0,R/2)
by Corollary
3.26
, and if it is constant on an interval then
u
is homogeneous of degree
G^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{C}}(u;\cdot)\in\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})
.

Lemma 4.6

(Decay order monotonicity)
.

Fix
\delta>0
. There exist
\varepsilon
,
\lambda>0
,
L\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
depending on
\mathcal{C}
,
\delta
with the following property.

Consider minimizing
\Sigma
,
p\in\breve{M}\cap\operatorname{spt}\Sigma
, a copy
\mathcal{C}_{p}:=\iota_{p}(\mathcal{C})\subset T_{p}M
,
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{<2}\setminus B_{\delta}(\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C}))
such that:

(1)

\ell\geq L
,

(2)

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})\leq\varepsilon
, and

(3)

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})\geq\Lambda
.

Then,
\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell+1})\geq\Lambda+\lambda
.

Proof.

Suppose not. Then, there are
\Sigma_{i}
,
p_{i}\in\breve{M}\cap\operatorname{spt}\Sigma_{i}
,
\mathcal{C}_{i}:=\iota_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{C})\subset T_{p_{i}}M
,
\ell_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and
\Lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{<2}\setminus B_{\delta}(\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C}))
as above so that

(1’)

\ell_{i}\to\infty
,

(2’)

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-\ell_{i}})\to 0
,

(3’)

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-\ell_{i}})\geq\Lambda_{i}
,

but

(4.4)

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-\ell_{i}+1})\leq\Lambda_{i}+\lambda_{i}.

with
\lambda_{i}\to 0
. Passing to a subsequence, we may assume that

(4.5)

\Lambda:=\lim_{i}\Lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\leq 2}\setminus B_{\delta}(\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{C})).

Since
\lambda_{i}\to 0
, (
4.5
) and Remark
3.18
imply that for sufficiently large
i
,

(4.6)

\Lambda_{i}+\lambda_{i}\leq\Lambda+\tfrac{1}{2}\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{<2}

Note that (1’), (2’), (
4.4
), and (
4.6
) imply that Proposition
4.5
is applicable for all sufficiently large
i
with
\tau_{i}:=2^{-\ell_{i}+1}
and
\Lambda+\tfrac{1}{2}\delta
in place of
\Lambda
. After perhaps passing to a subsequence, we obtain a Jacobi field
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{2})
satisfying, for some
\kappa\geq 0
,

\Lambda\leq\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-\ell_{i}})=G^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{C}}(u;2^{-1})\leq G^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{C}}(u;1)\leq\Lambda,

where we used (3’) in the first step and the monotonicity of frequency (Corollary
3.26
) in the penultimate step. Thus, equality holds in the monotonicity of frequency so
u
is homogeneous with degree
\Lambda
. This contradicts the inclusion statement in (
4.5
).
∎

Lemma 4.7

(Implication of far-from-linear decay)
.

Fix
\delta
,
\varepsilon>0
. There exist
\eta>0
,
L\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
depending on
\mathcal{C}
,
\delta
,
\varepsilon
with the following property.

Consider minimizing
\Sigma
,
p\in\breve{M}\cap\operatorname{spt}\Sigma
, a copy
\mathcal{C}_{p}:=\iota_{p}(\mathcal{C})\subset T_{p}M
, and
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that:

(1)

\ell\geq L
,

(2)

\Theta^{1}_{\Sigma}(p,2^{-\ell+1})-\Theta^{1}_{\Sigma}(p,2^{-\ell})\leq\eta
, and

(3)

|\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})-1|\geq\delta
.

Then,
\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell+k})<\varepsilon
for each
k\in\{-1,0,1\}
.

Proof.

If not, there are
\Sigma_{i}
,
p_{i}\in\breve{M}\cap\operatorname{spt}\Sigma_{i}
,
\mathcal{C}_{i}:=\iota_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{C})\subset T_{p_{i}}M
, and
\ell_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
so that

(1’)

\ell_{i}\to\infty
,

(2’)

\Theta^{1}_{\Sigma_{i}}(p_{i},2^{-\ell_{i}+1})-\Theta^{1}_{\Sigma_{i}}(p_{i},2^{-\ell_{i}})\to 0
,

(3’)

|\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-\ell_{i}})-1|\geq\delta
,

but, for some fixed
k\in\{-1,0,1\}
,

(4.7)

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-\ell_{i}+k})\geq\varepsilon.

Let

\tilde{\Sigma}_{i}:=(\Sigma_{i})_{p_{i},2^{-\ell_{i}+k}}\subset T_{p_{i}}M.

By (1’) and (2’) we can pass to a subsequence so that
\iota_{p_{i}}^{-1}(\tilde{\Sigma}_{i})\rightharpoonup\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, a nonempty minimizing hypercone. Since
\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}
is
1
-Lipschitz, we have

(4.8)

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\varrho 2^{-\ell_{i}})\to\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{\mathcal{C}};\mathbf{0},\varrho)=\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{\mathcal{C}};\mathbf{0},1)\neq 0

for any fixed
\varrho>0
; the penultimate step is a consequence of the dilation invariance of
\mathcal{C}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
, and the last inequality follows since
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\neq\mathcal{C}
in view of (
4.7
).

Finally, (
4.8
) and the definition of decay order imply

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-\ell_{i}})\to 1+\tfrac{1}{2}\log_{2}\frac{\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{\mathcal{C}};\mathbf{0},2)}{\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{\mathcal{C}};\mathbf{0},1)}=1,

contradicting (3’).
∎

Lemma 4.8

(Decay is at least almost linear)
.

Fix
\delta>0
. There exist
\eta>0
,
L\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
depending on
\mathcal{C}
,
\delta
with the following property.

Consider minimizing
\Sigma
,
p\in\breve{M}\cap\operatorname{spt}\Sigma
, and
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that:

(1)

\ell\geq L
, and

(2)

\Theta^{1}_{\Sigma}(p,2^{-\ell+1})-\Theta_{\Sigma}(p)\leq\eta
.

Then,
\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\iota_{p}(\mathcal{C})}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})>1-\delta
for all linear isometries
\iota_{p}:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to(T_{p}M,g_{p})
.

Proof.

Take
\varepsilon
,
\lambda
,
L
so that decay order monotonicity (Lemma
4.6
) holds with
\delta
as given. Then take
\eta>0
and possibly increase
L
so that Lemma
4.7
holds with
\delta
and
\varepsilon
. Let
\iota_{p}
be any linear isometry.

Let us first show that

(4.9)

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell^{\prime}})>1-\delta\text{ for some }\ell^{\prime}\geq\ell.

If (
4.9
) failed, then iterating it would yield

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})^{2}+2^{-2\ell}\leq 2^{-\delta(\ell^{\prime}-\ell)}(\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell^{\prime}})^{2}+2^{-2\ell^{\prime}})\leq C(n)2^{-\delta(\ell^{\prime}-\ell)},

and sending
\ell^{\prime}\to\infty
would yield a contradiction. This proves (
4.9
).

Let
\ell^{\prime}
be such that (
4.9
) holds. If
\ell^{\prime}=\ell
, we are done. Otherwise, we apply Lemma
4.7
at scale
2^{-\ell^{\prime}}
to get
\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell^{\prime}})\leq\varepsilon
. Lemma
4.6
applied at scale
2^{-\ell^{\prime}}
gives

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell^{\prime}+1})>1-\delta+\lambda>1-\delta,

so (
4.9
) holds with
\ell^{\prime}+1
, too. Iterating upwards, we can get to
\ell^{\prime}=\ell
. This completes the proof.
∎

4.4.
Fast decay analysis

We continue to denote

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

where
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
is a regular, strictly stable, strictly minimizing hypercone.
We now define the isometry-independent quantities

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma;p,\tau)

\displaystyle:=\sup\{\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\iota_{p}(\mathcal{C})}(\Sigma;p,\tau):\text{ all isometries }\iota:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to(T_{p}M,g_{p})\},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma;p)

\displaystyle:=\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell}).

It follows from Lemma
4.8
that
\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma;p)\geq 1
when
\Sigma
is minimizing and
p\in\operatorname{spt}\Sigma
.
15
15

15

In fact, Lemma
4.8
implies
\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\iota_{p}(\mathcal{C})}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})\geq 1
for each fixed isometry
\iota
.
We call
\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma;p)>1
the
fast
decay case, and
\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma;p)=1
the
slow
decay case.

The following lemma is an important observation in our study of fast decay. Recall the definition of
\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{>1}
from Remark
3.18
.

Lemma 4.9

(Propagating fast decay to bigger scales)
.

Fix
\delta\in(0,\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{>1})
. There exist
\eta>0
,
L\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
depending on
\mathcal{C}
,
\delta
with the following property.

Consider minimizing
\Sigma
,
p\in\breve{M}\cap\operatorname{spt}\Sigma
, a copy
\mathcal{C}_{p}:=\iota_{p}(\mathcal{C})\subset T_{p}M
, and
L_{0},L_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that:

(1)

L_{1}>L_{0}\geq L
,

(2)

\Theta^{1}_{\Sigma}(p,2^{-\ell})-\Theta_{\Sigma}(p)\leq\eta
at
\ell=L_{0}
, and

(3)

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})\geq 1+\delta
at
\ell=L_{1}
.

Then,
\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})\geq 1+\delta
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
L_{0}\leq\ell\leq L_{1}
.

Proof.

Take
\varepsilon
,
\lambda
,
L
so that decay order monotonicity (Lemma
4.6
) holds with
\delta
as given. Then take
\eta>0
and possibly increase
L
so that Lemma
4.7
holds with
\delta
and
\varepsilon
.

Assuming the assertion fails, we can choose
\ell\in\{L_{0},\dots,L_{1}-1\}
to be the maximal (so the scale
2^{-\ell}
is minimal) counterexample. This gives

(4.10)

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})<1+\delta\leq\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell-1}).

By Lemma
4.7
, applied at scale
2^{-\ell-1}
by virtue of the right hand side of (
4.10
), we find that
\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell-1})\leq\varepsilon
. Lemma
4.6
applied at scale
2^{-\ell-1}
yields

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})\geq 1+\delta+\lambda>1+\delta,

which contradicts the left hand side of (
4.10
).
∎

Corollary 4.10

(Fast decay, qualitatively)
.

Consider minimizing
\Sigma
,
p\in\operatorname{spt}\Sigma
, and assume that
\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma;p)>1
.

Then, there exists a fixed linear isometry
\iota_{p}:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to(T_{p}M,g_{p})
such that
\mathcal{C}_{p}:=\iota_{p}(\mathcal{C})
satisfies:

(i)

\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})\geq 1+\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{>1}
, and

(ii)

\mathcal{C}_{p}
is the unique tangent cone to
\Sigma
at
p
.

Proof.

Claim (i) follows from iterating Lemma
4.9
and Lemma
4.6
. Claim (ii) follows from (i) and Lemma
4.7
.
∎

A straightforward quantitative consequence is:

Corollary 4.11

(Fast decay, quantitative)
.

Fix
\delta\in(0,\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{>1})
. There exist
\eta>0
,
L\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
depending on
\mathcal{C}
,
\delta
with the following property.

Consider minimizing
\Sigma
,
p\in\breve{M}\cap\operatorname{spt}\Sigma
, and
L_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that:

(1)

L_{0}\geq L
,

(2)

\Theta^{1}_{\Sigma}(p,2^{-\ell})-\Theta_{\Sigma}(p)\leq\eta
at
\ell=L_{0}
, and

(3)

\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma;p)>1
.

Let
\iota_{p}:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to(T_{p}M,g_{p})
be a linear isometry so that
\mathcal{C}_{p}:=\iota_{p}(\mathcal{C})
is the unique tangent cone to
\Sigma
at
p
(per Corollary
4.10
). Then, for all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\ell\geq L_{0}
,

(i)

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})\geq 1+\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{>1}-\delta
, and

(ii)

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})\leq C(n)\cdot 2^{-(\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{>1}-\delta)(\ell-L_{0})}
.

4.5.
Slow decay analysis

Lemma 4.12

(Jacobi fields in
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{1}\cap\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{rot}}^{\perp}
)
.

Consider minimizing
\Sigma_{i}
,
p_{i}\in\breve{M}\cap\operatorname{spt}\Sigma_{i}
, isometric copies
\mathcal{C}_{i}:=\iota_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{C})\subset T_{p_{i}}M
,
L_{0,i},L_{1,i},L_{2,i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
16
16

16

By monotonicity, one can always take
L_{0,i}:=L_{1,i}
. However, these two variables will be decoupled in our applications (see Remark
7.12
) so we have chosen to decouple them here too.
and
\delta_{i}\to 0
such that:

(1)

L_{1,i}\geq L_{0,i}\to\infty
,

(2)

L_{2,i}-L_{1,i}\to\infty
,

(3)

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-L_{2,i}})\to 0
,

(4)

\Theta^{1}_{\Sigma_{i}}(p_{i},2^{-L_{0,i}})-\Theta_{\Sigma_{i}}(p_{i})\to 0
, and

(5)

\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-\ell})\leq 1+\delta_{i}
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\ell\geq L_{1,i}
.

Then, there exist rotations
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}=\iota_{p_{i}}^{\prime}(\mathcal{C})
of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
with
\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{i};p,2^{-L_{2,i}})\to 0
so that Proposition
4.5
applies with
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}
in place of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
at all scales and yields a Jacobi field
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{1}(\mathcal{C})
which is
L^{2}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{1})
-orthogonal to
\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{rot}}(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}
.

Proof.

We first slightly regularize
\overline{\operatorname{dist}}
.
Let
\kappa>0
be such that
\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}
is a smooth function in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1}:|\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}(x)|<2\kappa|x|\}
; And let
\chi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be an odd nondecreasing function such that
\chi(s)=s
for
|s|\leq\kappa
and
\chi(s)=2\kappa
for
s\geq 2\kappa
and
\chi(s)=-2\kappa
for
s\leq-2\kappa
.

For a fixed copy
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\iota(\mathcal{C})\subset T_{p}M
, we denote by
r_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})
the distance from
\mathbf{x}\in T_{p}M
to
\operatorname{spine}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
.
We define the regularized signed distance function
\widehat{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}:T_{p}M\to\mathbb{R}
by

\widehat{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}):=r_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\chi\left(\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})/r_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})\right)\,.

Note that
(\widehat{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}})^{2}
is a 2-homogeneous
C^{1}
function on
T_{p}M
and there exists some constant
\hat{C}(n,\kappa)>1
such that

(4.11)

\displaystyle\widehat{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}=\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\text{on }\{\mathbf{x}:|\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})|\leq\kappa\,r_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})\}\,,

(4.12)

\displaystyle\hat{C}^{-1}\leq\;

\displaystyle\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\cdot\widehat{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})\leq\hat{C},

\displaystyle\forall\,\mathbf{x}\in T_{p}M\setminus\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\,.

Choose
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Rot}(\mathcal{C}_{i})
minimizing

(4.13)

\operatorname{Rot}(\mathcal{C}_{i})\ni\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\mapsto\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-L_{2,i}})^{2}:=\int_{(\Sigma_{i})_{p_{i},L_{2,i}}\cap B_{1}}(\widehat{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}})^{2}.

Clearly, (3) and (
4.12
) implies

(4.14)

\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-L_{2,i}}),\ \mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-L_{2,i}})\leq\hat{C}^{2}\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-L_{2,i}})\to 0.

Enlarging
\delta_{i}
if necessary while still preserving
\delta_{i}\to 0
, we can use (1), (4), and (5) to invoke Lemma
4.8
to conclude:

(4.15)

|\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-\ell})-1|\leq\delta_{i}\text{ for all }\ell>L_{1,i}.

In view of (2), (3), (
4.14
), and (
4.15
), we can apply Lemma
4.4
and Proposition
4.5
at all scales and obtain
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
and
\kappa\geq 0
satisfying

G_{\mathcal{C}}^{\kappa}(u;2^{k})=\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-L_{2,i}+k})=1

for all
k
. Thus,
u\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{1}(\mathcal{C})
and
\kappa=0
by Corollary
3.26
.

It remains to show that
u
is also
L^{2}(\mathcal{C}\cap B_{1})
-orthogonal to
\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{rot}}(\mathcal{C})
. We will use the minimizing nature of
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}
for (
4.13
). To that end note that, for all
\mathbf{A}\in\mathfrak{so}({n+1})
,

\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\widehat{\operatorname{dist}}_{e^{s\mathbf{A}}\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\widehat{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}}(e^{-s\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{x})=-\langle\nabla\widehat{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rangle

at all points
\mathbf{x}\notin\operatorname{spine}(\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime})
, where
\widehat{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}}
is differentiable; above,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and
\nabla
are taken in
T_{p_{i}}M
with respect to
g_{p_{i}}
. Since
(\widehat{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}})^{2}
is
C^{1}
, the minimizing nature of
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}
in (
4.13
) implies

(4.16)

\int_{(\Sigma_{i})_{p_{i},L_{2,i}}\cap B_{1}}\langle\nabla\widehat{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rangle\,\widehat{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})\ =0

for any
A\in\mathfrak{so}(n+1)
. We also recall that Proposition
4.5
and (
4.12
) give the non-concentration estimate

\limsup_{i\to\infty}\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},L_{2,i})^{-2}\int_{(\Sigma_{j})_{p_{i},L_{2,i}}\cap B_{1}}r^{-2}\widehat{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}}^{2}<\infty\,.

Since
\langle\nabla\widehat{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rangle
limits to
\langle\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x},\nu_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle
in
C^{2}_{\textrm{loc}}(\mathcal{C})
we can combine this with (
4.16
) and (
4.11
) to conclude that

\int_{\mathcal{C}\cap B_{1}}\langle\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x},\nu_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle\,u=0.

for all
\mathbf{A}\in\mathfrak{so}(n+1)
, completing the proof.
∎

We now impose the additional assumption that
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
is strongly integrable (cf. Definition
3.12
).

Theorem 4.13
.

Suppose that the factor
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
of
\mathcal{C}
is also strongly integrable.
17
17

17

So far,
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
had only been assumed to be a regular, strictly stable, and strictly minimizing hypercone.
Fix
\theta>0
. There exist
\varepsilon
,
\delta
,
\eta>0
,
L\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, depending on
\mathcal{C}
,
\theta
, with the following properties.

Consider minimizing
\Sigma
,
p\in\breve{M}\cap\operatorname{spt}\Sigma
, a copy
\mathcal{C}_{p}:=\iota_{p}(\mathcal{C})\subset T_{p}M
, and
L_{0},L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
18
18

18

Again, one could have taken
L_{0}:=L_{1}
, but see footnote
16
.
such that:

(1)

L_{1}\geq L_{0}\geq L
,

(2)

L_{2}-L_{1}\geq L
,

(3)

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{p}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})\leq\varepsilon
at
\ell=L_{2}
,

(4)

\Theta^{1}_{\Sigma}(p,2^{-\ell})-\Theta_{\Sigma}(p)\leq\eta
at
\ell=L_{0}
, and

(5)

\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})\leq 1+\delta
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\ell\geq L_{1}
.

There exists a proper linear subspace
V\subsetneq\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}
, so that if (4), (5) hold with
\Sigma^{+}
,
p^{+}
in place of
\Sigma
,
p
, and

(6)

p^{+}\in B_{2^{-L_{2}}}(p)
,

(7)

\Sigma^{+}
does not cross
\Sigma
smoothly,

then
p^{+}\in(\eta_{p,2^{-L_{2}}}\circ\iota_{p})(B_{\theta}(V))
.

Remark 4.14
.

Since
\Sigma
doesn’t cross itself, we are allowed to take
\Sigma^{+}=\Sigma
in Theorem
4.13
above. The theorem’s conclusion gives novel information in this case as well.

Proof of Theorem
4.13
.

Consider a sequence of counterexamples. That is,
\Sigma_{i}
,
p_{i}\in\breve{M}\cap\operatorname{spt}\Sigma_{i}
,
p_{i}\in M
,
\mathcal{C}_{i}:=\iota_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{C})\subset T_{p_{i}}M
,
\delta_{i}\to 0
,
L_{0,i},L_{1,i},L_{2,i},L_{i}\to\infty
contradicting the statement of the theorem. In particular, we are assuming that:

(1’)

L_{1,i}\geq L_{0,i}\geq L_{i}
,

(2’)

L_{2,i}-L_{1,i}\geq L_{i}
,

(3’)

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-L_{2,i}})\to 0
,

(4’)

\Theta^{1}_{\Sigma_{i}}(p_{i},2^{-L_{0,i}})-\Theta_{\Sigma_{i}}(p_{i})\to 0
, and

(5’)

\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},2^{-\ell})\leq 1+\delta_{i}
for all
\ell\geq L_{1,i}
.

By
L_{i}\to\infty
and (1’), (2’), (3’), (4’), and (5’) we can apply Lemma
4.12
to replace
\mathcal{C}_{i}
with a nearby element of
\operatorname{Rot}(\mathcal{C}_{i})
, not relabeled, so that Proposition
4.5
and Corollary
3.26
applied at all scales produce a Jacobi field
u\in\operatorname{Jac}_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{C})\cap(\operatorname{Jac}_{\textnormal{rot}}(\mathcal{C}))^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
. For later use, we introduce the notation

\tau_{i}:=2^{-L_{2,i}},

and, per Proposition
4.5
(ii) applied at scale
\tau_{i}
, we denote

h_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}^{\Sigma_{i}}\circ\Pi:=\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}\circ\iota_{p_{i}}=\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}

on a subset of
\iota_{p_{i}}^{-1}((\Sigma_{i})_{p_{i},\tau_{i}})
on which the closest-point projection
\Pi
to
\operatorname{spt}\mathcal{C}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is a diffeomorphism onto some open
U_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}^{\Sigma_{i}}\subset\mathcal{C}
. Note that
U_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}^{\Sigma_{i}}\to\mathcal{C}
as
i\to\infty
by (2’). By construction,

(4.17)

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})^{-1}h^{\Sigma_{i}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\to u\text{ in }C^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathcal{C}),

by passing to subsequences (not relabeled) as appropriate.

We fix

\mathcal{J}_{u}:=\operatorname{span}\{u\}+\operatorname{Jac}_{\textrm{trl}}(\mathcal{C})+\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textrm{pos}}(\mathcal{C}),

and then

V_{u}\subsetneq\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}

as in Corollary
3.34
. We proceed to define

V_{i}:=\iota_{p_{i}}(V_{u})\subset T_{p_{i}}M,

and take
\Sigma_{i}^{+}
,
p_{i}^{+}
to be such that:

(4”)

\Theta^{1}_{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}(p_{i}^{+},2^{-L_{0,i}})-\Theta^{1}_{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}(p_{i}^{+})\to 0
,

(5”)

\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma_{i}^{+};p_{i}^{+},2^{-\ell})\leq 1+\delta_{i}
for all
\ell\geq L_{1,i}
,

(6”)

p_{i}^{+}\in B_{\tau_{i}}(p_{i})
,

(7”)

\Sigma^{+}_{i}
does not cross
\Sigma_{i}
smoothly,

and violating the desired conclusion, i.e.,

(4.18)

p_{i}^{+}\in B_{\tau_{i}}(p_{i})\setminus\eta_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}(B_{\theta}(V_{i})).

Note that (1’), (4”), (6”), (7”), and Lemma
B.1
imply that

(4.19)

\iota_{p_{i}}^{-1}((\Sigma_{i}^{+})_{p_{i},\tau_{i}})\rightharpoonup\mathcal{C}.

After choosing
\mathbf{v}_{i}\in B_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
so that

p_{i}^{+}=\eta_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}(\iota_{p_{i}}(\mathbf{v}_{i})),

it follows from (
4.18
), (
4.19
), and Lemma
B.1
that, after passing to a subsequence,

(4.20)

\mathbf{v}_{i}\to\mathbf{v}\in\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}\cap\bar{B}_{1}\setminus B_{\theta}(V_{u}).

We may then invoke Corollaries
3.26
and
3.34
to find that

(4.21)

G_{\mathcal{C}}(w;\mathbf{v})<1\text{ for all }w\in\mathcal{J}_{u}\setminus\{0\}.

Let
\iota_{p_{i}\to p_{i}^{+}}
the isometry from
(T_{p_{i}}M,g_{p_{i}})
to
(T_{p_{i}^{+}}M,g_{p_{i}^{+}})
obtained by parallel transport along the minimizing geodesic from
p_{i}
to
p_{i}^{+}
, and
\iota_{p_{i}^{+}}:=\iota_{p_{i}\to p_{i}^{+}}\circ\iota_{p_{i}}
. Define

\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+}:=\iota_{p_{i}\to p_{i}^{+}}(\mathcal{C}_{i})=\iota_{p_{i}^{+}}(\mathcal{C})\subset T_{p_{i}^{+}}M.

Note that
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+}
is a hypercone centered at
\mathbf{0}\in T_{p_{i}^{+}}M
and (
4.19
) plus Lemma
E.2
imply

(3”)

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+}}(\Sigma_{i}^{+};p_{i}^{+},2^{-L_{2,i}})\to 0
.

Proceeding exactly as above, with
\Sigma_{i}^{+}
,
p_{i}^{+}
in place of
\Sigma_{i}
,
p_{i}
, and (3”), (4”), (5”) in place of (3’), (4’), (5’), we obtain a Jacobi field
u^{+}\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{1}(\mathcal{C})\setminus\{0\}
by Proposition
4.5
and Corollary
3.26
. We introduce similar notation to before,

h_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}\circ\Pi:=\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}^{+}_{i}}\circ\iota_{p_{i}^{+}}=\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}},

where
\Pi
is unchanged, and the corresponding domains are
U^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}\to\mathcal{C}
as
i\to\infty
. By construction,

(4.22)

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}^{+}_{i}}(\Sigma^{+}_{i};p^{+}_{i},\tau_{i})^{-1}h_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}\to u^{+}\text{ in }C^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathcal{C}),

by passing to subsequences (not relabeled) as appropriate.

Next, orthogonally decompose

\mathbf{v}_{i}=:\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\parallel}+\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\perp}\in\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}\oplus(\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C})^{\perp}

and set

(4.23)

\lambda_{i}:=\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})+\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+}}(\Sigma_{i}^{+};p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i})+|\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\perp}|>0.

Note that
\lambda_{i}\to 0
by (3’), (3”), and (
4.20
). After passing to a subsequence, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})

\displaystyle\to c\in[0,1],

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+}}(\Sigma_{i}^{+};p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i})

\displaystyle\to c^{+}\in[0,1],

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\perp}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{z}\in(\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C})^{\perp}\cap\bar{B}_{1},

with

c+c^{+}+|\mathbf{z}|=1

by construction. The definitions above together with (
4.17
) and (
4.22
) imply

(4.24)

\lambda_{i}^{-1}h^{\Sigma_{i}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\to cu\text{ in }C^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathcal{C}),

(4.25)

\lambda_{i}^{-1}h^{\Sigma^{+}_{i}}_{p^{+}_{i},\tau_{i}}\to c^{+}u^{+}\text{ in }C^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathcal{C}),

Note that the left hand sides are centered at different points, so the fact that
\Sigma_{i}
,
\Sigma^{+}_{i}
do not cross does
not
imply that
c^{+}u^{+}-cu
does not change sign. We in fact have to consider the new height map

h^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}

for
\iota_{p_{i}}^{-1}((\Sigma_{i}^{+})_{p_{i},\tau_{i}})
over subsets
U^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\to\mathcal{C}
; this is well-defined by (
4.19
). We do then, in fact, after possibly swapping orientations get a quantity that doesn’t change sign,

(4.26)

h^{\Sigma^{+}_{i}}_{p_{i},\tau}-h^{\Sigma_{i}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\geq 0

on the subset
U_{i}=U^{\Sigma_{i}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\cap U^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\to\mathcal{C}
as
i\to\infty
. Note that (
4.24
) tells us how to take limits on
\lambda_{i}^{-1}
times the second term of (
4.26
), but (
4.25
) falls short of telling us the same for the first term due to the change in basepoint. We claim that:

(4.27)

\lambda_{i}^{-1}h^{\Sigma^{+}_{i}}_{p_{i},\tau}\to c^{+}u^{+}(\cdot-\mathbf{v})+\langle\mathbf{z},\nu_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle\text{ in }C^{0}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathcal{C})\,.

We briefly postpone the proof and show how (
4.27
) implies the result. Together with (
4.24
) and (
4.26
) it follows that

c^{+}u^{+}(\cdot-\mathbf{v})+\langle\mathbf{z},\nu_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle\geq cu\,.

Note that both sides are elements of
\operatorname{Jac}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
. Thus, there’s
w\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{\textnormal{pos}}(\mathcal{C})
so that

c^{+}u^{+}(\cdot-\mathbf{v})+\langle\mathbf{z},\nu_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle=cu+w\,.

If
c^{+}=0
, then
c+|\mathbf{z}|=1
, so
w
is the sum of a
0
- and
1
-homogeneous Jacobi field, at least one of which is non-zero, a contradiction to Proposition
3.19
and Lemma
3.21
. Thus,
c^{+}\neq 0
, so we can rearrange our expression to

u^{+}(\cdot-\mathbf{v})=(c^{+})^{-1}(cu-\langle\mathbf{z},\nu_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle+w)\in\mathcal{J}_{u}\setminus\{0\}.

On the other hand,
u^{+}\in\operatorname{Jac}^{*}_{1}(\mathcal{C})
by construction, which contradicts (
4.21
) and completes the proof of the theorem.

It remains to prove (
4.27
). We will do so by a sequence of coordinate change transformations. To that end, let

\mathcal{T}_{p_{i}\to p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}=(\eta_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}\circ\iota_{p_{i}^{+}})^{-1}\circ(\eta_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\circ\iota_{p_{i}})

be the change of basepoint map from
p_{i}
to
p_{i}^{+}
in scale
\tau_{i}
from (
E.7
) in the appendix, pulled back to the corresponding
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
’s. It follows from Lemmas
E.1
and
E.2
that

R^{1}_{i}(\cdot):=\mathcal{T}_{p_{i}\to p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}(\cdot)-(\cdot-\mathbf{v}_{i})

satisfies, for all compact
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,

(4.28)

\|R^{1}_{i}\|_{C^{0}(K)}\leq C_{K}\tau_{i}^{2}.

On the other hand, Lemma
4.4
gives
C>0
so that

\tau_{i}^{2}\leq C\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(\Sigma_{i};p_{i},\tau_{i})^{2},

(or the same with
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{+}
,
\Sigma_{i}^{+}
,
p_{i}^{+}
) so in particular (
4.28
) implies

(4.29)

\|R^{1}_{i}\|_{C^{0}(K)}\leq C_{K}\lambda_{i}^{2},

where
\lambda_{i}
is as in (
4.23
).

Next, set:

R^{2}_{i}(\cdot):=\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\cdot)-\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\mathcal{T}_{p_{i}\to p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}(\cdot)+\mathbf{v}_{i}\right].

Since
\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}
is
1
-Lipschitz, (
4.29
) implies, for all compact
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,

(4.30)

\|R^{2}_{i}\|_{C^{0}(K)}\leq C_{K}\lambda_{i}^{2}.

It will be convenient to introduce some further notation. Namely, let

\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}:U^{\Sigma_{i}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\to\iota_{p_{i}}^{-1}((\Sigma_{i})_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}),

\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}:U^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}\to\iota_{p_{i}^{+}}^{-1}((\Sigma^{+}_{i})_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}),

\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}:U^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\to\iota_{p_{i}}^{-1}((\Sigma^{+}_{i})_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}),

be the graph maps corresponding to the height functions we have (which are inverted by
\Pi
, on each corresponding domain). Note that

\displaystyle h^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}(\cdot)\right]

\displaystyle=\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\left(\mathcal{T}_{p_{i}\to p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}\circ\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)(\cdot)+\mathbf{v}_{i}\right]+\left(R^{2}_{i}\circ\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)(\cdot)

(4.31)

\displaystyle=\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\left(\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}\circ\Pi\circ\mathcal{T}_{p_{i}\to p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}\circ\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)(\cdot)+\mathbf{v}_{i}\right]+\left(R^{2}_{i}\circ\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)(\cdot),

as long as

(4.32)

\left(\Pi\circ\mathcal{T}_{p_{i}\to p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}\circ\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)(\cdot)\in U^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}.

Choose a new slower exhaustion
U_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\to\mathcal{C}
such that
U_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\subset U^{\Sigma_{i}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}
and (
4.32
) holds over
U_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}
. Moreover, denote

\Psi_{i}=\left(\Pi\circ\mathcal{T}_{p_{i}\to p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}\circ\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\right):U_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\to U^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}},

and

\tilde{R}^{2}_{i}:=\left(R^{2}_{i}\circ\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\right):U_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\to\mathbb{R}.

Note that (
4.20
) implies that

(4.33)

\Psi_{i}(\cdot)\to(\cdot)-\mathbf{v}\text{ in }C^{0}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathcal{C}),

while (
4.30
) implies that, for all fixed compact
L\subset\mathcal{C}
, and
i\geq i_{1}(L)
,

(4.34)

\|\tilde{R}^{2}_{i}\|_{C^{0}(L)}\leq C_{L}\lambda_{i}^{2}.

Using our definitions for
\Psi_{i}
and
\tilde{R}^{2}_{i}
, we can rewrite (
4.31
) as follows:

\displaystyle h^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}(\Psi_{i}(\cdot))+\mathbf{v}_{i}\right]+\tilde{R}^{2}_{i}(\cdot)

(4.35)

\displaystyle=\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}(\Psi_{i}(\cdot))+\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\perp}\right]+\tilde{R}^{2}_{i}(\cdot),

where, in the second step, we used the translation invariance of
\mathcal{C}
along
\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\parallel}\in\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}
.

Finally, denote over
U^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}
:

R^{3}_{i}(\cdot):=\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}(\cdot)+\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\perp}\right]-\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}(\cdot)\right]-\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\nu_{\mathcal{C}}(\cdot)\rangle.

It follows from (
4.19
), Allard’s theorem
[
All72
]
, and Lemma
G.1
that, for all compact
L\subset\mathcal{C}
and all
i\geq i_{2}(L)
,

(4.36)

\|R^{3}_{i}\|_{C^{0}(L)}\leq\epsilon_{L,i}|\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\perp}|\leq\epsilon_{L,i}\lambda_{i},

where
\lambda_{i}
is as in (
4.23
) again and, for
L
held fixed, one has

(4.37)

\epsilon_{L,i}\to 0\text{ as }i\to\infty.

Writing

\tilde{R}^{3}_{i}:=R^{3}_{i}\circ\Psi_{i}:U_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}\to\mathbb{R},

note that (
4.33
) and (
4.36
) imply that

(4.38)

\|R^{3}_{i}\|_{C^{0}(L)}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}_{L,i}\lambda_{i},

for a possibly different

(4.39)

\epsilon^{\prime}_{L,i}\to 0\text{ as }i\to\infty.

In summary, (
4.5
) can be once more rewritten as:

\displaystyle h^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i},\tau_{i}}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\Phi^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}(\Psi_{i}(\cdot))\right]+\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\nu_{\mathcal{C}}(\Psi_{i}(\cdot))\rangle+\tilde{R}^{2}_{i}(\cdot)+\tilde{R}^{3}_{i}(\cdot)

(4.40)

\displaystyle=h^{\Sigma_{i}^{+}}_{p_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}}(\Psi_{i}(\cdot))+\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\nu_{\mathcal{C}}(\Psi_{i}(\cdot))\rangle+\tilde{R}^{2}_{i}(\cdot)+\tilde{R}^{3}_{i}(\cdot).

Now, the desired (
4.27
) follows from (
4.40
) together with (
4.25
), (
4.33
), (
4.34
), (
4.38
), and (
4.39
). This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

5.
Spectral quantity
\alpha(\mathcal{C})
for general minimizing hypercones

5.1.
Spectral quantity
\alpha(\mathcal{C})

Our sheet separation analysis relies on Simon’s partial Harnack theory for positive Jacobi fields (cf.
[
Wan24
, Appendix A]
). For minimizing hypercones
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, a spectral quantity that we will call
\alpha(\mathcal{C})
controls the separation of disjoint minimizers that are sufficiently well modeled on
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be any minimizing hypercone. Denote by
\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{C}\cap\mathbb{S}^{n}
the link of the regular part of
\mathcal{C}
. We will be interested in the preliminary spectral quantity

(5.1)

\mu(\mathcal{C}):=\inf\left\{\int_{\mathcal{L}}|\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}\varphi|^{2}-|A_{\mathcal{L}}|^{2}\varphi^{2}:\varphi\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathcal{L}),\;\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{L})}=1\right\},

where
\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}
denotes the covariant derivative on
\mathcal{L}
and
A_{\mathcal{L}}
denotes the second fundamental form of
\mathcal{L}
as a submanifold of
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.

The roots of the equation
x^{2}-(n-2)x-\mu(\mathcal{C})=0
are necessarily real when
\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
(the argument in
[
CHS84
, Section 4]
applies), and we denote the smaller as:

(5.2)

\alpha(\mathcal{C}):=\frac{n-2}{2}-\sqrt{\frac{(n-2)^{2}}{4}+\mu(\mathcal{C})}.

Remark 5.2
.

It is worth comparing this spectral quantity with those discussed in Section
3.2
in case
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
is a regular hypercone. In the language of that section:

\alpha(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})=-\gamma_{1}.

A key property of
\alpha(\cdot)
is that it, among nonflat minimizing hypercones, it is precisely minimized by quadratic hypercones. Indeed:

Lemma 5.3
.

Define:

(5.3)

\alpha_{n}:=\frac{n-2}{2}-\sqrt{\frac{(n-2)^{2}}{4}-(n-1)}.

Then:

(i)

The
\alpha_{n}
form a decreasing sequence with
2=\alpha_{7}>\alpha_{8}>\ldots>1
.
19
19

19

See also
[
CMS23a
, Remark 3.9]
for approximations of the first few values. The reference uses the notation
\kappa_{n}
in place of
\alpha_{n}
.

(ii)

For every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
,
\alpha(\mathcal{C})\geq\alpha_{n}
with equality if and only if
\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd}}
.

Proof.

(i) This is an elementary computation.

(ii) This was shown by J. Zhu
[
Zhu18
]
; cf.
[
Sim68
,
Law69
,
CdCK70
,
Per02
,
Wu93
]
.
∎

The non-rigid portion of Lemma
5.3
improves to:

Lemma 5.4
.

Denote
20
20

20

It is an open question whether
\mathscr{C}_{7}=\mathscr{C}_{7}^{\textnormal{qd}}
; if it is, then we may interpret
\Delta^{\operatorname{non-qd}}_{7}=\infty
using the convention
\inf\emptyset=\infty
.

(5.4)

\Delta^{\operatorname{non-qd}}_{n}=\inf\{\alpha(\mathcal{C}):\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}\setminus\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd}}\}-\alpha_{n}.

Then:

(i)

\Delta^{\operatorname{non-qd}}_{n}>0
.

(ii)

For every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}\setminus\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd}}
,
\alpha(\mathcal{C})\geq\alpha_{n}+\Delta^{\operatorname{non-qd}}_{n}
.

Proof.

This was shown in
[
Wan24
, Appendix A]
.
∎

It also follows that
\alpha(\cdot)
behaves well under cone-splitting:

Proposition 5.5
.

If
\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}\cap\operatorname{Rot}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k})
,
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\in\mathscr{C}_{n-k}
, then

\alpha(\mathcal{C})=\alpha(\mathcal{C}_{\circ}).

A proof is given below using a localized version
\alpha(\mathcal{C};\sigma)
of
\alpha(\mathcal{C})
. For hypercones that are not regular, such as our intended cylindrical hypercones, it will help to work with a localization of
\alpha(\mathcal{C})
to the controlled-regularity subsets
\operatorname{reg}_{>\sigma}\mathcal{C}
,
\sigma>0
.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be any minimizing hypercone, with
\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{C}\cap\mathbb{S}^{n}
the link of the regular part. Consider, for
\sigma>0
, the open subsets

\mathcal{L}_{>\sigma}:=\operatorname{reg}_{>\sigma}\mathcal{C}\cap\mathbb{S}^{n}\subset\mathcal{L}.

Then, one defines, in analogy with (
5.1
), (
5.2
),

(5.5)

\mu(\mathcal{C};\sigma):=\inf\left\{\int_{\mathcal{L}}|\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}\varphi|^{2}-|A_{\mathcal{L}}|^{2}\varphi^{2}:\varphi\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathcal{L}_{>\sigma}),\;\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{L})}=1\right\},

(5.6)

\alpha(\mathcal{C};\sigma):=\frac{n-2}{2}-\sqrt{\frac{(n-2)^{2}}{4}+\mu(\mathcal{C};\sigma)}.

More generally, for every open subset
\Omega\subset\mathcal{L}
, define

(5.7)

\mu(\mathcal{C};\Omega):=\inf\left\{\int_{\Omega}|\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}\varphi|^{2}-|A_{\mathcal{L}}|^{2}\varphi^{2}:\varphi\in C^{1}_{c}(\Omega),\;\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{L})}=1\right\},

(5.8)

\alpha(\mathcal{C};\Omega):=\frac{n-2}{2}-\sqrt{\frac{(n-2)^{2}}{4}+\mu(\mathcal{C};\Omega)}.

Clearly,
\mu(\mathcal{C};\Omega)
is monotone non-increasing in
\Omega
,
\alpha(\mathcal{C};\Omega)
is monotone non-decreasing in
\Omega
, and if
Z\subset\mathcal{L}
is a submanifold of codimension
>2
,
\Omega_{1}\subset\Omega_{2}\subset\dots
is an exhaustion of
\mathcal{L}\setminus Z
, then by a standard cut-off argument we have

(5.9)

\lim_{j\to\infty}\alpha(\mathcal{C};\Omega_{j})=\alpha(\mathcal{C};\mathcal{C}\setminus Z)=\alpha(\mathcal{C})\,.

In particular,

(5.10)

\lim_{\sigma\to 0}\alpha(\mathcal{C};\sigma)=\alpha(\mathcal{C}).

Moreover, if
\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}
within
\mathscr{C}_{n}
,
\sigma_{i}\to\sigma
within
(0,\infty)
, and
t\in(0,1)
, then

(5.11)

\alpha(\mathcal{C};\sigma)\leq\liminf_{i}\alpha(\mathcal{C}_{i};\sigma_{i})\leq\limsup_{i}\alpha(\mathcal{C}_{i};\sigma_{i})\leq\alpha(\mathcal{C};t\sigma)

One can verify, using
\alpha(\mathcal{C};\sigma)
, that Proposition
5.5
from the introduction holds:

Proof of Proposition
5.5
.

Without loss of generality, we rotate
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
so that

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}.

Denote
n_{\circ}=n-k
, so that
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1}
, and write
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
for the links of
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
.

Fix an arbitrary domain
\Omega_{\circ}\Subset\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
with smooth boundary. Denote

\Omega:=\mathbb{S}^{n}\cap(\operatorname{Cone}_{\mathbf{0}}(\Omega_{\circ})\times\mathbb{R}^{k})\subset\mathcal{L}.

The primary goal is to show that

(5.12)

\alpha(\mathcal{C};\Omega)=\alpha(\mathcal{C}_{\circ};\Omega_{\circ})\,.

It suffices to show this for
\Omega_{\circ}\neq\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
.
Note that combining (
5.12
) and (
5.9
) proves Proposition
5.5
.

To prove (
5.12
) for
\Omega_{\circ}\neq\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
, let
\mu_{\circ}:=\mu(\mathcal{C}_{\circ};\Omega_{\circ})
be the principal Dirichlet eigenvalue for
-\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}-|A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}|^{2}
on
\Omega_{\circ}
,
\varphi_{\circ}:\overline{\Omega_{\circ}}\to[0,\infty)
be a corresponding principal Dirichlet eigenfunction, i.e.,

(5.13)

(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}+|A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}|^{2})\varphi_{\circ}=-\mu_{\circ}\varphi_{\circ}\text{ in }\Omega_{\circ},\quad\varphi_{\circ}=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega_{\circ}.

Note that by strong maximum principle,
\varphi_{\circ}>0
on
\Omega_{\circ}
. Also denote for simplicity
\alpha_{\circ}:=\alpha(\mathcal{C}_{\circ};\Omega_{\circ})
. At this point, the assumption
\Omega_{\circ}\neq\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
enters to give
\alpha_{\circ}<\alpha(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})\leq(n_{\circ}-2)/2
. Let

\psi:\operatorname{Cone}_{\mathbf{0}}(\Omega_{\circ})\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\to[0,+\infty)\,,\quad\psi:=r^{-\alpha_{\circ}}\varphi_{\circ}

be the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-invariant
(-\alpha_{\circ})
-homogeneous extension of
\varphi_{\circ}
. Then

\displaystyle(\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}+|A_{\mathcal{C}}|^{2})\psi

\displaystyle=(\Delta_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}+|A_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}|^{2})(r^{-\alpha_{\circ}}\varphi_{\circ})

\displaystyle=(r^{-2}\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}+(n_{\circ}-1)r^{-1}\partial_{r}+\partial_{r}^{2}+r^{-2}|A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}|^{2})(r^{-\alpha_{\circ}}\varphi_{\circ})

\displaystyle=r^{-2}(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}+|A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}|^{2})(r^{-\alpha_{\circ}}\varphi_{\circ})+r^{-2}(\alpha_{\circ}^{2}-(n_{\circ}-2)\alpha_{\circ})(r^{-\alpha_{\circ}}\varphi_{\circ})

\displaystyle=r^{-2}(-\mu_{\circ})(r^{-\alpha_{\circ}}\varphi_{\circ})+r^{-2}(\alpha_{\circ}^{2}-(n_{\circ}-2)\alpha_{\circ})(r^{-\alpha_{\circ}}\varphi_{\circ})

\displaystyle=0,

where we used (
5.13
), (
5.8
) in the last two steps. Thus,
\psi
is a positive Jacobi field with Dirichlet boundary conditions on
\operatorname{Cone}_{\mathbf{0}}(\Omega_{\circ})\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\subset\mathcal{C}
. Note, also, that
\psi
being
(-\alpha_{\circ})
-homogeneous implies,

(5.14)

\partial_{\rho}\psi=-\alpha_{\circ}\rho^{-1}\psi,

where
\rho
denotes the radial distance function from the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

Following the same computation as before but backwards, and in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
rather than
\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1}
, we deduce from the Jacobi field nature of
\psi
that:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}+|A_{\mathcal{C}}|^{2})\psi

\displaystyle=\left(\rho^{-2}\Delta_{\mathcal{L}}+(n-1)\rho^{-1}\partial_{\rho}+\partial_{\rho}^{2}+\rho^{-2}|A_{\mathcal{L}}|^{2}\right)\psi

\displaystyle=\rho^{-2}(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}}+|A_{\mathcal{L}}|^{2}+(\alpha_{\circ}^{2}-(n-2)\alpha_{\circ}))\psi,

where we used (
5.14
) in the last step. This implies that
\varphi:=\psi|_{\Omega}
is a positive solution to

(5.15)

-(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}}+|A_{\mathcal{L}}|^{2})\varphi=(\alpha_{\circ}^{2}-(n-2)\alpha_{\circ}))\varphi\quad\text{ on }\Omega\,.

Hence, for every
\xi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
, let
\zeta:=\varphi^{-1}\xi
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}\xi|^{2}-|A_{\mathcal{L}}|^{2}\xi^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}(\varphi\zeta)|^{2}-|A_{\mathcal{L}}|^{2}(\varphi\zeta)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}\zeta|^{2}\varphi^{2}+|\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}\varphi|^{2}\zeta^{2}+\varphi\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}(\zeta^{2})-|A_{\mathcal{L}}|^{2}(\varphi\zeta)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}\zeta|^{2}\varphi^{2}-\zeta^{2}\varphi\cdot(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}}+|A_{\mathcal{L}}|^{2})\varphi

\displaystyle\geq(\alpha_{\circ}^{2}-(n-2)\alpha_{\circ})\|\xi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\,.

where we use (
5.15
) in the last inequality. This means

\mu(\mathcal{C};\Omega)\geq\alpha_{\circ}^{2}-(n-2)\alpha_{\circ}\,.

Since by (
5.8
),
\mu(\mathcal{C};\Omega)=\alpha(\mathcal{C};\Omega)^{2}-(n-2)\alpha(\mathcal{C};\Omega)
and
\alpha(\mathcal{C};\Omega)\leq(n-2)/2
,
\alpha_{\circ}<(n_{\circ}-2)/2<(n-2)/2
, this proves “
\leq
” of (
5.12
).

To prove the reverse inequality, we again use (
5.15
) to choose
\varphi
as a test function after some cut-off near
\operatorname{sing}\mathcal{L}=\bar{\mathcal{L}}\setminus\mathcal{L}
. Recall that by the definition of
\Omega
, we have

\overline{\Omega}\cap\operatorname{sing}\mathcal{L}=\mathbb{S}^{n}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{\mathbf{0}\})\,.

For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, let
\eta_{\varepsilon}:=\eta(r/\varepsilon)
, viewed as a smooth function on
\mathcal{L}
, where
\eta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};[0,1])
such that
\eta=0
on
(-\infty,1/2]
,
\eta=1
on
[1,+\infty)
and
|\eta^{\prime}|\leq 10
.
Then
\eta_{\varepsilon}\varphi
is a Lipschitz function on
\mathcal{L}
supported away from
\operatorname{sing}\mathcal{L}
and vanishes outside
\Omega
, therefore by the same calculation as above,

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{C};\Omega)\int_{\Omega}(\eta_{\varepsilon}\varphi)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}|\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}(\eta_{\varepsilon}\varphi)|^{2}-|A_{\mathcal{L}}|^{2}(\eta_{\varepsilon}\varphi)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}\eta_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\varphi^{2}-\eta_{\varepsilon}^{2}\varphi\cdot(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}}+|A_{\mathcal{L}}|^{2})\varphi

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}\eta_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\varphi^{2}+(\alpha_{\circ}-(n-2)\alpha_{\circ})\eta_{\varepsilon}^{2}\varphi^{2}\,.

Recall that
\varphi=r^{-\alpha_{\circ}}\varphi_{\circ}
, where
\alpha_{\circ}<(n_{\circ}-2)/2
, we then have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(1-\eta_{\varepsilon}^{2})\varphi^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\int_{\Omega\cap\{2^{-j}\varepsilon\leq r\leq 2^{-j+1}\varepsilon\}}\|\varphi_{\circ}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\cdot r^{-2\alpha_{\circ}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,\varphi_{\circ})\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(2^{-j}\varepsilon)^{-2\alpha_{\circ}}\operatorname{Area}(\mathcal{L}\cap\{r<2^{-j+1}\varepsilon\}).

Also,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}\eta_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\varphi^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega\cap\{\varepsilon/2\leq r\leq\varepsilon\}}\varepsilon^{-2}|\eta^{\prime}|^{2}\|\varphi_{\circ}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\cdot r^{-2\alpha_{\circ}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,\varphi_{\circ})\varepsilon^{-2-2\alpha_{\circ}}\operatorname{Area}(\mathcal{L}\cap\{r<\varepsilon\}).

By the monotonicity formula and a Vitali covering argument,

\|\mathcal{L}\|(\{r<\varepsilon\})\leq C(\mathcal{L})\varepsilon^{n_{\circ}}\,.

Combining these estimates and sending
\varepsilon\to 0
, we derive,

\mu(\mathcal{C};\Omega)\leq\alpha_{\circ}^{2}-(n-2)\alpha_{\circ}\,.

This proves “
\geq
” of (
5.12
).
∎

5.2.
Choosing good
\alpha(\mathcal{C};\sigma)
localizations

Let
d_{\mathscr{C}_{n}}
be a metric on
\mathscr{C}_{n}
(see (
2.1
)) compatible with its standard topology, e.g., constructed from the flat norm. In analogy with the notation
B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{C})\subset\mathscr{C}_{n}
for open
\epsilon
-balls centered at
\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
, we will also denote for any subset
S\subset\mathscr{C}_{n}

B_{\epsilon}(S):=\bigcup_{\mathcal{C}\in S}B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{C})

to be the standard
\epsilon
-neighborhood in
\mathscr{C}_{n}
with respect to
d_{\mathscr{C}_{n}}
.

It will also be convenient to decompose
\mathscr{C}_{n}
in terms of spine dimensions. Specifically:

\mathscr{C}_{n}^{k\textnormal{-cyl}}:=\{\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}:\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}\geq k\}

for
k=0,1,\ldots,n-7
. A standard consequence of the monotonicity formula is that:

(5.16)

\mathscr{C}_{n}^{k\textnormal{-cyl}}\subset\mathscr{C}_{n}\text{ is compact}.

Recall also the definition of
\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd-cyl}}
from (
2.3
).

Proposition 5.7
.

The following are all true:

(i)

Fix
\xi>0
. For all sufficiently small
\epsilon,\sigma>0
depending on
n,\xi
,

\mathcal{C}\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{k\textnormal{-cyl}})\implies\alpha(\mathcal{C};\sigma)>\alpha_{n-k}-\xi.

(ii)

Fix
\xi>0
. For all
\epsilon>0
, and all sufficiently small
\sigma>0
depending on
n,\xi,\epsilon
,

\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{k\textnormal{-cyl}}\setminus B_{\epsilon}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd-cyl}})\implies\alpha(\mathcal{C};\sigma)>\alpha_{n-k}+\Delta_{n-k}^{\textnormal{non-qd}}-\xi.

(iii)

For all sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
depending on
n
,

\mathcal{C}\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd-cyl}})\setminus\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd-cyl}}\implies\mathcal{C}\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{k^{\prime}\textnormal{-cyl}})\text{ for }k^{\prime}=\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}+1.

Proof.

(i) If not, there would exist
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n-7\}
,
\sigma_{i}\to 0
,
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
,
\mathcal{C}_{i}\in B_{\epsilon_{i}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{k\textnormal{-cyl}})
such that

(5.17)

\alpha(\mathcal{C}_{i};\sigma_{i})\leq\alpha_{n-k}-\xi.

Pass to a subsequence so that
\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}
. Then
\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{k\textnormal{-cyl}}
by
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
and (
5.16
), so

(5.18)

\alpha(\mathcal{C})\geq\alpha_{n-k}

by Proposition
5.5
. On the other hand, for each fixed
\sigma\in(0,1)
, we have from (
5.17
) together with (
5.11
) and
\sigma_{i}\to 0
that

\alpha(\mathcal{C};\sigma)\leq\lim_{i}\alpha(\mathcal{C}_{i};\sigma_{i})\leq\alpha_{n-k}-\xi.

Now using (
5.10
) to send
\sigma\to 0
in the inequality above, we contradict (
5.18
).

(ii) If not, there would exist
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n-7\}
,
\sigma_{i}\to 0
,
\mathcal{C}_{i}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{k\textnormal{-cyl}}\setminus B_{\epsilon}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd-cyl}})
such that

(5.19)

\alpha(\mathcal{C}_{i};\sigma_{i})\leq\alpha_{n-k}+\Delta_{n-k}^{\textnormal{non-qd}}-\xi.

Pass to a subsequence so that
\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}
. Then
\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{k\textnormal{-cyl}}\setminus\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd-cyl}}
by (
5.16
). It follows from Proposition
5.5
, and Lemma
5.4
that

(5.20)

\alpha(\mathcal{C})\geq\alpha_{n-k}+\Delta_{n-k}^{\textnormal{non-qd}}.

Working as in (i), one derives a contradiction from using (
5.19
) against (
5.20
).

(iii) If not, there would exist
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n-7\}
,
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
,
\mathcal{C}_{i}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}\setminus\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd-cyl}}
with
\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}_{i}=k
, and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd-cyl}}
with
d_{\mathscr{C}_{n}}(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime})<\epsilon_{i}
, so that

(5.21)

\mathcal{C}_{i}\not\in B_{\epsilon_{i}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{(k+1)\textnormal{-cyl}}).

As before,
\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}_{i}=k
plus (
5.16
) and passing to a subsequence implies that

(5.22)

\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{k\textnormal{-cyl}}.

In particular, (
5.21
) plus
d_{\mathscr{C}_{n}}(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime})<\epsilon_{i}
guarantee that

(5.23)

\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}\leq k.

Let us upgrade (
5.23
) to a strict inequality for large
i
. Indeed, otherwise,

(5.24)

\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Rot}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\circ,i}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}),\;\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\circ,i}\in\mathscr{C}_{n-k}^{\textnormal{qd}}.

Likewise,
\mathcal{C}_{i}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}\setminus\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd-cyl}}
plus
\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}_{i}=k
also imply

(5.25)

\mathcal{C}_{i}\in\operatorname{Rot}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ,i}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}),\;\mathcal{C}_{\circ,i}\in\mathscr{C}_{n-k}\setminus\mathscr{C}_{n-k}^{\textnormal{qd}}.

Now (
5.24
) and (
5.25
) plus
d_{\mathscr{C}_{n}}(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime})<\epsilon_{i}\to 0
imply that after possibly rotating
\mathcal{C}_{\circ,i}^{\prime}
to
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\circ,i}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{n-k}^{\textnormal{qd}}
, we have

d_{\mathscr{C}_{n}}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ,i},\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\circ,i})\to 0

even though
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\circ,i}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd}}\not\ni\mathcal{C}_{\circ,i}
. This contradicts the isolatedness of quadratic hypercones (Corollary
A.5
) as
i\to\infty
. This completes the proof that (
5.23
) is a strict inequality, as desired:

(5.26)

\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}\leq k-1.

Now note that the space
\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd-cyl}}
is discrete modulo rotations. By passing to a subsequence we have that spine dimensions get preserved in (
5.26
), so

(5.27)

\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd-cyl}},\;\dim\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\leq k-1.

On the other hand,
d_{\mathscr{C}_{n}}(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime})<\epsilon_{i}\to 0
implies that
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, so (
5.22
) and (
5.27
) are in contradiction. This completes the proof.
∎

6.
Covering tree structure

Standard measure theory manipulations can be used to understand the implications of Hölder continuity of a map to the dimension of its image and the dimensions of its level sets. See, e.g.,
[
FROS20
, Proposition 7.7]
for a general statement. In our case, the non-uniqueness of tangent cones requires a slightly coarser approach, where dimensions and Hölder continuities are scale-dependent. We will use the following result. In our applications of it to the proof of Theorem
1.4
and Corollary
1.6
, we will take
\mathcal{S}\subset\operatorname{sing}\mathscr{F}
,
\mathfrak{T}
to be the restriction to
\mathcal{S}
of our timestamp map
\mathfrak{T}
, and
\mathfrak{C}_{j}
to be covers of
\mathcal{S}
with subsets of balls of radius
a\cdot\theta^{j}
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold. Take some subset
\mathcal{S}\subset(M,g)
, a map
\mathfrak{T}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}
, and some constants
\theta\in(0,1)
,
a>0
, and
C_{1},C_{2}>0
.

Assume we can construct a countable family of coverings
\mathfrak{C}_{j}
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, of
\mathcal{S}
(i.e., for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have
\cup_{B\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}}B\supset\mathcal{S}
) with the following structural properties:

(1)

For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\mathfrak{C}_{j}
is a finite collection of sets
B\subset M
with

\operatorname{diam}_{g}B\leq 2a\cdot\theta^{j}.

(2)

For each
B_{j+1}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j+1}
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, there exists a “parent” element

\mathfrak{p}(B_{j+1})\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}.

(3)

For each
B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, there exists a “coarse dimension”

\mathfrak{D}(B_{j})\geq 0

such that

\#\mathfrak{p}^{-1}(B_{j})\leq C_{1}\cdot\theta^{-\mathfrak{D}(B_{j})}.

(4)

For each
B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, there exists a “coarse Hölder exponent”

\mathfrak{h}(B_{j})\geq 0

such that

\operatorname{diam}\mathfrak{T}(B_{j}\cap\mathcal{S})\leq C_{2}\cdot C_{1}^{j}\cdot\theta^{\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j}))},

where
\mathfrak{p}^{i}=\mathfrak{p}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{p}
taken
i
times.

Let
d>0
,
\xi>0
. There exists
\theta_{0}>0
depending on
\xi
and
C_{1}
(but not on
a
,
d
, or
C_{2}
) such that being able to carry out the setup above with
\theta\leq\theta_{0}
guarantees:

(i)

If
d\geq(\xi+\mathfrak{D}(B))/\mathfrak{h}(B)
for all
B\in\cup_{j}\mathfrak{C}_{j}
, then
\dim\mathfrak{T}(\mathcal{S})\leq d
.

(ii)

If
d\geq\xi+\mathfrak{D}(B)-\mathfrak{h}(B)
for all
B\in\cup_{j}\mathfrak{C}_{j}
, then
\dim\mathcal{S}\cap\mathfrak{T}^{-1}(t)\leq d
for a.e.
t
.

Proof.

Recall from
[
Sim83
, §2]
the definition of
\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\infty}
, the
d
-dimensional Hausdorff outer measure computed using countable covers without diameter constraints.

(i) Without loss of generality, we may assume
d\leq 1
and
C_{1}>1
. Fix
j\geq 1
. Then:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\mathfrak{T}(\mathcal{S}))

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\mathfrak{T}(B_{j}\cap\mathcal{S}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}\end{subarray}}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{j}(\#\mathfrak{p}^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j})))}{\prod_{i=1}^{j}(\#\mathfrak{p}^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j})))}\cdot\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\mathfrak{T}(B_{j}\cap\mathcal{S}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\prod_{i=1}^{j}(\#\mathfrak{p}^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j})))}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{j}(\#\mathfrak{p}^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j})))\cdot\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\mathfrak{T}(B_{j}\cap\mathcal{S}))

(6.1)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{j}(\#\mathfrak{p}^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j})))\cdot\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\mathfrak{T}(B_{j}\cap\mathcal{S}))\right]

where
\mathbb{E}_{B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}}[\cdot]
is the expected value among all
B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}
visited by a uniformly random walk starting from
\mathfrak{C}_{0}
, descending to
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
, then
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
, etc. Note that, by assumption (3),

\prod_{i=1}^{j}(\#\mathfrak{p}^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j})))\leq C_{1}^{j}\cdot\theta^{-\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j}))},

and by (4),

\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\mathfrak{T}(B_{j}\cap\mathcal{S}))\leq\omega_{d}\cdot(\operatorname{diam}\mathfrak{T}(B_{j}\cap\mathcal{S}))^{d}\leq\omega_{d}\cdot C_{2}^{d}\cdot C_{1}^{dj}\cdot\theta^{d\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j}))}.

Plugging back into (
6
) and using
d\geq(\xi+\mathfrak{D}(B_{j}))/\mathfrak{h}(B_{j})
,
C_{1}>1
,
d\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\mathfrak{T}(\mathcal{S}))

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{d}\cdot C_{2}^{d}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}}\left[\left(C_{1}^{d+1}\theta^{j^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{j}(-\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j}))+d\cdot\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j})))}\right)^{j}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{d}\cdot C_{2}^{d}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}}\left[\left(C_{1}^{2}\theta^{\xi}\right)^{j}\right].

Taking
\theta\leq\theta_{0}
with
C_{1}^{2}\theta_{0}^{\xi}<1
and sending
j\to\infty
implies
\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\mathfrak{T}(\mathcal{S}))=0
. It follows that
\mathcal{H}^{d}(\mathfrak{T}(\mathcal{S}))=0
by
[
Sim83
, Theorem 3.6]
, so
\dim\mathfrak{T}(\mathcal{S})\leq d
.

(ii) Fix
j\geq 1
. For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, consider the sub-covering

\mathfrak{C}_{j}[t]:=\{B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}:B_{j}\cap\mathcal{S}\cap\mathfrak{T}^{-1}(t)\neq\emptyset\}.

Then, since
\mathfrak{C}_{j}[t]
is a covering of
\mathcal{S}\cap\mathfrak{T}^{-1}(t)
with sets of diameter
\leq 2a\theta^{j}
, we can estimate

\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}\cap\mathfrak{T}^{-1}(t))\leq\omega_{d}\cdot(a\theta^{j})^{d}\cdot(\#\mathfrak{C}_{j}[t]).

As a result, we have the following elementary upper Lebesgue integral estimate (recall that these are subadditive, not additive):

\displaystyle\overline{\int_{\mathbb{R}}}\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}\cap\mathfrak{T}^{-1}(t))\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{d}\cdot(a\theta^{j})^{d}\cdot\overline{\int_{\mathbb{R}}}(\#\mathfrak{C}_{j}[t])\,dt

\displaystyle=\omega_{d}\cdot(a\theta^{j})^{d}\cdot\overline{\int_{\mathbb{R}}}\sum_{B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}}\mathbf{1}_{t\in\mathfrak{T}(B_{j}\cap\mathcal{S})}\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{d}\cdot(a\theta^{j})^{d}\cdot\sum_{B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}}\overline{\int_{\mathbb{R}}}\mathbf{1}_{t\in\mathfrak{T}(B_{j}\cap\mathcal{S})}\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{d}\cdot(a\theta^{j})^{d}\cdot\sum_{B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}}\operatorname{diam}\mathfrak{T}(B_{j}\cap\mathcal{S}).

At this point the proof is quite similar to (i), and we can again rewrite:

(6.2)

\overline{\int_{\mathbb{R}}}\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}\cap\mathfrak{T}^{-1}(t))\,dt\leq\omega_{d}\cdot(a\theta^{j})^{d}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{j}(\#\mathfrak{p}^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j})))\cdot\operatorname{diam}(\mathfrak{T}(B_{j}\cap\mathcal{S}))\right]

As before, by assumption (3),

\prod_{i=1}^{j}(\#\mathfrak{p}^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j})))\leq C_{1}^{j}\cdot\theta^{-\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j}))},

and by (4),

\operatorname{diam}\mathfrak{T}(B_{j}\cap\mathcal{S})\leq C_{2}\cdot C_{1}^{j}\cdot\theta^{\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j}))}.

Plugging back into (
6.2
) and using
\mathfrak{D}(B_{j})\leq\mathfrak{h}(B_{j})+d-\xi
:

\displaystyle\overline{\int_{\mathbb{R}}}\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}\cap\mathfrak{T}^{-1}(t))\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{d}\cdot a^{d}\cdot C_{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}}\left[\left(C_{1}^{2}\theta^{j^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{j}(d-\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j}))+\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{p}^{i}(B_{j})))}\right)^{j}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{d}\cdot a^{d}\cdot C_{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{B_{j}\in\mathfrak{C}_{j}}\left[\left(C_{1}^{2}\theta^{\xi}\right)^{j}\right].

Taking
\theta\leq\theta_{0}
with
C_{1}^{2}\theta_{0}^{\xi}<1
and sending
j\to\infty
implies
\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\mathcal{S}\cap\mathfrak{T}^{-1}(t))=0
for a.e.
t
. Proceeding as in (i),
\dim(\mathcal{S}\cap\mathfrak{T}^{-1}(t))\leq d
for a.e.
t
.
∎

7.
Proof of Theorem
1.4
and Corollary
1.6

7.1.
Setup

Fix
(M,g)
,
\mathscr{F}
, and
\mathfrak{T}
as in the statement of Theorem
1.4
. We note three facts:

(1)

By rescaling
M
and possibly excising closer to the compact subset
\operatorname{sing}\mathscr{F}\subset M
, we may assume that
\breve{M}:=B_{2}(\operatorname{sing}\mathscr{F})\subset M
and
(M,g)
satisfy the injectivity radius and curvature constraints laid out in the beginning of Section
4
.

(2)

By our assumption on
\mathscr{F}
, for each
p\in\operatorname{spt}\mathscr{F}
there is a unique
\Sigma\in\mathscr{F}
such that
p\in\operatorname{spt}\Sigma
. This
\Sigma\in\mathscr{F}
will just be referred to as “corresponding” to
p
.

(3)

By Allard’s theorem
[
All72
]
, if
p\in\operatorname{sing}\mathscr{F}
, then
p\in\operatorname{sing}\Sigma
for its corresponding
\Sigma\in\mathscr{F}
.

We will invoke these facts repeatedly without further comment.

All our constants may a priori depend on
M
,
g
, and
\mathscr{F}
. One may actually lift this dependence entirely by incorporating
M
,
g
,
\mathscr{F}
into the various contradiction arguments provided we hold the injectivity radius and curvature constraints
from Section
4
fixed, as well as the Lipschitz constant of
\mathfrak{T}
. We will not need to do this, so we avoid it.

7.2.
Conical scale structure

We will seek to understand points of
\operatorname{sing}\mathscr{F}
that look sufficiently conical with respect to
\mathbf{d}
at some uniform dyadic scale.

Proposition 7.1
.

Fix
\sigma\in(0,\sigma_{1}(n)]
,
H=H(n,\sigma)
as in Lemma
C.1
, and
\theta\in(0,1)
. There exist
\varepsilon>0
,
L\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
depending on
n,\sigma,\theta
, with the following significance.

Suppose that
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\ell\geq L
, and
\mathcal{S}\subset\operatorname{sing}\mathscr{F}
satisfies:

(1)

For all
p\in\mathcal{S}
, with
p\in\operatorname{sing}\Sigma
for
\Sigma\in\mathscr{F}
, we have

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{p,\ell}}(\Sigma;p,2^{-\ell})<\varepsilon

for some nonflat minimizing hypercone

.

Then, for all
p\in\mathcal{S}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\ell\geq L
, there exists some nonflat minimizing hypercone

so that the following all hold:

(i)

If
V_{p,\ell}:=\operatorname{spine}\check{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\ell}
, then

\mathcal{S}\cap B_{2^{-\ell}}(p)\subset\eta_{p,2^{-\ell}}(B_{\theta}(V_{p,\ell})).

For all

(iii)

For all

, if

with
\Sigma^{\pm}\in\mathscr{F}
, then
21
21

21

We emphasize that the balls in the left and right hand side are centered at different points.

\displaystyle\frac{d_{g}(\operatorname{spt}\Sigma^{-},\operatorname{reg}_{>2^{-\ell}\sigma}\Sigma^{+}\cap\partial B_{2^{-\ell}}(p))}{2^{-\ell}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq H\cdot\theta^{1+\alpha(\check{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\ell};\sigma)}\cdot\frac{d_{g}(\operatorname{spt}\Sigma^{-},\operatorname{reg}_{>2^{-\ell}\theta\sigma}\Sigma^{+}\cap\partial B_{2^{-\ell}\theta}(p^{\prime}))}{2^{-\ell}\theta}.

(iv)

For
p
and
\ell
whose

is a copy of a hypercone in
\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\textnormal{qd-cyl}}
, we may also take their
