:

\dim(\operatorname{sing}\mathscr{F}\cap\mathfrak{T}^{-1}(t))\leq{\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{\textnormal{dom}}}+\Psi(\xi,\delta).

We may thus conclude as before. This completes the proof of Theorem
1.4
.

7.9.
Proof of Corollary
1.6

We use that
\Delta^{\textnormal{non-qd}}_{n-k},\Delta^{\textnormal{qd}}_{n-k}>0
for all
k\leq n-7
without further comment. This follows from Lemma
5.4
and Remark
3.18
, respectively.

(i) Note that
n+1\leq 11
implies
k\leq 3
. We study the fractions in the “
\max
” expression for
\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{\textnormal{img}}
as
0\leq k\leq 3
varies and show they are all
<1
. There are two cases:

•

k\leq 2
. The first fraction has numerator
k\leq 2
, and denominator
>1+\alpha_{n-k}>2
by Lemma
5.3
, so its value is
<1
. The second fraction has numerator
k-1\leq 1
and denominator still
>2
, so this fraction’s value, too, is
<1
.

•

k=3
. Then,
n=10
. The first fraction has numerator
k=3
and denominator
>1+\alpha_{7}=3
by Lemma
5.3
, so its value is
<1
. The second fraction has numerator
k-1=2
and denominator
=3
, so this fraction’s value, too, is
<1
.

Altogether,
\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{\textnormal{img}}<1
.

(ii) Likewise, we show that each term in the “
\max
” expression for
\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{\textnormal{dom}}
, as
0\leq k\leq n-7
varies, is
\leq n-10-\epsilon_{n}
where

\displaystyle\epsilon_{n}

\displaystyle:=\min\Big\{\alpha_{7}+\min\{\Delta^{\textnormal{qd}}_{7},\Delta^{\textnormal{non-qd}}_{7}\}-2,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\min_{k=0,1,\ldots,n-8}\{\alpha_{n-k}+\min\{\Delta^{\textnormal{qd}}_{n-k},\Delta^{\textnormal{non-qd}}_{n-k}\}-1\}\Big\}.

Note that
\epsilon_{n}>0
. There are two cases:

•

k\leq n-8
. The parenthetical term is

1+\alpha_{n-k}+\min\{\Delta^{\textnormal{qd}}_{n-k},\Delta^{\textnormal{non-qd}}_{n-k}\}\geq 2+\epsilon_{n}.

Thus, the full term is
\leq k-(2+\epsilon_{n})\leq n-10-\epsilon_{n}
.

•

k=n-7
. The parenthetical term is

1+\alpha_{7}+\min\{\Delta^{\textnormal{qd}}_{n-k},\Delta^{\textnormal{non-qd}}_{n-k}\}\geq 3+\epsilon_{n}.

Thus, the full term is
\leq k-(3+\epsilon_{n})\leq n-10-\epsilon_{n}
.

This completes the proof.

8.
Proof of Theorem
1.1
(Plateau problem in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
)

In this section we provide the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be as in Theorem
1.1
and
T
be a minimizing integral
n
-current with
\partial T=[\![\Gamma]\!]
. Assume that
T
additionally satisfies:

(1)

T
is uniquely minimizing, and

(2)

T
has multiplicity one on
\operatorname{reg}T
.

There exist
C^{\infty}
-small perturbations
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
for which all minimizing integral
n
-currents
T^{\prime}
with
\partial T^{\prime}=[\![\Gamma^{\prime}]\!]
have the desired regularity of Theorem
1.1
, i.e.,
T^{\prime}=[\![\Sigma^{\prime}]\!]
for a smooth, precompact, oriented hypersurface
\Sigma^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with
\partial\Sigma^{\prime}=\Gamma^{\prime}
and

\operatorname{sing}\Sigma^{\prime}=\emptyset\text{ if }n+1\leq 11,\text{ else }\dim\operatorname{sing}\Sigma^{\prime}\leq n-10-\epsilon_{n},

where
\epsilon_{n}>0
is a dimensional constant.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
assuming Proposition
8.1
.

This reduction was carried out in
[
CMS24
, Section 6]
, and we recall the main steps here.

We may perturb
\Gamma\mapsto\Gamma^{\prime}
so that minimizing integral
n
-currents
T^{\prime}
with
\partial T^{\prime}=[\![\Gamma^{\prime}]\!]
satisfy the two extra constraints of Proposition
8.1
. Specifically, (1) gets arranged as in
[
CMS24
, Section 6.1]
, and (2) as in
[
CMS24
, Section 6.3]
. The argument relied on the Hardt–Simon boundary regularity theorem for minimizing integral
n
-currents and White’s improvement,
[
HS79
,
Whi83
]
.
23
23

23

See
[
CMS23a
, Proof of Theorem 4.1 assuming Lemmas 4.1, 4.2]
for another approach.

Thus, we may apply Proposition
8.1
with
\Gamma^{\prime},T^{\prime}
in place of
\Gamma,T
. This yields
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
and
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
with the asserted improved regularity.
∎

Proof of Proposition
8.1
.

First we note:

Claim 8.2
.

We may find a precompact open set
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with smooth boundary
\partial M
and the following properties:

(i)

\Gamma\subset\partial M
,
\operatorname{spt}T\setminus\Gamma\subset M
;

(ii)

\operatorname{spt}T
and
\partial M
meet smoothly and transversely along
\Gamma
;

(iii)

T\lfloor M
is a minimizing boundary in
M
.

We may also construct a smooth deformation
(\Gamma_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\partial M
of
\Gamma_{0}:=\Gamma
so that:

(iv)

for all
t\in[0,1]
, every minimizing
T_{t}
with
\partial T_{t}=[\![\Gamma_{t}]\!]
satisfies (i)-(iii) with
T_{t}
and
\Gamma_{t}
in place of
T
and
\Gamma
;

(v)

for all pairs
t^{\pm}\in[0,1]
, every pair of distinct minimizing
T^{\pm}
with
\partial T^{\pm}=[\![\Gamma_{t^{\pm}}]\!]
have disjoint supports in
M
;

(vi)

there exists
c>0
such that, for all
t^{\pm}\in[0,1]
,
T^{\pm}
as in (v),

|t^{+}-t^{-}|\leq c|\mathbf{x}^{+}-\mathbf{x}^{-}|,\;\text{ for all }\mathbf{x}^{\pm}\in\operatorname{spt}T^{\pm}.

Proof of claim.

Statements (i)-(iv) follow in a standard way. Indeed, from the uniqueness of
T_{0}
(assumption (1)), any choice of minimizers
T_{t_{i}}
with
t_{i}\to 0
converge to
T_{0}
. Moreover, from the unit density of
T_{0}
(assumption (2)) and Allard’s interior
[
All72
]
and boundary regularity
[
All75
]
, the convergence
T_{t_{i}}\to T_{0}
is smooth up to the boundary.
24
24

24

See the proof of
[
CMS23a
, Lemma 4.3]
for a full exposition.

It remains to establish (v)-(vi). To that end, we will take the deformation
(\Gamma_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
to be via a local foliation along
\partial M
such that:

(I)

the normal speed of the foliation
(\Gamma_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is positive;

(II)

for all
t^{\pm}\in[0,1]
with
t^{+}>t^{-}
,
[\![\Gamma_{t^{+}}]\!]-[\![\Gamma_{t^{-}}]\!]
is the boundary of a positively oriented smooth subdomain in
\partial M
.

Now, (v) follows from the standard cut-and-paste trick (see
[
CMS23a
, Lemma 2.8]
), whose boundary monotonicity assumptions are guaranteed by (II) above.

Finally, in view of (I), the inequality in (vi) is equivalent (for a different
c
) to:

(8.1)

d(\Gamma_{t^{+}},\Gamma_{t^{-}})\leq c\cdot d(\operatorname{spt}T^{+},\operatorname{spt}T^{-}).

The validity of (
8.1
) follows by elementary arguments since, by the maximum principle, the distance between
\operatorname{spt}T^{\pm}
is attained at either boundary, and all
T^{\pm}
are uniformly graphical as discussed for (i)-(iv).
∎

We wish to invoke Corollary
1.6
on:

•

the manifold
M
with the Euclidean metric,

•

the collection of minimizing boundaries

\mathscr{F}:=\bigcup_{t\in[0,1]}\{T\lfloor M:T\text{ minimizing with }\partial T=[\![\Gamma_{t}]\!]\},

•

the function

\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{x}):=t,\;\mathbf{x}\in M\cap\operatorname{spt}T_{t},\;t\in[0,1].

Note that the conditions of Corollary
1.6
(i.e., of Theorem
1.4
therein) are satisfied: (1) follows from (iv), (2) from (v), (3) is trivial, and (4) follows from (vi). Upon applying Corollary
1.6
, the result follows by choosing
t
close enough to
0
.
∎

9.
Proof of Theorem
1.2
(Homology minimization problem)

In this section we provide the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
(N^{n+1},g)
and
[\alpha]\in H_{n}(N,\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{[0]\}
be as in Theorem
1.2
and
T
be a
g
-minimizing integral
n
-current with in
[\alpha]
. Assume that
T
additionally satisfies:

(1)

T
is uniquely minimizing, and

(2)

T
has multiplicity one on
\operatorname{reg}T
.

There exist
C^{\infty}
-small perturbations
g^{\prime}
of
g
for which all
g^{\prime}
-minimizing integral
n
-currents
T^{\prime}
in
[\alpha]
have the desired regularity of Theorem
1.2
, i.e.,
T^{\prime}=[\![\Sigma^{\prime}]\!]
for a smooth, precompact, oriented hypersurface
\Sigma^{\prime}\subset N
with

\operatorname{sing}\Sigma^{\prime}=\emptyset\text{ if }n+1\leq 11,\text{ else }\dim\operatorname{sing}\Sigma^{\prime}\leq n-10-\epsilon_{n},

where
\epsilon_{n}>0
is a dimensional constant.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
assuming Proposition
9.1
.

This reduction was essentially carried out in
[
CMS23a
]
but not in
[
CMS24
]
, and we recall it here.

Claim 9.2
.

Let
T
be
g
-minimizing in
[\alpha]
. There exist
C^{\infty}
-small perturbations
g^{\prime}
of
g
such that
T
is uniquely
g^{\prime}
-minimizing in
[\alpha]
.

Proof of claim.

One may take
g^{\prime}:=(1+\varepsilon u^{2})g
, where
\varepsilon>0
is small and
u:N\to[0,\infty)
is smooth and
u=0
precisely on
\operatorname{spt}T
. See
[
CMS23a
, Lemma 5.2]
.
∎

Use Claim
9.2
to replace
g,T
with
g^{\prime},T^{\prime}=T
so that (1) of Proposition
9.1
holds. Write

(9.1)

T^{\prime}=:\sum_{i=1}^{Q}k^{\prime}_{i}[\![\Sigma^{\prime}_{i}]\!],

where the
\Sigma^{\prime}_{i}
are disjoint, connected, smooth, precompact, oriented hypersurfaces (with the usual a priori regularity
\dim\operatorname{sing}\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}\leq n-7
). Note that

T_{1}^{\prime}:=\sum_{i=1}^{Q}[\![\Sigma^{\prime}_{i}]\!]

is
g^{\prime}
-minimizing in some
[\alpha_{1}]
by
[
Sim83
, Theorem 33.4]
, and it clearly satisfies (2) of Proposition
9.1
by construction. It also satisfies (1) of Proposition
9.1
, otherwise
T^{\prime}
would not.

We may invoke Proposition
9.1
with
g^{\prime},[\alpha_{1}]\ni T_{1}^{\prime}
in place of
g,[\alpha]\ni T
. We obtain
g^{\prime\prime}
with the improved regularity property for all
g^{\prime\prime}
-minimizers in
[\alpha_{1}]
. Choose some
g^{\prime\prime}
-minimizing
T_{1}^{\prime\prime}
in
[\alpha_{1}]
. Since
T_{1}^{\prime}
satisfies (1) and (2), if
g^{\prime\prime}
is close enough to
g^{\prime}
, then we may write

T_{1}^{\prime\prime}=:\sum_{i=1}^{Q}[\![\Sigma^{\prime\prime}_{i}]\!]

where, for each
i
,

(9.2)

[\![\Sigma^{\prime\prime}_{i}]\!]\text{ is homologous to }[\![\Sigma^{\prime}_{i}]\!],

in addition to having the desired improved regularity

(9.3)

\operatorname{sing}\Sigma^{\prime\prime}_{i}=\emptyset\text{ if }n+1\leq 11,\text{ else }\dim\operatorname{sing}\Sigma^{\prime\prime}_{i}\leq n-10-\epsilon_{n}.

Fix such
g^{\prime\prime},T_{1}^{\prime\prime}
. Define

T^{\prime\prime}:=\sum_{i=1}^{Q}k_{i}^{\prime}[\![\Sigma^{\prime\prime}_{i}]\!],

with
k^{\prime}_{i}
from (
9.1
). It follows that
T^{\prime\prime}\in[\alpha]
in view of (
9.2
). Moreover,
T^{\prime\prime}
is also
g^{\prime\prime}
-minimizing in
[\alpha]
, in view of
[
Whi83
, Corollary 3]
since
T_{1}^{\prime\prime}
is
g^{\prime\prime}
-minimizing in
[\alpha_{1}]
. Note that
T^{\prime\prime}
has the desired improved regularity in view of (
9.3
). To conclude, we may guarantee that
T^{\prime\prime}
is uniquely
g^{\prime\prime}
-minimizing in
[\alpha]
by applying Claim
9.2
once more.
∎

Our proof of Proposition
9.1
mirrors our proof of Proposition
8.1
:

Proof of Proposition
9.1
.

Retracing the proof of
[
CMS23a
, Lemma 5.7]
, we may arrange for an open subset
\breve{N}\subset N
with the following properties:

(i)

\operatorname{spt}T\subset\breve{N}
;

(ii)

T
is a
g
-minimizing boundary in
\breve{N}
.

The result now follows as in Proposition
8.1
provided we prove:

Claim 9.3
.

There exists
w\in C^{\infty}(N)
so that the smooth deformation

g_{t}:=e^{2tw}g,\;t\in[0,1],

of
g_{0}:=g
exhibits the following properties:

(iii)

for all
t\in[0,1]
, all
g_{t}
-minimizing
T
in
[\alpha]
satisfy (i)-(ii) with
g_{t}
in place of
g
;

(iv)

for all
t^{\pm}\in[0,1]
, all distinct
g_{t^{\pm}}
-minimizing
T^{\pm}
in
[\alpha]
have disjoint supports;

(v)

there exists
c^{\prime}>0
such that, for all
t^{\pm}\in[0,1]
,
T^{\pm}
as in (iv),

d_{g}(\mathbf{x}^{+},\mathbf{x}^{-})\geq c^{\prime}|t^{+}-t^{-}|,\;\text{ for all }\mathbf{x}^{\pm}\in\operatorname{spt}T^{\pm}.

Obtaining
\breve{N}
satisfying (i)-(ii) follows from
[
CMS23a
, Lemma 5.7]
and assumption (2). Moreover, (iii) is a standard consequence of assumption (1).

It remains to establish (iv)-(v). To that end, we will take

g_{t}:=e^{2tw}g,\;t\in[0,1],

for suitable
w\in C^{\infty}(N)
. We also fix
p\in\operatorname{reg}T
, and let
r,h>0
be such that

\Phi:D_{r}\times(-h,h)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\approx}}{{\to}}C_{r,h}\subset N

are well-defined Fermi coordinates off of a radius-
r
normal ball
D_{r}\subset\operatorname{reg}T
centered at
p
, to a height-
h
cylinder over it. We may take
w\in C^{\infty}(N)
to satisfy, for some
c>1
:

(I)

\operatorname{spt}w\cap\breve{N}\subset C_{r,h}\cap\breve{N}
;

(II)

C_{r/c,h}\cap\breve{N}\subset\{w\neq 0\}
;

(III)

\partial_{z}w\leq-c^{-1}(|\nabla_{g}w|_{g}+|w|)
on
\breve{N}
, with
z
the vertical Fermi coordinate.

These conditions can be arranged by taking
w
to be
(-1)
times a suitable smoothing of the indicator of
B^{g}_{\varsigma/2}(\Phi(p,\varsigma/3))
, where
0<\varsigma<\min\{r,h\}
, and a posteriori shrinking
\breve{N}
(
\supset\operatorname{spt}T
) and enlarging
c
as appropriate. Note that to arrange for (III) it is convenient to shrink
\breve{N}
so far such that
B^{g}_{\varsigma/4}(\Phi(p,\varsigma/3))\cap\breve{N}=\emptyset
. In view of assumption (1), we may take a small enough multiple of
w
(note this does not affect (I)-(III)) to also guarantee:

(IV)

for all
t\in[0,1]
, every
g_{t}
-minimizing
T
in
[\alpha]
satisfies

\operatorname{spt}T\subset\breve{N};

(V)

for all
t
,
T
as above, there exists a smooth
u:D_{r}\to(-h,h)
with

\operatorname{spt}T\cap C_{r,h}=\operatorname{graph}_{D_{r}}u,

\|u\|_{C^{2}(D_{r})}+\|w\|_{C^{1}(C_{r,h})}<\delta,

and

(9.4)

g(\nu,\nabla_{g}w)\leq-c^{-1}|\partial_{z}w|\text{ on }C_{r,h},

where
c
is from (III),
\delta\in(0,h)
is small, and
\nu
is the upward pointing normal to
\operatorname{spt}T
. Graphs are always with respect to the fixed background metric
g
.

Proof of Claim
9.3
(iv).

Consider, first, the following weaker version of (iv):

(iv’)

If
t\in[0,1]
, and
T
,
T^{\prime}
are distinct
g_{t}
-minimizers in
[\alpha]
, then their supports are disjoint.

We know that this holds from the standard cut-and-paste trick (see
[
CMS23a
, Lemma 2.8]
) on a manifold with a fixed metric. For distinct metrics, we assert:

(iv”)

If
t^{\pm}\in[0,1]
,
t^{+}>t^{-}
,
T^{\pm}
are
g_{t^{\pm}}
-minimizers in
[\alpha]
, and
u^{\pm}
are as in (V) for
T^{\pm}
, then the supports of
T^{\pm}
are disjoint, with
u^{+}>u^{-}
on
D_{r}
.

Let us reorient the boundaries
T^{\pm}
so that, in
C_{r,h}
, the upward pointing unit normals
\nu^{\pm}
from (V) are both inward pointing (with respect to the regions they bound). Write
T^{\pm}=:\partial[\![E^{\pm}]\!]
. We may then construct new minimizing boundaries,

\hat{T}^{+}:=\partial[\![E^{+}\cap E^{-}]\!],

\hat{T}^{-}:=\partial[\![E^{+}\cup E^{-}]\!].

A weaker version of (V) holds for
\hat{T}^{\pm}
. The Lipschitz functions

\hat{u}^{+}:=\max\{u^{+},u^{-}\},

\hat{u}^{-}:=\min\{u^{+},u^{-}\}.

satisfy

(9.5)

\operatorname{spt}\hat{T}^{\pm}\cap C_{r,h}=\operatorname{graph}_{D_{r}}\hat{u}^{\pm}.

By construction and our assumption, we have

(9.6)

u^{+}\leq\hat{u}^{+}\text{ on }D_{r},

By construction,
T^{\pm}
,
\hat{T}^{\pm}
are all homologous and, with respect to any metric, we have (see
[
Whi83
, Decomposition Theorem]
) the following equality of Radon measures:

(9.7)

\|T^{+}\|+\|T^{-}\|=\|\hat{T}^{+}\|+\|\hat{T}^{-}\|.

Note that (
9.7
) and the
g_{t^{-}}
-minimizing nature of
T^{-}
imply

(9.8)

\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{-}}}(T^{+})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{-}}}(\hat{T}^{+})=\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{-}}}(\hat{T}^{-})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{-}}}(T^{-})\geq 0.

Likewise,
T^{+}
being
g_{t^{+}}
-minimizing implies

(9.9)

\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(T^{+})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(\hat{T}^{+})\leq 0.

On the other hand, we also have

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(T^{+})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(\hat{T}^{+})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(T^{+})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{-}}}(T^{+})

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{-}}}(T^{+})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{-}}}(\hat{T}^{+})

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{-}}}(\hat{T}^{+})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(\hat{T}^{+})

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(T^{+})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{-}}}(T^{+})

(9.10)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{-}}}(\hat{T}^{+})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(\hat{T}^{+}),

where, for the inequality, we invoked (
9.8
). Thus, (
9
) implies:

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(T^{+})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(\hat{T}^{+})

\displaystyle\geq(\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(T^{+})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{-}}}(T^{+}))

(9.11)

\displaystyle\qquad-(\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(\hat{T}^{+})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{-}}}(\hat{T}^{+})).

We evaluate the right hand side of (
9
) within
C_{r,h}
using (I) and (IV):

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(T^{+})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(\hat{T}^{+})

\displaystyle\geq(\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(\operatorname{graph}_{D_{r}}u^{+})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{-}}}(\operatorname{graph}_{D_{r}}u^{+}))

(9.12)

\displaystyle\qquad-(\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(\operatorname{graph}_{D_{r}}\hat{u}^{+})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{-}}}(\operatorname{graph}_{D_{r}}\hat{u}^{+})).

We will study the right hand side of (
9
) via the following interpolating families: for
\sigma,\tau\in[0,1]

\displaystyle u^{(\sigma)}

\displaystyle:=\hat{u}^{+}+\sigma(u^{+}-\hat{u}^{+}),

\displaystyle w^{(\tau)}

\displaystyle:=(t^{-}+\tau(t^{+}-t^{-}))w,

\displaystyle g^{(\tau)}

\displaystyle:=g_{t^{-}+\tau(t^{+}-t^{-})}=e^{2w^{(\tau)}}g.

Define
A:[0,1]\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
via

A(\sigma,\tau)=\mathbb{M}_{g^{(\tau)}}(\operatorname{graph}_{D_{r}}u^{(\sigma)})=\int_{\operatorname{graph}_{D_{r}}u^{(\sigma)}}e^{nw^{(\tau)}}.

All integrals are with respect to the measure induced by the fixed background metric
g
. We see that (
9
) implies:

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(T^{+})-\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(\hat{T}^{+})

\displaystyle\geq(A(1,1)-A(1,0))-(A(0,1)-A(0,0))

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}A_{\tau}(1,\tau)\,d\tau-\int_{0}^{1}A_{\tau}(0,\tau)\,d\tau

(9.13)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}A_{\tau\sigma}(\sigma,\tau)\,d\sigma\,d\tau.

To take the differentials, we rewrite, using the area formula

\displaystyle A(\sigma,\tau)=\int_{D_{r}}F[u^{(\sigma)}]\cdot(e^{nw^{(\tau)}}\circ\Phi_{\sigma})

where:

•

\Phi_{\sigma}(x):=\Phi(x,u^{(\sigma)}(x))
is the graphical parametrization of
\operatorname{graph}_{D_{r}}u^{(\sigma)}
, and

•

F=F(x,z,p)
satisfies the estimate on the derivatives on
D_{r}
:

(9.14)

\displaystyle F(x,0,0)=1,\quad F_{z}(x,0,0)=F_{p}(x,0,0)=0;\quad\|F\|_{C^{2}}\leq C(T,N);

see
[
CM20
, (A.12)]
for an exact formula for
F
. And
F[u]:=F(x,u(x),\nabla u(x))
.

Differentiating
A
in
\tau
and rearranging:

\frac{A_{\tau}(\sigma,\tau)}{n(t^{+}-t^{-})}=\int_{D_{r}}F[u^{(\sigma)}]\cdot\big((e^{nw^{(\tau)}}w)\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\big).

Then differentiating in
\sigma
too and letting
v:=u^{+}-\hat{u}^{+}(\leq 0)
:

\displaystyle\frac{A_{\tau\sigma}(\sigma,\tau)}{n(t^{+}-t^{-})}

\displaystyle=\int_{D_{r}}\left(F_{z}(x,u^{(\sigma)},\nabla u^{(\sigma)})v+F_{p}(x,u^{(\sigma)},\nabla u^{(\sigma)})\cdot\nabla v\right)\cdot\big((e^{nw^{(\tau)}}w)\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{D_{r}}F[u^{(\sigma)}]\cdot\big(\partial_{z}(e^{nw^{(\tau)}}w)\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\big)\cdot v,

Let
\Omega:=\{x\in D_{r}:u^{+}<\hat{u}^{+}\}
. We now assert that, for
\delta
sufficiently small depending on
N,g,T,D_{r},c
in (V), we have
\Omega\cap\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1}(\operatorname{spt}w)=\emptyset
for every
\sigma\in[0,1]
.
Suppose for contradiction that
\bar{\sigma}\in[0,1]
such that
\Omega\cap\Phi_{\bar{\sigma}}^{-1}(\operatorname{spt}w)\neq\emptyset
.
To handle the term with
\nabla v
in the equation of
A_{\tau\sigma}
above, first note that
v=u^{+}-\hat{u}^{+}
is smooth on
\Omega
and vanishes on
D_{r}\setminus\Omega
. Furthermore
\nabla v=0
a.e. on
D_{r}\setminus\Omega
.
Let
\Omega_{j}\Subset\Omega
be a increasing smooth exhaustion of
\Omega
such that
\|w\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\|_{C^{1}(\partial\Omega_{j})}\cdot\|v\|_{C^{0}(\partial\Omega_{j})}
tends to
0
as
j\to\infty
.
Then

\displaystyle\int_{D_{r}}\Big(F_{p}(x,u^{(\sigma)},\nabla u^{(\sigma)})\cdot\nabla v\Big)\cdot\big((e^{nw^{(\tau)}}w)\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\Omega_{j}}(F_{p}(x,u^{(\sigma)},\nabla u^{(\sigma)})\cdot\nabla v)\cdot\big((e^{nw^{(\tau)}}w)\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\Omega_{j}}-\operatorname{div}\Big(F_{p}(x,u^{(\sigma)},\nabla u^{(\sigma)})\cdot\big((e^{nw^{(\tau)}}w)\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\big)\Big)v

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\partial\Omega_{j}}\left(F_{p}(x,u^{(\sigma)},\nabla u^{(\sigma)})\cdot\zeta_{j}\right)\cdot\big((e^{nw^{(\tau)}}w)\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\big)v

\displaystyle\qquad=-\int_{\Omega}\operatorname{div}\Big(F_{p}(x,u^{(\sigma)},\nabla u^{(\sigma)})\cdot\big((e^{nw^{(\tau)}}w)\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\big)\Big)v

where
\zeta_{j}
denotes the outward unit normal field of
\partial\Omega_{j}
in
\Omega
. Thus we find,

(9.15)

\displaystyle\frac{A_{\tau\sigma}(\sigma,\tau)}{n(t^{+}-t^{-})}=\int_{\Omega}\Big(L(\sigma,\tau)+F[u^{(\sigma)}]\cdot\big(\partial_{z}(e^{nw^{(\tau)}}w)\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\big)\Big)v,

where

\displaystyle L(\sigma,\tau)

\displaystyle:=F_{z}(x,u^{(\sigma)},\nabla u^{(\sigma)})\cdot\big((e^{nw^{(\tau)}}w)\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad-\operatorname{div}\Big(F_{p}(x,u^{(\sigma)},\nabla u^{(\sigma)})\cdot\big((e^{nw^{(\tau)}}w)\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\big)\Big)

Note that on
\Omega
,
u^{(\sigma)}=u^{-}+\sigma(u^{+}-u^{-})
is smooth, hence together with (
9.14
), we have pointwise estimate on
\Omega
,

(9.16)

\displaystyle|L(\sigma,\tau)|\leq C(T,N,D_{r})\left(\|u^{+}\|_{C^{2}(D_{r})}+\|u^{-}\|_{C^{2}(D_{r})}\right)(|w|+|\nabla_{g}w|_{g})\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\,.

While by (III),
\partial_{z}w<0
, then by (
9.14
) and (V) with
\delta(N,g,T,D_{r})>0
small enough, we have

(9.17)

\displaystyle F[u^{(\sigma)}]\cdot\left(\partial_{z}(e^{nw^{(\tau)}}w)\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\right)\leq\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{z}w+C(n)\|w\|_{C^{1}(C_{r,h})}|w|\big)\circ\Phi_{\sigma}\,.

Since we assumed
\Omega\cap\Phi_{\bar{\sigma}}^{-1}(\operatorname{spt}w)\neq\emptyset
(for contradiction) and we have that
v<0
in
\Omega
, we can combine (
9.15
)-(
9.17
) with (III) and (V) to choose
\delta(N,g,T,D_{r},c)>0
small enough so that
A_{\tau\sigma}(\bar{\sigma},\tau)>0
. Since
A_{\tau\sigma}(\sigma,\tau)
is continuous in
\sigma
, this contradicts (
9.9
) and (
9
).

Now we fix the choice of
\delta
in (V) so that
\Omega\cap\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1}(\operatorname{spt}w)=\emptyset
for all
\sigma\in[0,1]
, i.e., so that we have the weak version of the inequality asserted in (iv”):

u^{+}=\hat{u}^{+}\geq u^{-}\text{ on }D_{r}\cap(\cup_{\sigma\in[0,1]}\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1}(\operatorname{spt}w)).

Plugging this back into (
9.15
), (
9
) and recalling (
9.9
) yields

\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(T^{+})=\mathbb{M}_{g_{t^{+}}}(\hat{T}^{+}).

Therefore,
T^{+}
,
\hat{T}^{+}
are both
g_{t^{+}}
-minimizers in
[\alpha]
.

We claim that
T^{+}
,
\hat{T}^{+}
coincide. Indeed, if they did not, then they would be disjoint by (iv’). Thus,
\hat{T}^{+}=T^{-}
would be disjoint from
T^{+}
and
\hat{u}^{+}=u^{-}>u^{+}
, this contradicts the weak inequality
u^{+}\geq u^{-}
on
D_{r}\cap(\cup_{\sigma\in[0,1]}\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1}(\operatorname{spt}w))
that we have already concluded. Thus
\hat{T}^{+}=T^{+}
.

We assert that strict inequality
u^{+}>u^{-}
holds on
D_{r}
and that the supports of
T^{+}
,
T^{-}
are disjoint. Indeed, carrying out a conformal metric change computation shows that

(9.18)

H(T^{-},g_{t^{+}})=(n-1)(t^{+}-t^{-})e^{-t^{+}w}\langle\nabla_{g}w,\nu\rangle_{g},

where
\nu
is the upward unit normal to
\operatorname{spt}T^{-}
with respect to
g
. Using (II), (III), and (V)’s (
9.4
), we see that
H(T^{-},g_{t^{+}})\leq 0
and that this inequality is strict over
D_{r/c}
. Since
H(T^{+},g_{t^{+}})=0
, the maximum principle implies that the supports of
T^{+}
,
T^{-}
are disjoint, with
u^{+}>u^{-}
. This completes the proof of (iv”) which yields
(iv)
.
∎

Proof of Claim
9.3
(v).

We pick up from (
9.18
). By a standard PDE sliding argument (see, e.g., the proof of
[
CMS23a
, Proposition 5.6 (f)]
), and the fact that
H(T^{-},g_{t^{+}})<0
over
D_{r/c}
, we deduce that

(9.19)

u^{+}-u^{-}\geq c^{\prime}(t^{+}-t^{-})\text{ over }D_{r},

for a constant
c^{\prime}>0
independent of
t^{\pm}
,
T^{\pm}
. Note that (
9.19
) trivially implies

(9.20)

d_{g}(\operatorname{spt}\partial(T^{+}\lfloor(N\setminus C_{r,h})),\operatorname{spt}\partial(T^{-}\lfloor(N\setminus C_{r,h})))\geq c^{\prime}(t^{+}-t^{-}),

for a possibly smaller
c^{\prime}>0
. In view of condition (I), both these currents are minimizing, so arguing as in (
8.1
), we deduce from (
9.20
) that

(9.21)

d_{g}(\operatorname{spt}T^{+},\operatorname{spt}T^{-})\geq c^{\prime}(t^{+}-t^{-}),

for a different yet
c^{\prime}>0
. This implies the required result.
∎

The result now follows as in Proposition
8.1
. We wish to invoke Corollary
1.6
on:

•

the manifold
(M,g):=(\breve{N}\setminus C_{r,h},g)
,

•

the collection of minimizing boundaries

\mathscr{F}:=\bigcup_{t\in[0,1]}\{T\lfloor(\breve{N}\setminus C_{r,h}):T\text{ $g_{t}$-minimizing in }[\alpha]\},

•

the function

\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{x}):=t,\;\mathbf{x}\in(\breve{N}\setminus C_{r,h})\cap\operatorname{spt}T_{t},\;t\in[0,1].

Note that the conditions of Corollary
1.6
(i.e., of Theorem
1.4
therein) are satisfied: (1) follows from (iii), (2) from (iv’) and (iv”), (3) is trivial, and (4) follows from (v). Upon applying Corollary
1.6
, the result follows by choosing
t
close enough to
0
.
∎

Appendix A
Quadratic hypercones

We recall that quadratic hypercones are precisely rotations of

\mathcal{C}_{p,q}=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{p+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{q+1}:q|x|^{2}=p|y|^{2}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

where
p,q>0
are integers with
p+q=n-1
.

We shall recall facts about quadratic hypercones that are pertinent to this paper, but we direct the interested reader to
[
Sim74
,
SS86
,
ES23
]
for a comprehensive treatment.

Proposition A.1
.

\mathcal{C}_{p,q}
is stable if and only if
p+q\geq 6
(or equivalently
n+1\geq 8
);

\mathcal{C}_{p,q}
is minimizing if and only if
p+q\geq 6
and
(p,q)\neq(1,5),\ (5,1)
.

Proof.

The stability of
\mathcal{C}_{p,q}
follows from
[
Sim68
]
; The minimizing property of
\mathcal{C}_{p,q}
follows from
[
BDGG69
,
Law72
,
Sim74
]
.
∎

Proposition A.2
.

Each stable quadratic hypercone
\mathcal{C}
is strictly stable (Definition
3.9
).

Proof.

This follows from the explicit spectral characterization in
[
CHS84
, Theorem 4.5]
and the fact that
|A|^{2}=n-1<\tfrac{(n-2)^{2}}{4}
(for
n+1\geq 8
) on the link of every such
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Proposition A.3
.

Each minimizing quadratic hypercone
\mathcal{C}
is strictly minimizing (Definition
H.2
).

Proof.

See
[
Lin87
]
or alternatively
[
Law91
]
(see the discussion in (3) on p. 100 there and then the calculation on p. 69–70).
∎

Proposition A.4
.

Each quadratic hypercone
\mathcal{C}
is strongly integrable (Definition
3.12
).

Proof.

This follows from
[
SS86
, Proposition 2.7]
.
∎

Corollary A.5
.

Each quadratic hypercone is
\mathcal{C}
isolated in the space of minimal hypercones.

Appendix B
A Frankel property of nonflat minimizing hypercones

Lemma B.1
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is a minimizing hypercone, and
\Sigma
is a minimizing boundary in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
that does not cross
\mathcal{C}
smoothly that is conical about some
p\in\operatorname{sing}\Sigma
. Then,

\Sigma=\pm\mathcal{C}\text{ and }p\in\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}.

Proof.

This is contained in the proof of
[
CMS23a
, Proposition 3.3]
. We briefly recall the strategy. By blowing down
\Sigma
, one obtains a minimizing hypercone
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
that does not cross
\mathcal{C}
smoothly (or else
\Sigma
would have since
\mathcal{C}
is dilation invariant). Therefore, by Simon’s strong maximum principle for minimizers (
[
Sim87
]
),
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\pm\mathcal{C}
.

Since
\Sigma
was conical about
p
to begin with, we must have

\Sigma=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}+p=\mathcal{C}+p.

If
p=\mathbf{0}
, we are done. Otherwise, consider the rescaled minimizers

\Sigma_{t}:=t\Sigma=\mathcal{C}+tp,\;t\in(0,1).

By sending
t\to 0
and writing
\Sigma_{t}
as graphs locally over compact subsets of
\mathcal{C}
and using the regular Harnack inequality, we obtain the Jacobi field
\langle\nu_{\mathcal{C}},p\rangle
on
\mathcal{C}
. Since
\mathcal{C}
is dilation invariant, none of the
\Sigma_{t}
cross
\mathcal{C}
smoothly, and therefore

\langle\nu_{\mathcal{C}},p\rangle\geq 0\text{ on }\mathcal{C}.

By the strong maximum principle on the Jacobi field
\langle\nu_{\mathcal{C}},p\rangle
,
\langle\nu_{\mathcal{C}},p\rangle\equiv 0
or
\langle\nu_{\mathcal{C}},p\rangle>0
. The latter is impossible:
\langle\nu_{\mathcal{C}},p\rangle
would be a bounded Jacobi field on
\mathcal{C}
, in contradiction to Simon’s Harnack theory for positive Jacobi fields on nonflat minimizing hypercones
[
Sim08
]
(cf.
[
Wan24
, Theorem 1.4]
). Thus,
\langle\nu_{\mathcal{C}},p\rangle\equiv 0
, so
\mathcal{C}
splits a line parallel to
p
, so
p\in\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}
, and
\Sigma=\mathcal{C}+p=\mathcal{C}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
\alpha(\mathcal{C};\sigma)
as decay rate of positive Jacobi fields

Lemma C.1
.

There exist
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{1}(n)
such that for all
\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
:

(i)

\operatorname{reg}_{>4\sigma_{1}}\mathcal{C}\cap\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{0})\neq\emptyset
, and

(ii)

for all
\sigma\in(0,\sigma_{1}]
, there exists
H=H(n,\sigma)
such that

\inf_{\operatorname{reg}_{>2\sigma}\mathcal{C}\cap\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{0})}u<H\rho^{\alpha(\mathcal{C};\sigma/2)}\inf_{\operatorname{reg}_{>\frac{1}{2}\rho\sigma}\mathcal{C}\cap\partial B_{\rho}(\mathbf{y})}u

for all positive Jacobi fields
u
on
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathbf{y}\in\operatorname{spine}\mathcal{C}\cap\bar{B}_{1}(\mathbf{0})
, and
\rho\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

(i) follows from
[
CMS23a
, Proposition 3.10]
.

For (ii), note that the proofs of
[
CMS23a
, Lemmas 3.8, B.1]
apply with
\Omega=\operatorname{reg}_{>\sigma}
and with
\alpha(\mathcal{C};\sigma)
in place of the cruder
\kappa_{n}^{*}
to yield

\inf_{\operatorname{reg}_{>\sigma}\mathcal{C}\cap\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{y})}u\leq\rho^{\alpha(\mathcal{C};\sigma)}\sup_{\operatorname{reg}_{>\sigma}\mathcal{C}\cap\partial B_{\rho}(\mathbf{y})}u.

The result now follows by replacing
\sigma
with
\sigma/2
and two invocations of Harnack (see, e.g.,
[
CMS23a
, Proposition 3.10]
) to adjust the left and right hand sides to the desired ones.
∎

Appendix D
A splitting lemma for homogeneous polynomials

Recall that for a polynomial
p\neq 0
on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
we denote by

\operatorname{ord}_{p}(y_{0})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

the degree of the lowest-order nonzero term in the expansion of
p
about
y_{0}
. First we record a trivial consequence of Taylor expansions:

Lemma D.1
.

Let
p\neq 0
be a polynomial on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Then

\operatorname{ord}_{p}(y_{0})\geq d\iff D^{\alpha}p(y_{0})=0\text{ for every }\alpha\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{k}\text{ with }|\alpha|\leq d-1.

We also have the following splitting result for homogeneous polynomials:

Lemma D.2
.

Let
p\neq 0
be a homogeneous polynomial on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

(i)

For every
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we have

\operatorname{ord}_{p}(y_{0})\geq d\iff D^{\alpha}p(y_{0})=0\text{ for every }\alpha\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{k}\text{ with }|\alpha|=d-1.

(ii)

Assume
\deg p=d
. Then, the set

V=\{y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\operatorname{ord}_{p}(y_{0})\geq d\}

is a linear subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
p
is invariant under translations in
V
.

Proof.

(i) In view of Lemma
D.1
, we need only check “
\impliedby
”. The proof for that follows by induction on
d
. The case
d=1
is again handled by Lemma
D.1
. Now suppose that
d\geq 2
and that the assertion holds for
d-1
in place of
d
. Fix
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
D^{\alpha}p(y_{0})=0
for all
|\alpha|=d-1
. Then for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
D_{i}p
is a homogeneous polynomial with

D^{\beta}(D_{i}p)(y_{0})=0\text{ for all }|\beta|=d-2.

By the inductive assumption, then,

\operatorname{ord}_{p}D_{i}p(y_{0})\geq d-1\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}.

By Lemma
D.1
again, for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,

D^{\beta}(D_{i}p)(y_{0})=0\text{ for all }|\beta|\leq d-2,

i.e.,

D^{\alpha}p(y_{0})=0\text{ for all }1\leq|\alpha|\leq d-1,

For
\alpha=0
it suffices to note that by homogeneity and the case of
|\alpha|=1
above,

(\deg p)\cdot p(y_{0})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(y_{0})_{i}D_{i}p(y_{0})=0,

which completes the inductive step and thus the proof.

(ii) Clearly
V
is a linear subspace since
D^{\alpha}p
is linear if
|\alpha|=d-1=\deg p-1
. It remains to show the translation invariance along
V
. To that end, fix
y_{0}\in V
. Then

\operatorname{ord}_{p}(y_{0})\geq d=\deg p\implies\operatorname{ord}_{p}(y_{0})=\deg p=d

in turn implies that
p(\cdot+y_{0})
is homogenous. Now for
t\neq 0
we can use homogeneity at
y_{0}
and then homogeneity at the origin to conclude that,

p(y_{0}+y)=t^{-d}p(y_{0}+ty)=p(t^{-1}y_{0}+y_{0})

for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Sending
t\to\infty
yields
p(y_{0}+y)=p(y)
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
∎

Appendix E
Normal coordinates

We consider
(M^{d},g)
satisfying

(E.1)

|\operatorname{Rm}|+|\nabla\operatorname{Rm}|+|\nabla^{2}\operatorname{Rm}|\leq C^{-1}\,,

We will choose
C=C(d)
later, in Lemma
E.1
. We will work with the subset:

\breve{M}:=\{p\in M:\operatorname{injrad}(M,g,p)\geq 2\}.

E.1.
Basics

Let
p\in\breve{M}
. For
r\in(0,2]
, write
B_{r}\subset T_{p}M
for the radius
r
ball centered at
\mathbf{0}\in T_{p}M
(with respect to the inner product
g_{p}
), and
B_{r}(p)\subset M
for the radius
r
ball centered at
p\in M
. For
\tau>0
, let

(E.2)

\eta_{p,\tau}:(B_{2\tau^{-1}}\subset T_{p}M)\to(B_{2}(p)\subset M),\qquad\mathbf{v}\mapsto\exp_{p}(\tau\mathbf{v}).

The inverse
\eta_{p,\tau}^{-1}:B_{2}(p)\to B_{2\tau^{-1}}
is also well-defined and smooth. We define

(E.3)

g_{p,\tau}:=\tau^{-2}\eta_{p,\tau}^{*}g\,.

This is a Riemannian metric on
B_{2\tau^{-1}}\subset T_{p}M
. If
g_{p}
denotes the flat metric on
T_{p}M
obtained by the inner product induced by
g
at
p
, then:

Lemma E.1
.

There is
C=C(d)
for (
E.1
) with the following property.

For every
p\in\breve{M}
,
k\in\{0,1,2\}
, and
R,\tau>0
with
R\tau\leq 2
, we have

\rVert\nabla^{k}(g_{p,\tau}-g_{p})\rVert_{C^{0}(B_{R})}\leq CR^{2-k}\tau^{2}.

The covariant derivative is taken with respect to
g_{p}
.

Proof.

Fix
p,\tau,R
and a
g_{p}
-orthonormal basis
\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{d}
for
T_{p}M
. Our goal is to prove the asserted estimate at
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{d})\in B_{R}
. To this end, define

\Gamma(t,s_{1},\dots,s_{d}):=\exp_{p}(t\tau(\mathbf{v}+s_{1}\mathbf{e}_{1}+\dots+s_{d}\mathbf{e}_{d})).

Let
\mathbf{T}=\partial_{t}\Gamma
and
\mathbf{S}_{i}=\partial_{s_{i}}\Gamma
. We define
\mathbf{E}_{i}(t)
by parallel transporting
\mathbf{e}_{i}
along
\gamma(t):=\Gamma(t,0,\dots,0)
.

Noting that
\Gamma(1,s_{1},\dots,s_{d})=\eta_{p,\tau}(s_{1}+v_{1},\dots,s_{d}+v_{d})
, we have
25
25

25

We write
D_{s_{i}}
for the derivative along the curve
s\mapsto\Gamma(t,s_{1},\dots,s_{i-1},s,s_{i+1},\dots,s_{d})
. (Alternatively, this is the pullback connection
\Gamma^{*}\nabla
in the direction
\partial_{s_{i}}
.)

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{i}(1,0,\dots,0)

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}\eta_{p,\tau}|_{\mathbf{v}}

\displaystyle D_{s_{i}}\mathbf{S}_{j}(1,0,\dots,0)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\partial_{i}\eta_{p,\tau}}\partial_{j}\eta_{p,\tau}|_{\mathbf{v}}

\displaystyle D_{s_{i}}D_{s_{j}}\mathbf{S}_{k}(1,0,\dots,0)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\partial_{i}\eta_{p,\tau}}\nabla_{\partial_{j}\eta_{p,\tau}}\partial_{k}\eta_{p,\tau}|_{\mathbf{v}}.

We observe the following boundary conditions for
\mathbf{S}_{i}
and its derivatives:

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{i}(0,0,\dots,0)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle D_{t}\mathbf{S}_{i}(0,0,\dots,0)

\displaystyle=D_{s_{i}}\mathbf{T}(0,0,\dots,0)=\tau\mathbf{e}_{i}

\displaystyle D_{s_{i}}\mathbf{S}_{j}(0,0,\dots,0)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle D_{t}D_{s_{i}}\mathbf{S}_{j}(0,0,\dots,0)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{Rm}(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{S}_{i})\mathbf{S}_{j}+D_{s_{i}}D_{s_{j}}\mathbf{T})(0,0,\dots,0)=0

\displaystyle D_{s_{i}}D_{s_{j}}\mathbf{S}_{k}(0,0,\dots,0)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle D_{t}D_{s_{i}}D_{s_{j}}\mathbf{S}_{k}(0,0,\dots,0)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{Rm}(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{S}_{i})D_{s_{j}}\mathbf{S}_{k}+D_{s_{i}}(\operatorname{Rm}(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{S}_{j})\mathbf{S}_{k})

\displaystyle\qquad+D_{s_{i}}D_{s_{j}}D_{s_{k}}\mathbf{T})(0,0,\dots,0)=0.

Note also that
\mathbf{S}_{i}
satisfies the Jacobi equation

D^{2}_{t}\mathbf{S}_{i}+\operatorname{Rm}(\mathbf{S}_{i},\mathbf{T})\mathbf{T}=0.

Combined with
|\mathbf{T}(t,0,\dots,0)|\leq R\tau
, the boundary conditions for
\mathbf{S}_{i}
, and curvature estimates (
E.1
) we obtain

(E.4)

|\mathbf{S}_{i}(t,0,\dots,0)-t\tau\mathbf{E}_{i}(t)|+|D_{t}\mathbf{S}_{i}(t,0,\dots,0)-\tau\mathbf{E}_{i}(t)|\leq CR^{2}\tau^{3}.

Indeed, for
C>0
to be chosen these estimates obviously hold for
t>0
very small. If the first time they fail is
t_{0}\in(0,1)
then on
(0,t_{0}]
we find that

\displaystyle|D_{t}^{2}(\mathbf{S}_{i}(t)-t\tau\mathbf{E}_{i}(t))|

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\operatorname{Rm}(\mathbf{S}_{i}(t),\mathbf{T})\mathbf{T}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\operatorname{Rm}(t\tau\mathbf{E}_{i}(t),\mathbf{T})\mathbf{T}\rvert+\lvert\operatorname{Rm}(\mathbf{S}_{i}(t)-t\tau\mathbf{E}_{i}(t),\mathbf{T})\mathbf{T}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C^{-1}tR^{2}\tau^{3}+R^{4}\tau^{5}

\displaystyle\leq(C^{-1}+4)R^{2}\tau^{3}

Integrating this twice and taking
C>0
sufficiently large proves that the asserted estimates hold past
t_{0}
, a contradiction.

Now, we consider the higher derivatives. Differentiating the Jacobi field equation and then commuting we obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=D_{s_{i}}D^{2}_{t}\mathbf{S}_{j}+D_{s_{i}}(\operatorname{Rm}(\mathbf{S}_{j},\mathbf{T})\mathbf{T})

\displaystyle=D_{t}^{2}D_{s_{i}}\mathbf{S}_{j}+D_{t}(\operatorname{Rm}(\mathbf{S}_{i},\mathbf{T})\mathbf{S}_{j})+\operatorname{Rm}(\mathbf{S}_{i},\mathbf{T})D_{t}\mathbf{S}_{j}+D_{s_{i}}(\operatorname{Rm}(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{S}_{j})\mathbf{T}).

As before, we obtain that

(E.5)

|D_{s_{i}}\mathbf{S}_{j}(t,0,\dots,0)|+|D_{t}D_{s_{i}}\mathbf{S}_{j}(t,0,\dots,0)|\leq CR\tau^{3}

possibly after increasing
C
. A similar argument yields

(E.6)

|D_{s_{i}}D_{s_{j}}\mathbf{S}_{k}(t,0,\dots,0)|+|D_{t}D_{s_{i}}D_{s_{j}}\mathbf{S}_{k}(t,0,\dots,0)|\leq C\tau^{3}

To complete the proof, we now observe that

g_{\tau,p}(\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbf{e}_{j})=\tau^{-2}g(\partial_{i}\eta_{p,\tau},\partial_{j}\eta_{p,\tau})

so

g_{\tau,p}(\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbf{e}_{j})|_{\mathbf{v}}=\tau^{-2}g(\mathbf{S}_{i},\mathbf{S}_{j})|_{(1,0,\dots,0)}

Using (
E.4
) we thus obtain (after taking
C
larger if necessary):

|g_{\tau,p}(\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbf{e}_{j})|_{\mathbf{v}}-\delta_{ij}|\leq CR^{2}\tau^{2}

Similarly, we have that

\partial_{i}(g_{\tau,p}(\mathbf{e}_{j},\mathbf{e}_{k}))|_{\mathbf{v}}=\tau^{-2}(g(D_{s_{i}}\mathbf{S}_{j},\mathbf{S}_{k})+g(\mathbf{S}_{j},D_{s_{i}}\mathbf{S}_{k}))|_{(1,0,\dots,0)}

so (
E.4
) and (
E.5
) give

|\partial_{i}(g_{\tau,p}(\mathbf{e}_{j},\mathbf{e}_{k}))|_{\mathbf{v}}|\leq CR\tau^{2}.

Using also (
E.6
) the second derivatives follow similarly. This proves the assertion.
∎

E.2.
Change of basepoint

Let
p\in\breve{M}
,
q\in B_{2}(p)
.

We define the
change of basepoint
map, from
p
to
q
at some scale
\tau>0
, to be

(E.7)

\eta_{q,\tau}^{-1}\circ\eta_{p,\tau}:(B_{(2-d(p,q))\tau^{-1}}\subset T_{p}M)\to(B_{2\tau^{-1}}\subset T_{q}M).

In a purely Euclidean background we would have had

(\eta_{q,\tau}^{-1}\circ\eta_{p,\tau})(\mathbf{v})=\mathbf{v}-\tau^{-1}(q-p).

In our Riemannian setting, there is an analogous estimate that makes use of the linear isometry that parallel transports along the minimizing geodesic from
p
to
q
:

\iota_{p\to q}:(T_{p}M,g_{p})\to(T_{q}M,g_{q})

Lemma E.2
.

Choose
C=C(d)
for (
E.1
) such that Lemma
E.1
holds.

Let
p\in\breve{M}
,
R,\tau>0
,
B_{\tau}(p)\subset\breve{M}
,
\mathbf{q}\in B_{1}\subset T_{p}M
,
q=\eta_{p,\tau}(\mathbf{q})
. If
(1+R)\tau<2
, then

\|(\eta_{q,\tau}^{-1}\circ\eta_{p,\tau})(\cdot)-\iota_{p\to q}(\cdot-\mathbf{q})\|_{C^{0}(B_{R})}\leq C^{\prime}\tau^{2},

where
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(C,R)
.

Proof.

Fix any
\mathbf{v}\in B_{R}\subset T_{p}M
and denote
v=\eta_{p,\tau}(\mathbf{v})
. Also let

\gamma(s):=\eta_{p,\tau}(s\mathbf{q})=\exp_{p}(s\tau\mathbf{q})

be the minimal geodesic between
p
and
q
parametrized by constant speed for
s\in[0,1]
.

For
s\in[0,1]
, we have

(E.8)

d(\gamma(s),v)\leq d(p,\gamma(s))+d(p,v)\leq d(p,q)+d(p,v)<(1+R)\tau.

Recalling that, by assumption,
(1+R)\tau<2
and
\gamma([0,1])\subset B_{\tau}(p)\subset\breve{M}
, there exists a smooth vector field
\mathbf{V}(s)
along
\gamma(s)
so that

\eta_{\gamma(s),\tau}(\mathbf{V}(s))=v.

Note that
(\eta_{q,\tau}^{-1}\circ\eta_{p,\tau})(\mathbf{v})=\mathbf{V}(1)

On the other hand, let
\bar{\mathbf{V}}(s)
denote the parallel transport of
\mathbf{v}
along
\gamma(s)
, i.e.,

\bar{\mathbf{V}}(s)=\iota_{p\to\gamma(s)}(\mathbf{v}).

Note that
\iota_{p\to q}(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{q})=\iota_{p\to q}(\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{p}=\bar{\mathbf{V}}(1)+\mathbf{p}
, where
p=\eta_{q,\tau}(\mathbf{p})
.

Since
\mathbf{p}=-\tau^{-1}\gamma^{\prime}(1)
, if we define

\hat{\mathbf{V}}(s):=\mathbf{V}(s)-\bar{\mathbf{V}}(s)+s\tau^{-1}\gamma^{\prime}(s),

then we will be done if we show that
|\hat{\mathbf{V}}(1)|\leq C\tau^{2}
. Note that

D_{s}\hat{\mathbf{V}}(s)=D_{s}\mathbf{V}(s)+\tau^{-1}\gamma^{\prime}(s).

To estimate this quantity, we define the geodesic variation

\Gamma(s,t):=\exp_{\gamma(s)}(t\tau\mathbf{V}(s)).

In particular,
\mathbf{S}:=\partial_{s}\Gamma
is a Jacobi field along each
t\mapsto\Gamma(s,t)
. Note that
\tau\mathbf{V}(s)=\mathbf{T}(s,0)
for
\mathbf{T}=\partial_{t}\Gamma
. Let
\bar{\mathbf{G}}(s,t)
denote the parallel transport of
\gamma^{\prime}(s)\in T_{\Gamma(s,0)}M
along
t\mapsto\Gamma(s,t)
. Then
\hat{\mathbf{S}}(s,t):=\mathbf{S}(s,t)-(1-t)\bar{\mathbf{G}}(s,t)
satisfies

(E.9)

\hat{\mathbf{S}}(s,0)=\mathbf{0}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \hat{\mathbf{S}}(s,1)=\mathbf{0}

(note that
\mathbf{S}(s,0)=\gamma^{\prime}(s)
and
\mathbf{S}(s,1)=\mathbf{0}
). We compute

D^{2}_{t}\hat{\mathbf{S}}=-\operatorname{Rm}(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{T})\mathbf{T}=-\operatorname{Rm}(\hat{\mathbf{S}},\mathbf{T})\mathbf{T}-(1-t)\operatorname{Rm}(\bar{\mathbf{G}},\mathbf{T})\mathbf{T}.

Because
|\mathbf{T}|\leq(1+R)\tau
by (
E.8
) and
|\bar{\mathbf{G}}|=d(p,q)\leq\tau
, we can combine this with (
E.1
), (
E.9
) to prove that
|\hat{\mathbf{S}}(s,t)|+|D_{t}\hat{\mathbf{S}}(s,t)|\leq C^{\prime}\tau^{3}
.
In particular, we find that

\displaystyle D_{s}\hat{\mathbf{V}}(s)

\displaystyle=D_{s}\mathbf{V}(s)+\tau^{-1}\gamma^{\prime}(s)

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}(D_{s}\mathbf{T}(s,0)+\mathbf{S}(s,0))

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}(D_{t}\mathbf{S}(s,0)+\mathbf{S}(s,0))

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}(D_{t}\hat{\mathbf{S}}(s,0)-\bar{\mathbf{G}}(s,0)+\hat{\mathbf{S}}(s,0)+\bar{\mathbf{G}}(s,0))

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}(D_{t}\hat{\mathbf{S}}(s,0)+\hat{\mathbf{S}}(s,0)).

Thus
|D_{s}\hat{\mathbf{V}}(s)|\leq C^{\prime}\tau^{2}
. Since
\hat{\mathbf{V}}(0)=\mathbf{0}
we can integrate this to get

|\hat{\mathbf{V}}(s)|\leq C^{\prime}\tau^{2}.

Evaluating this at
s=1
yields the desired estimate.
∎

Appendix F
The exponential map of a matrix

For
\mathbf{A}\in M_{d}(\mathbb{R})
(space of
d\times d
matrices), we define the usual exponential map

\exp\mathbf{A}=e^{\mathbf{A}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbf{A}^{k}}{k!}.

For a small neighborhood of
\operatorname{\textbf{Id}}
we can define
\log:=\exp^{-1}
.

Lemma F.1
.

There is
\lambda=\lambda(d)>0
so that

|\log(e^{-\mathbf{A}_{0}}e^{\mathbf{A}_{0}+\mathbf{A}_{1}})-\mathbf{A}_{1}|\leq 2|\mathbf{A}_{0}||\mathbf{A}_{1}|

for all
\mathbf{A}_{0},\mathbf{A}_{1}\in M_{d}(\mathbb{R})
with
|\mathbf{A}_{0}||\mathbf{A}_{1}|<\lambda
.

Proof.

Define

F(\mathbf{A}_{0},\mathbf{A}_{1})=\log(e^{-\mathbf{A}_{0}}e^{\mathbf{A}_{0}+\mathbf{A}_{1}})-\mathbf{A}_{1}.

We take
\lambda>0
sufficiently small so that
F
is smooth for
|\mathbf{A}_{0}|,|\mathbf{A}_{1}|<2\lambda
. Define

f(s,t):=F(s\mathbf{A}_{0},t\mathbf{A}_{1}),

and note that

f(s,0)=\log(\operatorname{\mathbf{Id}})-\mathbf{O}=\mathbf{O},\qquad f(0,t)=\log(e^{t\mathbf{A}_{1}})-t\mathbf{A}_{1}=\mathbf{O},

and that

\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial s\partial t}(s,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}(DF|_{t\mathbf{A}_{0},s\mathbf{A}_{1}}(\mathbf{A}_{0},\mathbf{O}))=D^{2}F|_{t\mathbf{A}_{0},s\mathbf{A}_{1}}((\mathbf{A}_{0},\mathbf{O}),(\mathbf{O},\mathbf{A}_{1}))

so we find that

\left|\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial s\partial t}(s,t)\right|\leq C|\mathbf{A}_{0}||\mathbf{A}_{1}|

for
C=C(m)
. Then, we can write

\displaystyle f(s,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\partial f}{\partial t}(s,\tau)d\tau

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial s\partial t}(\sigma,\tau)d\tau d\sigma+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\partial f}{\partial t}(0,\tau)d\tau

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial s\partial t}(\sigma,\tau)d\tau d\sigma

where we used
f(s,0)=f(0,t)=0
in several places. Taking the norm of both sides and using the estimate for the second derivatives yields the assertion.
∎

Appendix G
Affine transformation acting on a graph

Lemma G.1
.

For
\delta\in(0,1)
and
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, there is
\lambda=\lambda(d,\delta)>0
,
C=C(d)>0
with the following property. Suppose that
\mathbf{z}\in B_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathbf{A}\in B_{\lambda}\subset M_{d}(\mathbb{R})
,
\mathbf{x}\in B_{\lambda}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\Pi\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a codimension
1
subspace with fixed unit normal
\nu
, and
w_{0}\in C^{\infty}(B_{\delta}\subset\Pi)
with
|w_{0}|+\delta|\nabla w_{0}|\leq\delta
on
B_{\delta}
. Letting
\Sigma_{0}
denote the
\nu
-normal graph of
w_{0}
over
\mathbf{z}+\Pi
then there exists a unique
w\in\mathbb{R}
such that,

\mathbf{z}+w\nu\in e^{\mathbf{A}}\Sigma_{0}+\mathbf{x}

where

|w-w_{0}(\mathbf{0})-\langle\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{z}+w_{0}(\mathbf{0})\nu),\nu\rangle-\langle\mathbf{x},\nu\rangle|\leq C(|\mathbf{A}|+|\mathbf{x}|)(|\mathbf{A}|+\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{C^{0}(B_{C(|\mathbf{A}|+|\mathbf{x}|)})}).

Proof.

Taking
\lambda
sufficiently small, it’s clear that a unique such
w
exists (conversely, one may derive the existence of
w
from the subsequent analysis). There is
\mathbf{w}\in B_{\delta}
so that

(G.1)

\mathbf{z}+w\nu=e^{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{z}+\mathbf{w}+w_{0}(\mathbf{w})\nu)+\mathbf{x}

Rearranging (
G.1
) and projecting to
\Pi
we obtain

\mathbf{w}=\operatorname{proj}_{\Pi}((e^{-\mathbf{A}}-\operatorname{id})\mathbf{z})+w\operatorname{proj}_{\Pi}(e^{-\mathbf{A}}\nu)-\operatorname{proj}_{\Pi}(e^{-\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{x})

so

|\mathbf{w}|\leq C(|\mathbf{A}|(1+|w|)+|\mathbf{x}|)

with
C=C(m)
. On the other hand, projecting (
G.1
) to
\nu
yields

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=\langle e^{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{z}+\mathbf{w}+w_{0}(\mathbf{w})\nu)-\mathbf{z},\nu\rangle+\langle\mathbf{x},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=w_{0}(\mathbf{w})+\langle\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{z}+\mathbf{w}+w_{0}(\mathbf{w})\nu),\nu\rangle+\langle\mathbf{x},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle(e^{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}-\operatorname{id})(\mathbf{z}+\mathbf{w}+w_{0}(\mathbf{w})\nu),\nu\rangle

A coarse estimate thus gives

|w|\leq\delta+C(|\mathbf{A}|(1+|\mathbf{w}|)+|\mathbf{x}|),

so we obtain

|\mathbf{w}|\leq C(|\mathbf{A}|+|\mathbf{x}|)

as long as
\lambda
is sufficiently small. Using this and Taylor’s theorem in the
\nu
-projected equation to estimate terms involving
\mathbf{w}
, we have proven the asserted expression.
∎

Appendix H
Simon’s non-concentration estimate in the Riemannian setting

We use the same notations as in Sections
3.2
and
3.3
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1}
be a strictly stable (cf. Definition
3.9
) and strictly minimizing (cf. Definition
H.2
below) hypercone, with cross section
\mathcal{L}_{\circ}:=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\cap\mathbb{S}^{n_{\circ}}
. Recall that we continue to identify minimizers with their regular part, and thus
\mathcal{C}_{\circ},\mathcal{L}_{\circ}
are smooth. We parametrize
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
by

(0,+\infty)\times\mathcal{L}_{\circ}\to\mathcal{C}_{\circ},\ \ \ (r,\omega)\mapsto x=r\omega\,.

Fix a continuous unit normal field
\nu_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}
of
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
. Let
U_{\circ}^{\pm}
be the connected components of
\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}
, where
\nu_{\circ}
points into
U_{\circ}^{+}
.

In the cylindrical setting, we fix an integer
k\geq 1
and take
n=n_{\circ}+k
and

\mathcal{C}:=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}

parametrized by

(0,+\infty)\times\mathcal{L}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{n+1},\quad(r,\omega,y)\mapsto(r\omega,y)\,.

We also write
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Throughout this section, we will use
r
to denote

r(x,y)=|x|,

including when
(x,y)\not\in\mathcal{C}
. We will also work with balls in any of
\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1}
,
\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, correspondingly denoted

B^{n_{\circ}+1}_{s}(x),\;B^{k}_{s}(y),\;B_{s}(x,y).

It will be convenient to work with cylinders

Q_{s}(x,y):=B^{n_{\circ}+1}_{s}(x)\times B^{k}_{s}(y).

The center point will be suppressed if it is the origin. We will also use without comment the fact that
Q_{s}\subset B_{\sqrt{2}s}\Subset B_{2s}
. We will write
\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}
for the signed distance to
\mathcal{C}
(cf. (
4.1
)).

We will study
g
-minimizing boundaries
\Sigma
in
B_{2}
with respect to smooth Riemannian metrics
g
satisfying

(H.1)

\max\{|g_{ij}-\bar{g}_{ij}|,|\partial_{k}g_{ij}|,|\partial^{2}_{kl}g_{ij}|\}\leq\delta\leq 10^{-1},

where
\bar{g}
denotes the standard Euclidean metric. We always assume (
H.1
) below. In applications we will always take
g
to be (rescaled) normal coordinates in a Riemannian metric (curvature bounds as in (
E.1
) guaranteeing that (
H.1
) holds on small scales) but for the sake of generality we do not assume this here.

Theorem H.1

(
[
Sim94
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

There exist
\varepsilon(\mathcal{C})
,
C(\mathcal{C})
,
\delta_{1}(\mathcal{C})>0
with the following property. Let
\delta\leq\delta_{1}
in (
H.1
), and
\Sigma
is a
g
-minimizing boundary in
B_{2}
with
\operatorname{spt}\Sigma\cap B_{1}\neq\emptyset
and
\int_{\Sigma}\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}d\|\Sigma\|_{\bar{g}}\leq\varepsilon
, then there exist a
C^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}
function
u
on a subset of
\mathcal{C}
and an open
U\supset Q_{1/2}\cap\{r>1/20\}
so that
\operatorname{graph}_{\mathcal{C}}u=\Sigma\cap U
and

\int_{Q_{1/2}}r^{-2}\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}d\|\Sigma\|_{\bar{g}}+\int_{Q_{1/2}\cap\Dom(u)}(|\nabla_{\mathcal{C}}u|^{2}+r^{-2}u^{2})\ d\|\mathcal{C}\|_{\bar{g}}\\
\leq C(\mathcal{C})\left(\int_{Q_{1}}\overline{\operatorname{dist}}_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}\ d\|\Sigma\|_{\bar{g}}+\delta\right)\,.

The remainder of this appendix contains a proof of Theorem
H.1
including the adaptations to the non-flat background. We will use the spectral analysis on
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
developed in Section
3.2
.

H.1.
Weighted norm

For
\Omega\subset\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
and
v\in C^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Omega)
we define

\|v\|_{C^{2}_{*}(\Omega)}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}\left(|x|^{-1}|v(x)|+|\nabla v(x)|+|x||\nabla^{2}v(x)|\right).

If
\Omega=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
, we will write
v=O^{*}_{2}(r^{\alpha})
when
r\|v\|_{C^{2}_{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\setminus B_{r})}=O(r^{\alpha})
for
r>0
.

We also extend this definition to the cylindrical setting. For
\Omega\subset\mathcal{C}
and
v\in C^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Omega)
, we define

\|v\|_{C^{2}_{*}(\Omega)}:=\sup_{(x,y)\in\Omega}\left(|x|^{-1}|v(x,y)|+|\nabla v(x,y)|+|x||\nabla^{2}v(x,y)|\right).

If
\Omega=\mathcal{C}
, we will write
v=O^{*}_{2}(r^{\alpha})
if
r\|v\|_{C^{2}_{*}((\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\setminus B_{r})\times\mathbb{R}^{k})}=O(r^{\alpha})
for
r>0
.

In both cases,
\Omega
might be denoted
\Dom(v)
or else suppressed if it can be understood from the context.

H.2.
Hardt-Simon Foliation

By
[
HS85
]
, there exists a pair of real numbers

\gamma(\pm)=\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{\circ},\pm)\in\{\gamma_{1},2-n_{\circ}-\gamma_{1}\},

where
\gamma_{1}=-\alpha(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})>-\frac{n_{\circ}-2}{2}
is defined in Section
3.2
, a unique pairwise-disjoint family of minimizing hypersurfaces
\{{S}_{\circ}(\lambda)\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}
foliating
\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1}\setminus\{0\}
parametrized by
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, as well as a family of
C^{\infty}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})
functions
\{(f_{\circ})_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}
such that

(i)

{S}_{\circ}(0)=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
,
(f_{\circ})_{0}\equiv 0
;
{S}_{\circ}(\lambda)\in U_{\circ}^{\textrm{sgn}(\lambda)}
for every
\lambda\neq 0
.

(ii)

For every
t>0
and
\lambda\neq 0
,

(H.2)

\displaystyle{S}_{\circ}(t\lambda)

\displaystyle=t^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma(\textrm{sgn}\lambda)}}\cdot{S}_{\circ}(\lambda)\,,

(H.3)

\displaystyle(f_{\circ})_{t\lambda}(x)

\displaystyle=t^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma(\textrm{sgn}\lambda)}}\cdot(f_{\circ})_{\lambda}(t^{-\frac{1}{1-\gamma(\textrm{sgn}\lambda)}}x)\,.

(iii)

There exists
R=R(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})>1
and
\delta=\delta(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})\in(0,1)
such that for every
\lambda\neq 0
, outside the ball of radius
R|\lambda|^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma(\textrm{sgn}\lambda)}}
, we have

(H.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{S}_{\circ}(\lambda)&=\operatorname{graph}_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}(f_{\circ})_{\lambda}\\
(f_{\circ})_{\lambda}(x)&=\lambda\cdot r^{\gamma(\textrm{sgn}\lambda)}\psi_{1}(\omega)+O_{2}^{*}\left(|\lambda|^{1+\frac{\delta}{1-\gamma(\textrm{sgn}\lambda)}}\cdot r^{\gamma(\textrm{sgn}\lambda)-\delta}\right)\,,\end{split}

where
\psi_{1}>0
is the
L^{2}
-unit first eigenfunction of
-(\Delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}+|A_{\mathcal{L}_{\circ}}|^{2})
as in section
3.2
.

We assume from now on that
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
is strictly stable and strictly minimizing in the sense of
[
HS85
]
:

Definition H.2
.

We say
\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
is
strictly minimizing
if
\gamma(+)=\gamma(-)=\gamma_{1}
above.

Let
\nu_{{S}_{\circ}}
be the unit
C^{1}_{\textnormal{loc}}
vector field on
\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1}\setminus\{0\}
such that

\nu_{{S}_{\circ}}(x)\perp T_{x}{S}_{\circ}(\lambda)\text{ whenever }x\in{S}_{\circ}(\lambda),

\nu_{{S}}=\nu_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}\text{ along }\mathcal{C}_{\circ}.

Note that
\nu_{{S}_{\circ}}
is homogeneous with degree
0
, i.e.

\nu_{{S}_{\circ}}(tx)=\nu_{{S}_{\circ}}(x)\,,\qquad\text{ for every }t>0\text{ and }x\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1}\setminus\{0\}\,.

Also define
\mathfrak{T}_{{S}_{\circ}}(x)\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1})\cap C^{1}_{\textrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{\circ}+1}\setminus\{0\})
by

(H.5)

\mathfrak{T}_{{S}_{\circ}}(x):=\lambda\qquad\text{ if }x\in{S}_{\circ}(\lambda)\,.

We now extend everything to the cylindrical case, where

\mathcal{C}:=\mathcal{C}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1=n_{\circ}+1+k}.

Let

U^{\pm}:=U^{\pm}_{\circ}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}

be the connected components of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{C}}
, and

{S}(\lambda):={S}_{\circ}(\lambda)\times\mathbb{R}^{k}

be the minimizing hypersurfaces foliating
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Then we write

f_{\lambda},\;\mathfrak{T}_{{S}},\;\nu_{{S}}

for the
y
-invariant extensions of
(f_{\circ})_{\lambda},\mathfrak{T}_{{S}_{\circ}},\nu_{{S}_{\circ}}
to
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k})
, respectively. Note that, since all the
{S}(\lambda)
are minimal, we have

(H.6)

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1},\bar{g}}(\nu_{{S}})\equiv 0\,.

Proposition H.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C},U^{\pm},{S}(\lambda),f_{\lambda},\mathfrak{T}_{{S}}
be defined as above.
Then for every
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k})
and every unit vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, we have

(H.7)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}_{{S}}(tx,y)

\displaystyle=t^{1-\gamma_{1}}\mathfrak{T}_{{S}}(x,y)\,;

(H.8)

\displaystyle C(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})^{-1}\cdot\frac{\operatorname{dist}_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}(x)}{|x|^{\gamma_{1}}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{T}_{{S}}(x,y)\leq C(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})\cdot\frac{\operatorname{dist}_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ}}(x)}{|x|^{\gamma_{1}}}\,;

(H.9)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{v^{\perp}}\mathfrak{T}_{{S}}(x,y)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})|x|^{-2\gamma_{1}}\left(1\pm\langle v,\nu_{{S}}(x,y)\rangle_{\bar{g}}\right)\,

where
\nabla_{v^{\perp}}\mathfrak{T}_{{S}}(x,y):=(\nabla\mathfrak{T}_{{S}}(x,y))^{\perp v}
is the Euclidean gradient in
v^{\perp}
direction and (
H.9
) holds for either choice of sign.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume that
y=0
. Also, since
\mathfrak{T}_{{S}}\in C^{1}_{\textnormal{loc}}
and
\nu_{{S}}\in C^{0}_{\textnormal{loc}}
, it suffices to consider when
x\notin\mathcal{C}_{\circ}
. Now we see that (
H.7
) follows directly from (
H.2
) and (
H.5
), while (
H.8
) follows directly from definition and (
H.4
). We now prove (
H.9
). Since
\mathfrak{T}_{{S}}
is constant on every
{S}(\lambda)
and independent of
y
, we have

\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\mathfrak{T}_{{S}}(x,y)=c(x)\nu_{{S}}(x,y)\,,

for some
c(x)\in\mathbb{R}
. We now determine the value of
c(x)
.
Differentiating both sides of (
H.7
) with respect to
t
and evaluating at
t=1
we find

c(x)\langle\nu_{{S}}(x,y),(x,0)\rangle=\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\mathfrak{T}_{{S}}(x,y),(x,0)\rangle=(1-\gamma_{1})\mathfrak{T}_{{S}}(x,y)\,.

Solving for
c(x)
(recall that
\gamma_{1}=-\alpha(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})<-1
by (
5.3
)) we find

|\nabla_{v^{\perp}}\mathfrak{T}_{{S}}(x,y)|^{2}=\frac{(1-\gamma_{1})^{2}\mathfrak{T}_{{S}}(x,y)^{2}}{\langle\nu_{{S}}(x,y),(x,0)\rangle^{2}}|\nu_{{S}}(x,y)^{\perp v}|^{2}.

For any unit vectors
v,X
we have
|X^{\perp v}|^{2}=1-(X\cdot v)^{2}\leq 2(1\pm X\cdot v)
(this holds for either choice of sign). Moreover, by (
H.4
) and
\gamma_{1}\neq 1
we find:

\langle\nu_{{S}_{\circ}}(x),x\rangle\geq C(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})^{-1}\lambda|x|^{\gamma_{1}}=C(\mathcal{C}_{\circ})^{-1}\mathfrak{T}_{{S}}(x,y)|x|^{\gamma_{1}}\,.

Putting these expressions together proves (
H.9
).
∎

H.3.
Divergence

Fix a metric
g
on
Q_{4}
satisfying (
H.1
). For a vector field
X
on
Q_{4}
we will write
\operatorname{div}_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1},g}X
for the divergence of
X
with respect to
g
and
\operatorname{div}_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1},\bar{g}}X
for the divergence of
X
the Euclidean metric
\bar{g}
. Similarly, for
\Sigma\subset Q_{4}
a hypersurface or varifold we write
\operatorname{div}_{\Sigma,g}X
for the divergence of
X
along
\Sigma
with respect to
g
and
\operatorname{div}_{\Sigma,\bar{g}}X
for the divergence of
X
along
\Sigma
with respect to the Euclidean metric
\bar{g}
. Set

\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}(X,g):=\operatorname{div}_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1},g}X-\operatorname{div}_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1},\bar{g}}X

and

\mathcal{E}_{\Sigma}(X,g):=\operatorname{div}_{\Sigma,g}X-\operatorname{div}_{\Sigma,\bar{g}}X

The following lemma is easily verified

Lemma H.4
.

We have
|\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}(X,g)|\leq C_{n}\delta|X|
and
|\mathcal{E}_{\Sigma}(X,g)|\leq C_{n}\delta(|X|+|\nabla_{\bar{g}}X|)
.

H.4.
L^{2}
non-concentration

Now we are ready to prove Simon’s

non-concentration estimate in the Riemannian setting. We continue to assume that the background metric

satisfies (

).

The following formula was essentially derived in

, Lemma 2.2, (24)]
with different notation.

26

26

Lemma H.5
.

Assume that (

) holds. Let

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{Q_{1}}\zeta(y)\varphi(r)f(x,y)^{2}(n_{\circ}+|\nu_{y}|^{2})\ d\|\Sigma\|_{\bar{g}}\\

&+O\left(\int_{Q_{1}}(|\zeta\varphi f^{2}|+r|\nabla_{\bar{g}}(\zeta\varphi f^{2})|)d\|\Sigma\|_{\bar{g}}\right)\delta\end{split}

where the implied constant depends only on
n
. Here

\partial_{r}^{\perp}:=\langle(\displaystyle\tfrac{x}{|x|},0),\nu_{\Sigma,\bar{g}}\rangle_{\bar{g}},\quad\nu_{y}:=\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle\partial_{y_{i}},\nu_{\Sigma,\bar{g}}\rangle_{\bar{g}}\partial_{y_{i}}\,,

where
\nu_{\Sigma,\bar{g}}
is a unit normal field on
\Sigma
under
\bar{g}
defined almost
27
27

27

Note that the formula is unchanged if we replace

by
-\nu_{\Sigma}
.
everywhere.

There is
\kappa_{\mathcal{C}}
is sufficiently small depending only on

so that if

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{y_{i}},\nu_{\Sigma,\bar{g}}\rangle_{\bar{g}}|_{(\hat{x},y)}

(H.12)

\displaystyle=\mp(1+O(\|u\|_{C^{1}_{*}}))\cdot\partial_{r}u(x,y)\,.

Proof.

To prove (
H.10

X=\zeta(y)\varphi(r)f(x,y)^{2}\cdot(x,0)

In particular, it suffices to study the term on the left hand side. Observe that

\operatorname{div}_{\Sigma,\bar{g}}(x,0)=n-\operatorname{div}_{\Sigma,\bar{g}}(0,y)=n-\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\partial_{y_{i}}^{T}|^{2}=n_{\circ}+|\nu_{y}|^{2},

Thus,

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}_{\Sigma,\bar{g}}X

\displaystyle+\zeta(y)\varphi^{\prime}(r)f(x,y)^{2}r(1-(\partial_{r}^{\perp})^{2})

\displaystyle+\zeta(y)\varphi(r)\langle\nabla_{\Sigma,\bar{g}}(f^{2}),(x,0)\rangle_{\bar{g}}

\displaystyle-\varphi(r)\langle\nabla_{\bar{g}}\zeta(y),\nu_{y}\rangle_{\bar{g}}r\partial_{r}^{\perp}f(x,y)^{2}

As explained, this proves (

). The formulae (

\Phi:D_{\Phi}\to\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\,,\quad(x,y,t)\mapsto(x+t\nu_{\mathcal{C}_{\circ},\bar{g}}(x),y)\,,
